So rule (i) forbids a free-form bind-any-row-to-any-person list and does **not** forbid a designation list, which is by construction the short list of one principal's own grants. **Revs 17 and 18 derived a “lane provisioning is first-connect-only” consequence from the eligibility predicate rev 19 deletes; that consequence is deleted with it** — a lane is provisionable at any time once at least one grant is bound, including for a grant that has never connected, because the designation is a persisted human choice and not an observation of the wire. **(ii) The consent row renders every mint-time fact the host actually holds**, so the human can match it to the act they just performed: the `--pair-name` string as a *label*, the deviceId's short prefix, the scope, `pairedAt`, `pendingExpiresAt`, and whether the grant has ever connected (`lastSeenAt === 0`, the same discriminator `isMintedPendingDevice` uses, `device-registry-pending-grants.ts:14-16`) **[code]**. **Rev 13 attached a *refusal* to that rendering — "when two candidate rows are indistinguishable on all of those, the consent step refuses rather than guessing" — and re-opening the registry shows that refusal can never fire, so it is deleted rather than reworded.** `DeviceEntry.deviceId` is `randomUUID()` (`device-registry.ts:95`) and `pairedAt` is `Date.now()` (`:99`) **[code]**: two rows are unique on those two facts *by construction*, so "indistinguishable on all of them" is unreachable and the rule was a control that looked like one. Worse, it was aimed at a problem rule (i) has already removed: at mint the host knows the exact row it just minted, and at a named invite it knows the row that invite produced — the row is never ambiguous *to the host* at either binding moment, and on any later connection the socket carries `socket.device.deviceId` (`runtime-rpc.ts:1384`) **[code]** anyway. **What (ii) actually is, stated as what it is:** a legibility requirement. The facts are rendered so the **human** can tell two `Ana` rows apart before ticking; they do not gate anything, and no refusal is derived from them. **And the failure this subsection opened by calling "not cosmetic" — the human ticking the *wrong person* for a device that is unambiguously identified — is not addressed by (i) or (ii) at all.** Its only control is (iii): unbind, plus the audit row that makes the mis-tick visible in the first place. That is stated plainly here rather than left implied by a refusal that cannot fire, because a reader who believed the refusal was the control would think the mis-tick was prevented, and it is not — it is *recovered*. **(iii) Bind is reversible and audited, which is what turns a mis-tick from permanent into a five-second correction.** `principal-registry.ts` (§6) ships five operations, not one: **bind** (only onto an *unbound* row), **unbind**, **re-bind as unbind-then-bind — never a rewrite in place** — added in rev 14, because §2e's delegation lease has no other writer once its trap-door is deleted — **designate this principal's `delegatedGrantId`** — since rev 19 a plain tick over the principal's already-bound grants, available at any time and persisted like the bindings themselves — and, added in rev 15 on the orchestrator's answer to §7 question 8, **bind a federated link's home-peer fingerprint to a principal**, without which a federated worktree create fails closed (§2a's per-site derivation) (§2a's pane-binding decision depends on the third of these: an in-place re-bind is the mutation that would silently re-point live panes). **Each of the five** writes a local, host-only audit row, and rev 13's "each of the three — device, principal, direction, timestamp" is stale in both halves (round 5 minor): the count is three revisions old, and two of the five do not have that shape. The row is *the subject the write names, the principal, what happened, and a timestamp*: bind / unbind / re-bind carry the device and a direction; **designate-the-pusher** carries the designated grant and no direction at all; and the **link binding** carries `{ homePeerFingerprint, boundDeviceId, boundAt }` per clause (b) below — no principal of its own, since the create reads `principalOf(boundDeviceId)` (rev 17). All five are surfaced in the consent UI, because without it the failure is invisible and the only remedy on offer is a revoke, which under §2f is not a lane wipe at all. **Effect on already-bound panes, stated because this rule and §2a's pane-binding rule are one design:** a pane persists a `lanePrincipalId` and never a device id, so unbind and re-bind **re-point no live pane** — panes bound to principal P stay on P's lane whoever that device now belongs to. What unbind *does* change is the wipe join (§2f): that grant's sockets stop counting toward P, so if it was P's last connected grant, P's lane wipes at the next close evaluation, exactly as a disconnect would. **And — rev 20, round 8's fourth minor — unbind *also* clears the designation when the row it removes is the designated one, because `delegatedGrantId` is a persisted field naming a **bound** grant and cannot outlive the binding it names.** The principal drops to *no designation*: the surface renders an unticked list rather than a label sourced from a `DeviceEntry` that no longer exists, the next provisioning is refused `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated`, and every push is refused `accounts.lane.push_not_delegated` (§2a). A **revoke** takes the same disposition for the same reason — `deviceRegistry.removeDevice` drops the registry row (`runtime-rpc.ts:648` on the paired path, `:660` on `revokeRuntimeAccess`) **[code]** — so on the case §2f is explicit about, revoking the designated desktop while the phone survives, the **lane survives and the designation does not**: the lane sits at `laneState: 'absent'`, no push arrives, and one re-tick on a surviving bound grant restores it. That is the same inert/recoverable outcome §2a already names for a mis-designation, and stating it here is what keeps a persisted field from dangling past the act that deleted its subject. The clear is itself an audited write (a `designate` row naming no grant), so the audit trail shows *why* the lane went silent.

**`principalId` is minted like a lane id and validated like one.** It is a fresh `randomUUID()` in the exact shape the registry already mints its *device* ids in (`device-registry.ts:95` is `deviceId: randomUUID()`; no principal mint exists in this tree yet, so the shape is a precedent, not an observation) **[inferred]**, validated **at creation** against the same v4 regex §2a applies to lane ids, so no principal id reaches `path.join` unvalidated and containment stays defence-in-depth rather than the primary check. `DeviceEntry` gains one nullable field, `principalId?: string` — additive, local schema, absent on every legacy row, and an absent value means "no lane", i.e. today.

**Everything rev 10 keyed by `pairedDeviceId` re-keys by `principalId`, and every re-key is a *widening*.** Named one by one, because each has its own site and its own test: **the ownership predicate** — `resolveInheritedLane` case (i) compares `sourcePane.lanePrincipalId === principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` instead of raw device ids — the pane already stores the principal, so the left side is a read and not a second lookup — and case (iii) reads "the caller's principal holds no lane"; case (ii) is unchanged. The normative statement of this predicate is at its own site below, not here; **the adopt/materialize gate** at `orca-runtime.ts:26704-26711` **[code]** compares principals, so the owner's phone may adopt or materialize a pane their own desktop minted — which is the single largest UX gain of Q1, because it makes the documented wake gesture (#11598, row 35) work across one person's devices instead of refusing `terminal.lane_not_owned` at their own pane; **`terminal.split`** and the **worker / federation `inherit`** edges likewise; **the sleeping-agent wake** matches the record's principal; **`openInMyLane`** (§2g) creates under the caller's principal's lane, and its attachment join walks `attachmentsOf(ptyId)` → `connectionId` → participant → `pairedDeviceId` → **principal**; **the presence owner label** (§2h) renders the *principal*, so a person's desktop pane and phone pane show one owner rather than two; **the residency index** (§2e) keys its rows by principal and `assertNotLaneResident` names the holding *principal* in its refusal; **push authorization** (§2d) resolves the target lane as `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` and refuses a grant with no principal; **revoke** removes the *grant*, and removes the lane only when the revoked grant was that principal's **last** (§2f states it normatively, at the site, with the ordering the two revoke paths force). **The pane binding is the one this list first omitted, and it does not re-key by indirection.** The value **persisted on the pane's binding row is the principal id itself** — `lanePrincipalId`, resolved once at bind time from `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` and never resolved again; the older name `laneGrantId` survives in this document only where a sentence is describing rev 10's shape, and every site listed in §3 rows 4 and 8 carries the principal. **Why storing the grant id and resolving late is not acceptable**, since it is the cheaper version and it fails twice: **(a)** revoke deletes the `DeviceEntry` first — `deviceRegistry.removeDevice(deviceId)` at `runtime-rpc.ts:648` and `:660`, *ahead* of `forgetGrant` at `:653`/`:666` **[code]** — so `principalOf` would return nothing for **every pane that grant created**, and under §2h's own rule those panes would render `credentialLane: 'unknown'` and refuse a split, a recovery *and* a resume while the lane is alive, the person is still working, and the degradation matrix promises “the lane survives”; **(b)** the device→principal binding is mutable by construction — the consent action “picks an existing principal or mints one” — so a re-bind would silently re-point every live pane that device created at a different person's lane. **Two rules close both, and both are asserted in §5:** the persisted value is a principal id, never a grant id; and a *bound* device row is never re-bound in place — re-binding is unbind-then-bind (below), and neither leg touches a pane, because no pane stores a device id to begin with. The **wipe** is the one that cannot simply re-key, below.

**The wipe predicate is the one re-key today's code cannot express, and it must be rebuilt rather than re-keyed.** §2f rests on B2's `hasOtherConnections`, and that value is computed by comparing **device tokens**: `Array.from(this.authenticatedSockets.values()).some((candidate) => candidate.device.deviceToken === socket.device.deviceToken)` (`rpc/mobile-socket-wiring.ts:209-221`, comparison `:215-219` esp. `:218`) **[code]**, then spent as `if (!hasOtherConnections) { this.runtime.onClientDisconnected(socket.device.deviceToken) }` (`runtime-rpc.ts:1408-1410` — rev 13 cited `:1407-1409`; `:1407` is `this.binaryStreamHandlers.delete(socket.connectionId)`, corrected here on re-opening) **[code]**. A token is per **grant**. Left alone, the desktop closing its socket would wipe the lane while the same person's phone is still attached to it, and the phone's next launch would fail closed — the precise opposite of what Q1 asks for. **Decision: the lane wipe is not driven by `hasOtherConnections` at all.** `onClientDisconnected` keeps its signature, its token argument and its existing callers; the lane wipe becomes a **separate** subscriber at the same close site, asking the socket registry directly — *does any authenticated socket remain whose grant is bound to this principal?* — over the same `authenticatedSockets` map `handleClose` already enumerates (`:218-227`) **[code]**, joined through `principalOf`. Rev 10's key-mismatch note survives and gets sharper: the site must read `socket.device.deviceId` — in scope there, and already what `registerConnection` is passed at `runtime-rpc.ts:1382-1387` (esp. `:1384`) **[code]** — resolve it to a principal, and use the token for nothing but B2's own bookkeeping. Control in §5: two devices of one principal connected, the desktop closes and the lane **survives**; the phone then closes and the lane **wipes**. Mutation proof: reusing `hasOtherConnections` as the lane predicate turns that test red.

**What Q1 costs, stated rather than buried.** (1) A principal is a **larger** blast radius than a grant: losing one of a person's devices exposes that person's lane, not that device's lane. That is the owner's explicit choice and it sits inside the same §4 envelope as everything else, but the design does not describe it as free. (2) Revoking **one** of a person's devices does **not** wipe the lane — only revoking the last one does — so the revoke confirmation must say which of the two it is doing, or the local human will believe a revoke removed the credential when it did not. Named residual, UI obligation in S9d. (3) L1 stops biting the case it was written to annoy: one person's desktop and phone now legitimately share one lane holding one account, which is **one** residency and not two. Rev 10's degradation row *"Second desktop/grant, same human … Convenient or wrong depending on Q1"* resolves to convenient and is rewritten in §3. (4) The hard part — **who pushes from the phone** — is not solved by the re-key and gets its own section, §2l.

A **lane** is one directory, `<userData>/claude-lanes/<principalId>/`, used verbatim as that principal's `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` on every grant bound to them. It holds `.credentials.json`, `.claude.json`, `settings.json`, and — as a consequence of relocation **[doc]** — that grant's session transcripts. Directory `0700`, files `0600`. New module `src/main/claude-accounts/principal-credential-lane.ts` reuses the ownership discipline of `managed-auth-path.ts:16-66` (no symlinks, canonical containment under the root, exact relative-segment match, marker file) — re-parameterised for this key, not borrowed as-is: that check hardcodes a two-segment `<accountId>/auth` shape (`:46-53`) **[code]**. Lane ids — now principal ids — are validated **at creation** against the exact `randomUUID` shape the registry mints (`device-registry.ts:95`) **[code]** — `/^[0-9a-f]{8}-[0-9a-f]{4}-4[0-9a-f]{3}-[89ab][0-9a-f]{3}-[0-9a-f]{12}$/` — so no grant id ever reaches `path.join` unvalidated; containment is defence-in-depth, not the primary check.

**Provisioning is an event, and no client can cause it.** A lane exists for principal P only after a **host-side consent action** — the local human ticks "this device belongs to \<person\>" (minting P, or joining this grant to an existing P) and "give this person their own Claude account lane" — **both on that device's row** in the pairing/accounts UI — **and, new in rev 14 because §2e's lease has no other writer, designates *which of this principal's bound grants pushes accounts into this lane***, which since rev 19 is a tick on a **list of that principal's grants** rather than a per-device checkbox (the paragraph below states the surface exactly; rev 14's "this device's row" phrasing is corrected here, round 8's fifth minor, because it is true only of the one-human/one-desktop bootstrap and stale for the re-designation the rewrite exists to allow). Or the human runs the local `orca` equivalent over the local socket. On the Windows host that local socket is a **named pipe**, not a Unix socket: the runtime's own endpoint is `\\.\pipe\orca-${pid}-${suffix}` on `win32` and `join(userDataPath, 'o-<pid>-<suffix>.sock')` on POSIX (`createRuntimeTransportMetadata`, `runtime/runtime-rpc.ts:1853-1870`, the win32 branch at `:1864` and the POSIX one at `:1869`) **[code]**. **Rev 13 cited `daemon/daemon-spawner.ts:105-108` for this, and re-opening shows that is the *daemon PTY-host* endpoint (`getDaemonSocketPath`, `:98-110`) — a different pipe, reached by a different client. The citation is corrected here rather than swapped silently, and the substance rev 13 missed is in the correct file:** the in-tree comment reads "named pipes lack the chmod hardening of Unix sockets; a per-runtime suffix avoids a stable, guessable endpoint name" (`:1863`) **[code]** — i.e. on Windows the POSIX socket's mode-bit protection is replaced by obscurity plus the pipe's default DACL, which is a materially different statement from "the Unix socket becomes a named pipe". §2m(6) states what that changes about who counts as "local". It is the same class of act as approving the pairing itself. **The lease is part of that write set, and provisioning fails closed without it.** §2e's `delegatedGrantId` is an *authority* value — it decides which grant may write a lane's credential — so under this paragraph's own rule ("no client can cause it") it cannot be written by a client action, and §2e's rev-13 trap-door ("pinned from the `delegation` member of the first accepted push") is **deleted** in rev 14: it would have let any grant of the principal seize the lease by racing to push first, including the second desktop §2e spends a paragraph closing. The consent step therefore also **designates one of that principal's bound grants as the pusher**, writing `delegatedGrantId`, and a lane whose principal has **no designation** is **not provisionable**, `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated`. **Designation is a plain host-side consent tick over already-bound grants, and rev 19 makes that the whole of the mechanism — the orchestrator's decision, 2026-08-22.** The surface lists the principal's own bound grants — the rows this human already vouched for under rule (i) — rendered with the same mint-time facts rule (ii) requires, and the human ticks one. It is the **same** local pairing/accounts UI, and the same local `orca` command, that binds grants, and it is offered at **any** time: in the provisioning tick itself (one human with one desktop is **one tick** — bind, provision, designate), or later, to move the designation to a different bound grant. The value is **persisted in the principal registry** beside the bindings — not memoized for a process lifetime and not read off a socket — so it survives a socket close, a disconnect and an Orca restart; and it writes an **audit row** like the other consent writes (rule (iii)): the designated grant, the principal, what happened, and a timestamp, with no direction. **What makes a grant eligible is nothing whatever except being bound to this principal, and that is a decision to stop inferring rather than a looser inference.** Rev 15 keyed eligibility on `scope: 'runtime'`; rev 16 on a capability the host advertises; revs 17 and 18 on a capability the client declares, plus a runtime-lifetime memo to hold it after the socket died. **All of it is deleted in rev 19, and the reason is one fact about this tree rather than three separate mistakes: every candidate signal is either absent or *client-asserted*, so none of them is evidence.** `export type DeviceScope = 'mobile' | 'runtime'` (`src/shared/runtime-types.ts:56`, the field at `device-registry.ts:29`) **[code]** has no desktop value at all — `'runtime'` covers an `orca` CLI grant exactly as it covers a desktop (`revokeRuntimeAccess` gates on `device?.scope !== 'runtime'`, `runtime-rpc.ts:660`) **[code]**. `clientCapabilities` is copied verbatim out of the client's own `e2ee_auth` payload (`rpc/core.ts:78-79`, set at `rpc/e2ee-channel.ts:256` through `parseRuntimeClientCapabilities`, which allowlists nothing — any array of ≤ 64 non-empty strings of ≤ 128 chars, `rpc/runtime-client-capabilities.ts:3-12`) **[code]**, and `rpc/core.ts:78`'s own comment says the set is *bound to the socket*, i.e. declared at authentication and never observed behaviour **[code]**. **`clientKind` is the other candidate a reader will reach for, and rev 19 refuses it for a *different* reason rather than lumping it in with the handshake — the drafted wording said "client-asserted" and did not survive re-opening.** `ctx.clientKind` (`rpc/core.ts:77`) is set from `device.scope` (`runtime-rpc.ts:1784`) **[code]**, so it is host-stored and not asserted per request. What it is *not* is an observation of the device: `scope` is written once, at **pairing-offer creation**, from `args.scope ?? 'runtime'` (`runtime-rpc.ts:725`, spent at `:733`/`:736`) **[code]** — a label chosen by whoever made the invite — and its type has no desktop value, so the other developer's desktop, an `orca` CLI grant and a script all carry the same `'runtime'`. It answers *what kind of invite was issued*, never *what kind of device is on the other end*. **So the host does not attempt to determine what kind of device a grant is — no signal in this tree can tell it — and no rule in this document depends on one. The human at the machine is the authority, full stop.** That is not a weakening: §4's pinned assumption already says a paired grant is shell-equivalent on this host, so a signal a grant can simply assert about itself was never going to add authority — what a designation costs an attacker is a human tick, and that is unchanged. **Mis-designation is harmless and recoverable, and that is what makes the simplification safe rather than merely smaller.** Tick a phone, or an `orca` CLI grant, or a desktop that has since been wiped, and the failure is **inert**: no push ever arrives, so no credential byte is written, `laneState` stays `absent`, and every launch into that lane fails closed exactly as it does for a lane that was never provisioned (§2f, §2h). Nothing leaks, no other principal is touched, and the mis-designated grant gains no authority it did not already hold — it may write **this** principal's lane and nothing else, and the human had already bound it to this principal. **The UI names the condition instead of rendering an empty state:** a designated-but-silent lane reads *“no push received from \<label\>”*, using rule (ii)'s label, beside the re-designate control. The remedy is one tick, audited like a re-bind, and it is the same remedy this subsection reached two paragraphs above for the consent mis-tick, for the same reason: **prevention is not claimed; recovery is.** A **correctly** designated phone is the identical case rather than a special one, because a phone genuinely cannot push — §2l establishes that from the tree, not by assumption — so it lands in `absent` with the same sentence and the same one-tick fix; and a phone-only principal is therefore provisionable and simply never loads, which is the honest rendering of “a phone can never push”. **One residual the recovery does not cover, named rather than folded away:** a designated grant that is *connected* **and holding a lane-status subscription** but cannot act still satisfies §2l's liveness precondition, so a phone's `requestSwitch` is emitted rather than refused up front and runs to `accounts.lane.switch_timed_out`. **Rev 22 narrows this clause from “connected”, which was false for the grant class rev 21 added (§8 item 1):** a designated `orca` CLI grant is *connected* and does **not** satisfy the precondition, because the second conjunct is a live lane-status subscription and the CLI has no subscribe entry point at all — it is refused `accounts.lane.desktop_unavailable` before any frame (§2l). That is this same mis-tick class arriving on the switch path — recovered by re-designating on this surface, not closed by any predicate — and §2l states its guarantee to that exact scope rather than over-claiming it. **And the refusals read for what they are:** `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated` says *no grant has been designated as this principal's pusher*, never “no `runtime`-scoped grant” and never “no grant declared a capability”, so the wording cannot drift back onto an enum or onto a claim of observation the host cannot make. Moving the designation later is that same consent surface and that same audit row as a re-bind (rule (iii) above); §7 question 4 asks only whether it should also move *automatically*, and the answer there is still no. **What this buys, and it is the whole of round 3's first blocker:** §3 row 1 and §5's arm can keep the flat rule — a push whose caller is not the principal's `delegatedGrantId` is refused `accounts.lane.push_not_delegated`, with no "unless the principal has no lease yet" carve-out — because provisioning writes one and a lane is never *created* without one. **Rev 20 states the one way a provisioned lane can later hold none, and it is a refusal rather than a carve-out:** rule (iii)'s unbind/revoke clear (above) leaves an existing lane with no designation, and that case is refused `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated` on both the push and the re-provisioning path — never permitted, never auto-designated. **The refusals on the push path carry codes too, and rev 20 names them rather than leaving the push arm as a consequence (round 8's sixth minor).** An `accounts.lane.push` from a grant bound to no principal, or naming a principal with no provisioned lane, is **refused** — `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated` whenever the named principal has no designation (the **push** arm of the same code the provisioning refusal uses, evaluated on the push path at the same site as §3 row 1's `delegatedGrantId` comparison and **before any write**), and `accounts.lane.push_not_delegated` when the principal *has* a designation and the caller is not it. Both carry a complete human sentence under §3's Rule-3 row, and both are in §3's old-client list. It is never auto-provisioning: otherwise any paired grant — including the other developer's phone — could flip host state that changes what *other* clients are allowed to do (§2d), which is exactly the cross-grant influence R1 exists to close. Deprovisioning is the same UI, and wipes (§f).

**Orphan reconciliation, gated on a registry read that actually succeeded.** At startup, walk `<userData>/claude-lanes/` and delete any lane directory whose principal has no surviving bound device entry — revoke-time wiping does not cover registry rebuilds or restore-from-backup. But `DeviceRegistry.load` wraps the whole read/normalize in a bare `try { … } catch { this.devices = [] }` (`device-registry.ts:350-385`, catch at `:382-385`) **[code]**, so a JSON parse error or a truncated write at `:359` — on **every** platform — yields **zero** devices. **The mode rev 11 named here is withdrawn, in the same breath as §2i withdraws it, rather than left standing in one section and retracted in the other:** the `hardenExistingSecureFile(this.registryPath)` at `:358` **[code]** cannot throw on `win32` at all — both of its arms end in an asynchronous, error-swallowing best-effort ACL call (§2i, re-verified at `secure-file.ts:167-175` → `:69-89` → `:201-209` → `secure-path-windows-acl.ts:24-41`, callback `:37-39`) **[code]**. It *can* throw on POSIX, where the same dispatch ends at a synchronous `chmodSync` (`secure-file.ts:210`) **[code]**. So the zero-device modes are corruption everywhere plus a POSIX permission failure — and Windows, the platform this design pivoted to, is the one platform on which this particular call is structurally incapable of producing them. Unconditional reconciliation would then delete *every* lane on the host, including the transcripts §f deliberately preserves and the watermarks §f keeps through a wipe. **Decision:** `load` records a `loadSucceeded` flag; reconciliation runs only when it is true **and** the registry is non-empty, and otherwise logs and deletes nothing. Negative control in §5.

**A grant is not automatically one human, and *binding* must refuse the ones that are not — this is now a principal-binding rule, not only a lane rule.** The whole authority model keys on `pairedDeviceId`, but the ordinary regenerate-QR link *coalesces*: `getOrCreatePendingDevice` returns the first never-scanned entry of that scope instead of minting (`device-registry.ts:118-136`, the `find(...)` call at `:125-127`; `:128` opens the `if (existing)` — rev 13 cited `:125-129`, corrected here on re-opening) **[code]**, and the registry states the consequence itself — "two humans handed one coalesced link land on one deviceId and one token, so nothing downstream can tell them apart. A grant can only be split per person at offer time" (`:138-141`) **[code]**. If the two developers paired through that link they share one `deviceId`, therefore one lane; either one's switch moves the other's terminals, **R3 fails silently**. B2's per-person path is `mintPendingDevice`, which never reuses a pending row and stamps a deadline (`:142-160`; `pendingExpiresAt` written at `:102-103`) **[code]**. **Decision:** principal **binding** — and therefore lane provisioning — **refuses** a device entry that carries no `pendingExpiresAt`. That field is the durable discriminator — written at mint, surviving scanning, whereas `isMintedPendingDevice` additionally requires `lastSeenAt === 0` and stops matching the moment the person connects (`device-registry-pending-grants.ts:14-16`) **[code]**. Consequence, stated: a pre-B2 or coalesced grant cannot be bound to a principal, and therefore cannot hold a lane, until it is re-paired per person — the correct failure, because nothing on the host can tell whether such a row is one human or two.

**The lane env must NOT be gated on the launch command. This is the correction rev 5 most needed.** Rev 5 threaded `laneGrantId` into `prepareClaudeAuth` and stopped — but `prepareClaudeAuth` is reached only when `isClaudeLaunch` is true (`pty.ts:4589-4592`, `:6205-6208`, predicate `:4517-4518`/`:6153-6154` over the regex at `:1924-1931`) **[code]**. So a lane-pinned pane created as a plain shell — bare `terminal.create` with no `command`, `session.tabs.createTerminal`, a worktree default tab, any non-Claude `startupAgent` — would receive **no** lane env, and the human typing `claude` at that prompt would resolve `~/.claude` (`runtime-paths.ts:14-25`) **[code]**: on this box, the other developer's credential. Meanwhile §h would still render the pane `credentialLane: 'grant'` from its `lanePrincipalId` and §k would attribute its statusline usage to the lane owner — silent misattribution, the exact mode the funnel exists to prevent. **Decision: decouple.** A lane-pinned spawn (`credentialLane.kind === 'principal'`, no `args.connectionId`, normalized target runtime `host`) gets the lane `envPatch` **regardless of `args.command`**; `isClaudeLaunchCommand` keeps its existing jobs and gains a lane arm. Three consequences, all deliberate:
1. `stripAuthEnv: true` now arms on **every** lane pane, so `hasClaudeAuthEnvConflict` (`environment.ts:59-72`) **[code]** refuses a lane pane that defines Anthropic auth vars and `authEnvToDelete` (`pty.ts:4742-4744`, `:6467-6469`) **[code]** deletes them from it. That is a behavior change for plain shells in a lane, and the right one.
2. `markClaudePtySpawned` (`pty.ts:5363-5365`, `:6998-7000`) **[code]** keeps its command-detected arm **and** additionally registers every lane-pinned pane into that lane's "may hold lane credentials" set, because the human can type `claude` at any prompt. Deliberately conservative: it over-defers rotation (§e) rather than risk a double rotation. The cost — a lane whose panes never run `claude` never gets a *host* rotation — is absorbed by the CLI's own refresh plus §c's `cli-observed` receipt, and it is what makes §f's "a lane launch fails closed when the lane is unloaded" true for ordinary panes.
3. The second Claude-compatible runtime, **OpenClaude**, is missed by the command regex entirely (`openclaude` has no path separator before `claude`, so `:1924-1931` cannot match), and decoupling gives it the lane *env* where a command regex never could. **But rev 7's “covers it for free” claimed isolation, and rev 8 grades that claim: it is unsupported.** Orca's own OpenClaude integration hardcodes `configDirName: '.openclaude'` (`claude/hook-settings.ts:28-31`), resolved as `join(homedir(), configDirName, 'settings.json')` (`:72-74`) **[code]**, and the tree contains no `OPENCLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and no config-dir env for it at all — `grep -rn "OPENCLAUDE_" src` returns **nine** hits (rev 13 said three, and the count is corrected here on re-opening rather than the claim quietly restated): `claude/hook-settings.ts:28`, `openclaude/hook-service.ts:2` and `:7`, and six in `claude/hook-service.test.ts` — none of them a config-dir env, which is the substance the sentence rests on and which re-opening confirms **[code]**. Whether the binary honors `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is **[inferred] and unverified**, so the design refuses the assumption instead of resting on it: if it does not, an OpenClaude pane in a lane shares one credential store across grants while §h renders `credentialLane: 'grant'` and §k bills the usage to the lane owner — the worst class in this design. **Decision, fail closed:** the host **refuses to build an OpenClaude launch for a lane pane** (`terminal.lane_agent_unsupported`), and lane provisioning marks OpenClaude unavailable *in that lane* — a lane-scoped, host-computed version of row 15's disable. What that cannot cover is a human typing `openclaude` at a lane prompt; there the managed OpenClaude hook reports the session per pane — the reporting is in the **shared** `ClaudeHookService`, whose hook script posts `--data-urlencode "paneKey=${ORCA_PANE_KEY}"` and exits early when that variable is unset (`claude/hook-service.ts:103-118`, the paneKey field at `:112`) **[code]**; `openclaude/hook-service.ts:4-8` merely constructs that service with `OPENCLAUDE_HOOK_SETTINGS`, and rev 13 cited the construction as if it were the reporting — so an observed OpenClaude session in a lane pane **downgrades that row to `credentialLane: 'shared-runtime'` and drops its usage** rather than misattributing it. Named residual in §4, control in §5, and a §5 live step settles the question on the pinned binary — if OpenClaude does honor `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, this item collapses to one allowlist line.

**Lane derivation *and binding* live at the `createTerminal` funnel, not at one handler.** `runtime.createTerminal` (`orca-runtime.ts:26639-26641`) **[code]** has **twenty** production call sites — fourteen `this.createTerminal(...)` inside `orca-runtime.ts` (`:17328`, `:22595`, `:22657`, `:22694`, `:22985`, `:23027`, `:23743`, `:23867`, `:24094`, `:24219`, `:26322`, `:26515`, `:27257`, `:27715`) plus `terminal.ts:1542`, `coordinator.ts:382`, `orchestration-worker-topology.ts:66`, `orchestration-federation.ts:171` and `ipc/worktree-remote.ts:315`/`:367` **[code]** — and several are reachable by a paired grant. **S9a makes `credentialLane` a required field of `TerminalCreateOptions`** (`orca-runtime.ts:1457-1507`) **[code]**: `{ kind: 'principal', principalId } | { kind: 'shared' } | { kind: 'inherit', fromPtyId }` — rev 10's `{ kind: 'grant', grantId }` re-keyed, not merely renamed (§2a). Required, not optional — the compiler enumerates every call site and a new spawner cannot be added without deciding. `createTerminal` asserts it at runtime and **throws for any caller — identified, anonymous, or host-internal — that supplies neither a lane nor an explicit `{ kind: 'shared' }`**. Fail-closed is a property of the field being required, not of the caller having an identity; rev 5's "throws for an identified caller" left the host-internal path (below) silently sharing. The resolved value is not passed onward as a launch option: it is **bound to the pane** the funnel is minting (§2 preamble), and every spawn edge reads it back from there.

**Per-site derivation** — the §2 table's rows 1–3 expanded only where a site needs its own rule. `terminal.create`, `session.tabs.createTerminal` and `worktree.create` derive the lane as `principalOf(socket.pairedDeviceId)` (`terminal.ts:1535`, `session-tabs.ts:60`, `worktree.ts:100`) **[code]** — the funnel resolver is `deviceId → principalId → lane` (§6), not `deviceId → lane`; `shared` for an anonymous socket **and for a socket whose grant is bound to no principal**, which is rev 10's lane-less grant byte-for-byte. `terminal.createAgentSession`/`ensureAgentSession` (`orca-runtime.ts:26322`, `:26515`) need a **distinct field**: `callerContext` collapses identity into one string, `pairedDeviceId ?? clientId` (`agent-session.ts:247`, `:253`) **[code]**, so `RuntimeAgentSessionRpcCaller` (`shared/agent-session-host-authority.ts:142`) **[code]** gains its own `pairedDeviceId`. `orchestration.workerStart` on both topology paths (`orchestration-worker-topology.ts:66`, `:137`), federated worker start (`orchestration-federation.ts:171`), the coordinator loop (`coordinator.ts:382`), `terminal.split` and relay-lease pane recovery (`orca-runtime.ts:17328`) take `inherit` through the ownership predicate below — recovery failing closed when the rehydrated record carries no lane (§h). `launchAgentTerminal` (`:27257`, local IPC), `ipc/worktree-remote.ts:315`/`:367` and the automation path (`index.ts:2613`) pass `shared` **explicitly**.

**The worker path that does not go through `:64`.** For `worktree: 'new-child' | 'new-top-level'` the worker's terminal and setup pane are created inside `runtime.createManagedWorktree({ … startupAgent: args.agent … })` (`orchestration-worker-topology.ts:137-160`) **[code]**, which reaches the nine `orca-runtime.ts` worktree sites. `createManagedWorktree`'s args carry no caller identity at all — only `lineage.callerTerminalHandle: params.from` (`orca-runtime.ts:22822-22867`; `lineage` at `:22866`; the call's `callerTerminalHandle` at `orchestration-worker-topology.ts:158`) **[code]**. Under the §2 table's row 3 that would resolve to **shared**, on the *primary* orchestration path, while the narrative says the worker runs on the coordinator's authority — a worker silently on the host/other developer's credential, mixed-lane with its own worktree, and no throw, because rev 5's rule only fired for an identified caller. **Decision:** (i) `createManagedWorktree` gains a required `credentialLane` in its args, resolved at `orchestration-worker-topology.ts:137` from `lineage.callerTerminalHandle` (already `params.from`) through the same `resolveInheritedLane` predicate as `:66`; (ii) the funnel's throw covers host-internal callers, per the rule stated above. `worktree.create` and the renderer path pass their own value explicitly, the automation path (`index.ts:2613`) passes `shared`. **The federated create is the one site whose lane rev 12 could not state, and saying "the link's grant" was not a shorthand for a principal — it named a key the required field has no case for.** `credentialLane` is `{ kind: 'principal', principalId } | { kind: 'shared' } | { kind: 'inherit', fromPtyId }` (above), and §2a's pane-binding decision says normatively that the value persisted on a pane's binding row **is a principal id, never a grant id**, for the two reasons stated there. There is no grant case to bind to. Re-opened, the site has neither of §2a's two derivations available: `orchestration-federation.ts:90` **[code]** carries `lineage: { noParent: true }` (`:106`) **[code]** so `inherit` has no source pane, and the handler's only caller identity is `orchestrationMutation.callerFingerprint`, stamped into the dispatch row as `homePeerFingerprint` (`:63`) **[code]** — a **home-peer fingerprint**, not a `pairedDeviceId`, so `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` has nothing to read. **Decision: the federated create binds `{ kind: 'principal', principalId }` when — and only when — the federated *link* carries a host-side principal binding, written by the same consent action and into the same registry that binds a device (`principal-registry.ts`, §6); otherwise it *fails closed* (`terminal.lane_link_unbound`).** It never binds a grant id, because the union has no such case and the pane row must not hold one; and it never falls through to `{ kind: 'shared' }`, because an implicit shared lane on a federated worker is the silent downgrade onto the other developer's credential that this whole subsection exists to prevent. **Consequence, stated rather than assumed away: no link→principal binding exists in this tree today**, so until the registry write below lands a federated worktree create fails closed for **every** caller — the correct failure, and also a real functional cut, since it is the primary federated-dispatch path. After it lands, the same refusal is what an **unticked** link still gets. **Decision, rev 15 — §7 question 8 answered (b) by the orchestrator, 2026-08-22: that binding ships, as one more consent write rather than as a new mechanism.** `principal-registry.ts` gains a **fifth** operation beside bind / unbind / re-bind / designate-the-pusher (rule (iii) above): **bind a federated link to a principal**, keyed by the link's **home-peer fingerprint** — the only stable link identity this tree has, stamped onto the dispatch row as `home_peer_fingerprint` (`runtime/orchestration/types.ts:205`, table column `runtime/orchestration/db.ts:504`) and already the value the federation paths authorize against (`orchestration-federation-relay.ts:227` and `orchestration-federation-control.ts:175`, both `attachment.home_peer_fingerprint !== callerFingerprint`) **[code]** — written by the **local human** at the same consent surface, audited with the same row as a device bind, and read at `orchestration-federation.ts:63`, where that fingerprint is already in scope. **The cost is one extra human tick per link and nothing else,** which is the whole of what option (b) buys over option (a): an unticked link still fails closed `terminal.lane_link_unbound`, so the conservative behaviour stays the default and the human opts in per link rather than per create. It remains a *host-side consent write*, which is what keeps it inside this section's provisioning rule — no client and no home peer can cause it. **But the key needs a rule, and rev 15 shipped the binding without one — round 4's blocker, and it is a fail-*open* one.** Rev 15 called the home-peer fingerprint “the only stable link identity this tree has” and cited only where the value is *stored* and *compared*. Re-opening the function that **produces** it changes the decision. `orchestration-federation.ts:63` stamps `homePeerFingerprint: orchestrationMutation.callerFingerprint`; that identity is built at `rpc/orchestration-mutation-executor.ts:32` from `authenticatedCallerFingerprint(request)`, whose entire body is `createHash('sha256').update(request.authToken || request.deviceToken || 'authenticated_transport').digest('hex')` (`:128-134`) **[code]**; `dispatcher.ts:100` passes it through, and no production caller supplies `callerFingerprintOverride` — the parameter at `:26` is its only other occurrence in `src` **[code]**. So the fingerprint is a hash of *whatever credential the request carried*, and that is two different things. **(1) Every local-socket caller collapses onto one value.** `RuntimeRpcServer` mints a single `authToken = randomBytes(24).toString('hex')` per runtime (`runtime-rpc.ts:507`), publishes it in the transport metadata (`:1807`), and `parseAndAuth` refuses any request whose `authToken !== this.authToken` (`:1664-1669`) **[code]** — so the `orca` CLI, the Electron renderer and, per §2m(6), **either developer** on this one-Windows-account box all present the same token and hash to the same fingerprint. Bound as written, a `principalId` attached to that value would make `orchestration.federationAttachStart` — which requires only a durable `orchestrationRequestId` (`orchestration-federation.ts:27-33`) **[code]** — create a worktree, its startup terminal and its setup pane **in that principal's lane for the other developer**. That is the exact fail-open twin of the silent downgrade `terminal.lane_link_unbound` exists to prevent, arriving through the one authority write rev 15 added, and R1 failing through it. **(2) For a paired peer the key is a *grant*, not a link.** Mobile and desktop clients put `deviceToken` on every request (`mobile/src/transport/rpc-client.ts:1057`, `:1092`; the relay twin at `mobile/src/transport/mobile-relay-rpc-session.ts:202`) **[code]**, so the fingerprint is `sha256(deviceToken)` — per grant, and **mutable by construction**, since a re-pair mints a fresh `token: randomBytes(24).toString('hex')` on the row (`device-registry.ts:97`) **[code]**. Rev 15's closing clause — “it never introduces a grant-keyed lane, because what it writes is a `principalId`” — confused the value with the key. What it *writes* is a principalId; what it is **keyed by** is a hash of one grant's device token, which has the two properties §2a rejected when it refused to persist a grant id on a pane and refused to bind a coalesced `getOrCreatePendingDevice` row (`device-registry.ts:118-136`, `:138-141`) **[code]**: non-unique across the local class, and mutable. **Decision, rev 16 — the binding refuses any fingerprint that is not derived from a per-link credential, and the rule is three clauses.** **(a) Two values are refused by name.** The host knows its own `authToken` (`runtime-rpc.ts:507`), so at tick time it computes `sha256(authToken)` and `sha256('authenticated_transport')` and refuses to bind either — the first is the shared local-socket identity of clause (1), the second is the fallback literal at `orchestration-mutation-executor.ts:132` **[code]** that any authenticated transport carrying neither token lands on. Neither names a link. **(b) A bindable fingerprint must resolve to exactly one paired grant.** The consent surface offers the tick only for a fingerprint that equals `sha256(device.token)` for exactly one row in the device registry — the host holds every `token` (`device-registry.ts:97`) **[code]**, so this is a local computation over the rows it already has, not a new protocol — and the binding row records `{ homePeerFingerprint, principalId, boundDeviceId, boundAt }` so the audit row names the grant the human actually ticked rather than an opaque hash — **the `principalId` of which rev 17 drops, for the reason stated two sentences on**. Zero matches, or more than one, is not offered and not bindable. **And clause (b) collapses the fifth consent write into a lookup, which rev 16 did not notice and round 5 did — so the disposition is stated rather than leaving two bindings whose disagreement has no defined winner.** A federated home peer *does* reach this host as an ordinary paired grant: `callOrchestrationWorkerServer` runs over the environment transport (`orca-runtime.ts:4946-4994`) **[code]** and paired clients put `deviceToken` on every request (`shared/remote-runtime-client.ts:140`; mobile twins `mobile/src/transport/rpc-client.ts:1057`/`:1092`, `mobile/src/transport/mobile-relay-rpc-session.ts:202`) **[code]**, which is exactly why clause (b) is satisfiable at all. But the `boundDeviceId` clause (b) resolves **already carries a `principalId`**, written by the *first* consent write — so a second `principalId` on the link row is a duplicate that can disagree with it, with no rule saying which wins. **Decision, rev 17: the link row does not carry a principal of its own.** It persists `{ homePeerFingerprint, boundDeviceId, boundAt }`, and the federated create reads `principalOf(boundDeviceId)` — one authority, one revocation point, and no second place for a mis-tick to hide. **What the fifth write still is, because option (b)'s cost does not change:** it is the tick that authorizes *this link* to create in that grant's lane at all — clause (a)'s two refusals by name, clause (b)'s exactly-one-row check, and the audit row are its whole content, and a federated create from an unticked link fails closed `terminal.lane_link_unbound` even when its grant is bound to a principal. One extra human tick per link stands; what does not stand is rev 15–16's implication that the tick names a principal. **One consequence, named rather than discovered later:** unbinding `boundDeviceId` from its principal leaves the link resolving to nothing and its next create refused `terminal.lane_link_unbound` — the same join §2f's wipe walks, and the correct direction. **(c) A re-pair unbinds, silently and correctly.** Because clause (b) keys on the token, a re-paired link's fingerprint no longer matches its stored row, and a federated create from it gets `terminal.lane_link_unbound` — the conservative refusal — until the human re-ticks. That is stated as a **cost**, not as an elegance: re-pairing a federated link costs one tick, and the failure between the two is a refusal rather than a create in the wrong lane. **What this leaves true and what it gives up:** it gives up federated creates from an anonymous local caller (clause (a)), which is correct, because that caller is not a link and §2a's `resolveInheritedLane` case (ii) already covers the in-pane ergonomic path; it keeps the whole of option (b)'s benefit for a real paired home peer; and the lane is still keyed by a `principalId` — what changed is that the *route* to that principalId is now required to be per-link, which is what rev 15 asserted and did not enforce.

Anonymous local launches (Unix-socket CLI, Electron renderer bridge) keep today's shared `~/.claude` lane, as do Orca's own host-side `claude` probes, which take the other env path entirely (`applyClaudeEnvPatch` is called only from `text-generation/commit-message-agent-environment.ts:131` and `rate-limits/claude-pty.ts:247`) **[code]**. Threading below the funnel: `lanePrincipalId` on `RuntimePtyWorktreeRecord` (beside `connectionId`, `:1399-1427` esp. `:1403`) **[code]** and on the pane's persisted binding row (§h), carrying the **principal id** resolved at bind time and never a device id → resolved at the spawn edge by pane identity — on path A it arrives as a host-computed field of the in-process `ptyController.spawn` args (never IPC JSON), on path B it is *looked up*, because that caller is the renderer — → `prepareClaudeAuth`, whose type grows from `(target)` to `(target, lanePrincipalId?)` (`ipc/pty.ts:1264-1268`, call sites `:4637-4638`, `:6205-6208`) **[code]** and whose *call condition* becomes `isClaudeLaunch || lanePinned`. `getPreparation` gains a branch returning `{ configDir: lanePath, envPatch: { CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR: lanePath }, stripAuthEnv: true, managedRefreshDeferredByLivePty, provenance: 'lane:<opaqueLabel>' }` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:660-743`) **[code]**, structurally like the WSL branch at `:683-697`. `managedRefreshDeferredByLivePty` is **not** dropped: the host branch returns it (`:733-737`) **[code]** and it drives the "Waiting for Claude session" UI state; the lane branch computes it from §e's account-scoped query.

**`provenance` must not carry the grant id either.** §k argues the posted `configDir` has to become an opaque label before reaching any peer-readable row, because after the re-key it embeds another **principal's** host-minted `principalId` (rev 13 still said "another grant's `pairedDeviceId`" here and in §2k; the *decision* is unaffected, but the premise was stale and a `principalId` is a different identifier with different properties — host-minted per person, never a device-row key) — and `provenance` is a second carrier of the same identifier: it is copied into `lastClaudeAuthSnapshot.provenance` (`rate-limits/claude-usage-attribution.ts:68-71`) and thence into `usageMetadata.authProvenance` (`:1523`) **[code]**, a *published* field (`shared/rate-limit-types.ts:41`) **[code]** already rendered in settings/stats surfaces. **Decision:** the lane's provenance is `lane:<opaqueLabel>`, where the label is a random token minted with the lane and stored beside it — never the `pairedDeviceId`. **What that buys, stated exactly:** it bounds *incidental* disclosure in published rows (usage metadata, status surfaces, logs). It is **not** a confidentiality property — R4 gives the other developer typing access to a lane pane, so `env | grep CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` or `/proc/<pid>/environ` — on the Windows host, the process environment block (§2m(6)) — discloses the **principal id** directly, and §h renders the owner label on the row anyway. No later slice may rely on the id staying host-private. And the predicate it must satisfy is a negative one: `isManagedClaudeAuth` keys on `provenance.startsWith('managed:')` (`rate-limits/claude-fetcher.ts:495`) **[code]** and gates CLI usage supplementation; a lane provenance must **not** match it, because §k scopes the usage *pull* to the shared lane and lane usage arrives by the statusline path instead.

**`terminal.split` inherits.** The split handler destructures only `{ runtime }` (`terminal.ts:1569-1579`, `defineMethod` at `:1569`, `name` at `:1570`, handler destructure at **`:1614`**) **[code]**, and `splitPtyBackedTerminal` (`orca-runtime.ts:28554-28568`) reaches `ptyController.spawn` with no lane **[code]**. The lane is a property of the pane, not of the request: the split reads `lanePrincipalId` off the parent record. The renderer-notified branch (`notifier.splitTerminal`, declared `:2037-2045`, called `:28544-28548`) **[code]** carries no env and re-enters through the anonymous renderer path — it gains a `lanePrincipalId` field in those opts, and if a build cannot carry it the split **fails closed**.

**`inherit` needs an owner check.** "The worker runs on the coordinator's authority" says nothing about whether the caller has any relationship to the pane it names. `orchestration.workerStart` destructures no identity (`orchestration-workers.ts:29`) **[code]** and resolves the coordinator pane from the client-supplied selector `params.from` (`:31`; schema `orchestration-worker-start-schema.ts:27`) **[code]**; the only gate is that the pane is the Run's current consumer (`:32-38`, `consumer_fenced` at `:35`) **[code]**. So grant B names any pane bound to a Run — including grant A's — and mints a worker terminal, worktree and setup pane on **A's credential**. `terminal.split` is the same shape (`terminal.ts:1276`) **[code]**, **and its handler is not the only door into a split**: `agentTeams.tmuxCompat` (`terminal.ts:1663-1670`, handler destructuring only `{ runtime }` at `:1682`) reaches `this.splitTerminal` through `claudeAgentTeams.handleTmuxCompat` (`orca-runtime.ts:28829-28845`, `splitTerminal` passed at `:28839`) **[code]** with no `pairedDeviceId` at all — so the predicate below lives inside `splitPtyBackedTerminal`, over the parent pane's record, not in the `terminal.split` handler. This is **not** the §4 shell-equivalence concession — that concession is about bytes a co-tenant can read anyway; this is Orca's own supported RPC *granting B use of A's Claude account*.

**Decision — one ownership predicate, applied to every `inherit` edge.** `resolveInheritedLane(sourcePtyId, ctx)` returns the source pane's lane only when one of: **(i)** the source pane's `lanePrincipalId === principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)`, **both defined** — a *principal* comparison, not a device-id one, which is the whole of Q1's gain here and is stated at this site because this paragraph, not §2a's re-key list, is what an implementer reads when writing `terminal-inherited-lane-authority.ts` (§6). Rev 10 compared `laneGrantId === ctx.pairedDeviceId`; left standing, one person's phone could not split, worker-start from, or inherit their own desktop's lane pane — refused `terminal.lane_not_owned` at their own pane, the exact refusal Q1 exists to remove; **(ii)** the caller is anonymous (Unix socket or renderer) — today's behavior, and the path an agent *inside* A's pane takes when it runs `orca worker-start --from <its own pane>`, so the ergonomic case is untouched; **(iii)** the source pane is in the shared lane **and** the caller's principal holds no lane (a grant bound to no principal is included, since it holds none by definition). Anything else is refused with `terminal.lane_not_owned` — no fallback to `shared`, no silent downgrade. Attachment is deliberately **not** the predicate here: R4 gives B an attachment on A's pane, and §g's attachment check is right for `openInMyLane` only because that operation spends the *caller's* credential. Applies to `orchestration.workerStart` (whose handler gains `pairedDeviceId`) on **both** its topology paths (`:64` and `:133`), federated worker start, `coordinator.ts:382` (always case (ii)), `terminal.split` (gains `pairedDeviceId`) and pane recovery at `orca-runtime.ts:17328` — **not** host-internal: its client route is `terminal.recoverPane` (`terminal.ts:1245-1255`, handler destructuring only `{ runtime }`, client-supplied `paneKey`/`worktreeId`), which re-enters `createTerminal` with the client's own `tabId`/`leafId`. It does not escalate today only because `getRecentExpiredSshLease` (`:6893`, gate at `:17322`) bounds it to relay-lease panes, which are `credentialLane: 'remote'` and never lane-bound; §a(ii)'s adopt rule covers it if that bound ever widens, and it fails closed when the rehydrated record carries no lane (§h). Residual, named: an anonymous local caller can still inherit any pane's lane — same Unix user, inside §4's conceded envelope, and the reason (ii) is safe to keep.

**Branch order — the lane is *not* unconditionally first.** `getPreparation`'s WSL branches (`:612-626`, `:627-654`) run for a WSL target **[code]**. A lane path is a host-side `<userData>\claude-lanes\<id>` path; handing it to a Linux-side `claude` either hard-fails or silently creates a fresh empty config dir that prompts for login. The WSL branch is not a counter-precedent: it deliberately emits `wslLinuxAuthPath`, *a path visible from the far side* (`:622`) **[code]**. **Decision:** the lane branch is evaluated first only when `normalizeClaudeAccountSelectionTarget` (`runtime-selection.ts:17-26`) **[code]** yields runtime `host`; a WSL target falls through and the terminal is labelled `credentialLane: 'wsl'`. **That classifier is not the one that selects the WSL *shell* branch**, and the gap matters: the WSL export keys on the variable's mere **presence** — `if (finalEnv.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR) { addWslEnvKeys(finalEnv, ['CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR']) }` (`providers/local-pty-provider.ts:762-764`, mirrored `daemon/pty-subprocess.ts:762-764`) **[code]** — while that branch is chosen by `resolveWslSessionContext`/`getCodexSelectionTargetForPty`. On a Windows host a pane the two classify differently would export a Windows lane path into the distro through WSLENV, producing exactly the empty-config-dir failure above. **Invariant, asserted and controlled (§5):** a lane path is never present in a spawn env that takes the WSL branch. A WSL-visible lane is **S9e** (§2n), which narrows this invariant rather than deleting it — a *Windows* lane path is still never present, a *Linux* one under that distro's lane root is. **And the invariant needs an enforcement *site*. It is not `computeLaneLaunch`, and this is a correction to rev 12 rather than a preference: the input type says that function cannot see what the rule turns on.** `PtySpawnOptions` (`src/main/providers/pty-provider-contract.ts:41-108`, read in full) carries `shellOverride`, `terminalWindowsWslDistro` and `terminalWindowsPowerShellImplementation` and **no resolved shell and no `isWsl`** **[code]**. The value is computed *inside* the provider: `shellPath` is resolved at `local-pty-provider.ts:578-668` from cwd-derived `wslInfo`, the worktree WSL context, `this.opts.getWindowsShell?.()`, `COMSPEC`, Git-Bash resolution and an async pwsh probe, and the export gate reads exactly that resolved value (`:733`, export at `:764`) **[code]**. A refusal written at the anchor is therefore unimplementable there, and implementing it there would mean synthesizing a **fourth** shell classifier beside the two this section already warns are 22 lines apart (§2m(5)). **Decision: the assertion lives in the lane-aware `buildSpawnEnv` arm this design already created for exactly this class of problem.** The §2 preamble threads the pane's lane into that ctx (`local-pty-provider.ts:712-722`) — and that ctx is precisely where `shellPath` and `isWsl` already sit (`:718-719`) **[code]** — so the rule is evaluated over the *same* value the export gate reads, two steps later in the same function. It reuses the export gate's own predicate, `pathWin32.basename(ctx.shellPath).toLowerCase() === 'wsl.exe'` (`:733`), and deliberately **not** the broader `isWslShell` of `:707`, so it refuses exactly the panes the export would have touched and no others: **no new classifier is introduced.** The daemon twin resolves its own `shellPath` at the same gate (`daemon/pty-subprocess.ts:723`, export at `:762-764`) over an env it rebuilds in its own process (`:610`) **[code]**, so the assertion is stated over that value too, and a lane spawn on the daemon path that cannot carry the lane into it **fails closed**. `computeLaneLaunch` keeps its job unchanged — it computes the lane env at the anchor; it simply never sees the shell, and the design no longer pretends it does. The refusal is `terminal.lane_wsl_shell_unsupported` and carries a complete human sentence, because it surfaces from inside the provider rather than from a pre-spawn RPC check (§3's Rule-3 row). **What that mis-siting cost, pre-S9e, stated because it is the harm and not the tidiness:** a Windows lane path reaches `addWslEnvKeys` and crosses WSLENV into a distro that cannot use it, while the pane still renders `credentialLane: 'grant'` (§h) and its statusline usage joins to the lane's account (§k) — a pane labelled isolated, billed to its owner, and sitting at a login prompt in an empty config dir.

**SSH panes must never receive a lane path — and after the decoupling above, that guard becomes real.** Correction to rev 5: *no leak exists today*. `isClaudeLaunch` already requires `!args.connectionId` (`pty.ts:4517-4518`, `:6153-6154`) **[code]**, so for an SSH pane `claudeAuth` is null and the merge yields `env = sshScopedEnv` with nothing added (`:4632-4634`, `:6221-6223`) **[code]**. The exclusion is incidental behavior, not a fix. But decoupling the lane from `isClaudeLaunch` removes that incidental guard, so `args.connectionId` must become an **explicit** condition of the lane resolver, and the two-clause invariant of guard 3(a) becomes an asserted property with **both** a positive control and a mutation proof — deleting the `connectionId` condition must turn a named test red, since this is exactly the moment its accidental protection is withdrawn. Such a pane is labelled `credentialLane: 'remote'`.

**`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is a client-supplied key today, and that is a hole S9 must close first.** The merge is `{ ...sshScopedEnv, ...claudeAuth.envPatch }` (`pty.ts:4632-4634`) **[code]**; for a caller with no lane the host branch returns `envPatch: paths.envPatch` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:731`) **[code]**, which `runtime-paths.ts:23` **[code]** makes `{}` unless the Orca process itself inherited the variable. An empty patch lets the client's own `env.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` survive the spread verbatim. The only launch-time guard, `hasClaudeAuthEnvConflict` (`environment.ts:59-72`) **[code]**, does not list `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, and it runs only when `claudeAuth.stripAuthEnv` is true — itself false unless a managed account is selected (`:732`) **[code]**. **This is exploitable today, before any lane exists:** the injection target that exists right now is `<userData>/claude-accounts/<id>/auth`, a real managed-account credential store (`managed-auth-path.ts:12-14`) **[code]**, reachable by any identified grant. `terminal.split` carries an `env` record too (`terminal.ts:1054`, passthrough `:1577`) **[code]**, and `pty.ts:7015`'s own comment already flags `args.env` as "untrusted IPC JSON" **[code]**.

**And `terminal.*` is not the only door.** `session.tabs.createTerminal` accepts `env` (`session-tabs-schemas.ts:147`), `envToDelete` (`:148`) and `launchConfig` (`:150` → `shared/workspace-session-sleeping-agents.ts:66-73`: `agentCommand`, `agentArgs`, `agentEnv`, `ompResumeFilePath`) **[code]** and forwards every one (`session-tabs.ts:67-68`, `:72`) into `createMobileSessionTerminal` → `createTerminal` (`orca-runtime.ts:27720-27722`) **[code]**; `worktree.create` accepts `startupCommand`/`startupEnv`/`startupLaunchConfig` (`worktree-schemas.ts:172-174`) **[code]**. Both are lane-derived rows (§2 rows 1 and 3), and their payloads are §2 rows 5–9. Three escapes follow: **(a)** `envToDelete: ['CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR']` merges into the spawn's deletion list and is applied *after* the patch merge, so the launch silently falls back to the shared `~/.claude` — on this box, the other developer's credential — while the pane still carries `lanePrincipalId`, presence (§h) and usage (§k) still name the lane owner: R1/R3 fail silently, and an *override* check does not see a *deletion*. **(b)** An `agentArgs` flag that redirects settings resolution reaches the CLI unfiltered, so §j's allowlist inspects files the CLI was told to ignore — and `apiKeyHelper`/`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` from such a file **outrank** stored logins **[doc]**. **(c)** `ompResumeFilePath` and `agentEnv` escape §g's containment check for the same reason.

**Fix, in S9a, before any lane is provisionable — as the closure principle's single computation point.** `computeLaneLaunch` runs for every spawn whose **pane record** carries a lane, at the convergence point named in the §2 preamble — `spawnForStablePane` (`pty.ts:907-919`) plus the agent-session-ensure spawn (`:5043`), i.e. over the `spawnOptions` both assembly points hand to `provider.spawn`. That is downstream of all twenty `createTerminal` call sites, of `splitPtyBackedTerminal`'s own spawn (guard `:28569`, spawn `:28602`) **[code]**, of the renderer-backed create, of the mobile-session create, of the wake, of cold restore and of the `agentTeamsPlan` re-derivation alike — one anchor instead of rev 7's two, under the two-part post-anchor invariant stated in the §2 preamble rather than rev 7's “nothing may re-derive” blanket. Three guards over four vectors:

1. **Env and deletions.** Strip `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, every `CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS` key (`environment.ts:9-14`) and `ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS` — absent from that tuple but auth-bearing by the tree's own model, deleted under `stripAuthEnv` when `isAuthLikeCustomHeaders()` says so (`:23-24`) and tested as the second arm of `hasClaudeAuthEnvConflict` (`:44`) **[code]** — from `options.env`, from `options.envToDelete`, and from `launchConfig.agentEnv` — silently for `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, loudly (typed refusal) for the auth vars. Deletion counts as much as override, and it must be stripped from **`spawnOptions.envToDelete` itself**, not merely from the host env: the provider replays the list after its own env build (`local-pty-provider.ts:725-726`, and once before it at `:689-691`; daemon twins `pty-subprocess.ts:622`/`:781`) **[code]**, post-anchor. Path B hands the merged list over as `spawnOptions.envToDelete = combinedEnvToDelete` (`pty.ts:6522`) **[code]**, so the sanitation must sit on that object on both paths, not only on the host env applied at `:4839`/`:6498`. Sources: `terminal.ts:1009`/`:1010`/`:1015` and split `:1054`/`:1577`; `session-tabs-schemas.ts:147`/`:148`/`:150`; `worktree-schemas.ts:173`/`:174` **[code]**.
2. **Launch strings.** `launchConfig.agentCommand`/`agentArgs` (`workspace-session-sleeping-agents.ts:66-67`) **[code]** are parsed and matched against an allowlist for a lane launch; a settings-, config-dir- or auth-redirecting flag is refused (`terminal.agent_args_refused`). `ompResumeFilePath` (`:71-77`) and `resumeProviderSession.transcriptPath` (`terminal.ts:1027`) **[code]** are containment-checked against the caller's own lane root and the workspace (§g).
3. **A post-merge assertion in `pty.ts`, on *both* spawn paths, stated as a condition rather than a blanket.** The only place all three sources converge is the spawn env itself. Path A builds `env` at `:4632-4634`, merges deletions at `:4770-4783` and applies them at `:4839`; path B builds at `:6221-6223`, merges at `:6470-6482` and applies at `:6498` **[code]**. The invariant is *"no lane path and no client-supplied `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` enters an env carrying `args.connectionId`"* — two clauses with different scopes, so the assertion branches on `args.connectionId`: **(a) `connectionId` set (SSH pane):** assert `claudeAuth === null` and that **no value in the merged env canonicalizes under `<userData>/claude-lanes/`**; the client's own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is **left alone**, because it names a config dir on the *remote* host, has nothing to do with host lanes, and the env is `sshScopedEnv` verbatim (`:4632-4634`, `:6221-6223`; `stripRemotePaneEnvWhenHooksDisabled` removes only `ORCA_*`, `pty.ts:972-994`) **[code]**. Deleting it would be a silent, undocumented break of a legitimate remote setting, which `AGENTS.md:40-42` requires us to consider. **(b) `connectionId` absent:** when the auth patch carries a lane path, assert `env.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` equals it **and** `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is absent from the merged deletion list; when it does not, assert no client-supplied `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` survives. Clause (b) is **not** behind `stripAuthEnv` and **not** conditioned on a lane existing, because the injection is exploitable in the zero-lane state, where the target is a real managed-account store (`managed-auth-path.ts:12-14`) **[code]**.

Each guard carries its own mutation proof, and §5 runs the four vectors through **all three** spawning RPCs.

**A lane loses Orca's managed hooks unless we mirror them.** `getConfigPath` is `join(homedir(), settings.configDirName, 'settings.json')`, with no `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` awareness (`claude/hook-settings.ts:78-80`) **[code]**, so a lane launch reads a `settings.json` that has never been written and loses status/notification hooks and the statusline. S9a writes the managed hook block into each lane's `settings.json` at lane creation and on hook refresh, alongside the mirrored allowlist below — and never `env` or `apiKeyHelper`.

**What else a lane loses — and the recorded decision this reverses.** `global-settings-types.ts:253` records "persist only per-account auth (not a `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` swap) so switching accounts doesn't fork Claude's shared chat/session context" **[code]**. That is right for the problem it was written for — *one human*, several of their own accounts, one machine, where forking history is pure loss. S9's problem is the inverse: *two humans*, one machine, where a shared history is the thing to remove. The reversal is deliberate, scoped to grants, and the shared lane keeps today's behavior byte-for-byte. **The loss list, complete and with a decision per item**, because §1's own [doc] bullet concedes `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` moves `settings.json` and `.claude.json` as a unit: **(i) sessions/transcripts** — `--resume`/`/resume` sees only that lane's sessions; **not mirrored** either way (they are the other developer's content, and copying them into a credential directory defeats the wipe promise); **(ii) user-level memory** (`~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`, `memories/`) — **mirrored** host→lane at creation and on hook refresh (workspace guidance, not secrets; silent loss would be the worst regression here); **(iii)** `resumeProviderSession.transcriptPath` (`terminal.ts:1027`) **[code]** points outside the lane for any pre-lane session — refused by §g containment, the message naming the shared-lane pane as the place to do it; **(iv) permission allowlists** — `permissions.allow`/`deny`/`ask`/`additionalDirectories` **mirrored** (without them every agent re-prompts for every tool, on both lanes, permanently), but `permissions.defaultMode` **not** (a host-wide `bypassPermissions` must not silently widen a lane); **(v) `model`/`outputStyle`** — mirrored (preferences, no credential influence); **(vi) the user's own `hooks` and `statusLine`, and `env`/`apiKeyHelper`/`awsAuthRefresh`/`awsCredentialExport`** — **never** mirrored: the first two are owned by the lane's managed block (§k depends on it), the last four are exactly the credential-redirecting keys §j exists to keep out; **(vii) `.claude.json` `mcpServers` and per-project `mcpServers`/`enabledMcpjsonServers`** — **mirrored**, minus any entry whose `env` names `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` or a `CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS` key; the security reasoning is that an MCP entry names a *subprocess*, cannot redirect Claude's own credential resolution, and is reachable by a co-tenant per §4 anyway, whereas zero MCP servers in every lane is a larger regression than either Q9 ask (**Q9 = confirmed**: the mirror list and the forking both ship as written); **(viii) per-project trust map** — **not** copied (a trust decision belongs to the grant that runs the agent), so each lane re-prompts once per workspace; **(ix) onboarding-completion and theme keys** — copied by allowlist (§ fresh-lane bootstrap); **(x) project prompt `history`** — not copied (the other developer's prompts); **(xi) plugins/marketplace state** — **not** mirrored in S9, named as an accepted loss because plugin state is coupled to caches inside the config dir, Q9. **(xii) user-level `agents/`, `commands/`, `skills/` and custom `output-styles/`** — all inside the config dir and therefore relocated as a unit **[doc]**, so an unmirrored lane starts with zero custom slash commands, zero user subagents, zero user skills and zero custom output styles: the same silent-regression class as (iv), and the omission round 7 caught. **Mirrored** host→lane, one-way, on memory's schedule and for memory's reason — authored workspace tooling, not secrets. Two caveats stated: such a file can carry arbitrary shell, which §4’s shell-equivalence already concedes for this Unix user’s own files; and a lane’s *own* additions are not mirrored back (Q9). So the lane `settings.json` rule is not "the managed block and nothing else" but **"the managed block plus the mirrored allowlist above, all computed host-side, and nothing client-supplied"** — clause (ii) of the closure principle, item by item. The mirror is one-way host→lane, recomputed at creation and on hook refresh; the only lane-scoped *override* path is that grant's own push (§b), which is clause (i). §5 asserts each item individually — "reaches a usable prompt" would pass while most of them were broken.

**Fresh-lane bootstrap, with the trust question answered.** A lane's `.claude.json` carries first-run state as well as identity, so a lane's first terminal may sit at an onboarding prompt instead of running **[inferred]**. S9a seeds it with `oauthAccount` plus an **explicit allowlist** copied from the host's own `.claude.json` (`runtime-paths.ts:27-33` resolves which file that is) **[code]**: onboarding-completion and theme/appearance keys, plus the MCP entries item (vii) mirrors, and nothing else. The per-project trust map is **not** copied — that would inherit the host's trust acceptances for workspaces the grant never approved. Consequence: each lane's first launch in a workspace re-prompts for trust, once per lane. Correct, because a trust decision belongs to the grant that runs the agent.

### (b) What the desktop pushes

The owner's desktop pushes **the selected account only**: the byte contents of that account's `.credentials.json` and `oauth-account.json` from `<userData>/claude-accounts/<id>/auth/` — the two filenames Orca already reads and writes (`managed-auth-path.ts:68-81`, `:83-93`) **[code]**. From `oauth-account.json` the host needs, and stores, only what it already parses: `accountUuid`/`accountId`, `emailAddress`/`email`, `organizationUuid`/`organizationId` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:986-988`) **[code]** — enough to merge `oauthAccount` into the lane's `.claude.json`, enforce §e's residency invariant, and render an identity label. **Plus exactly one thing it does *not* parse out of those files, added in rev 11 because Q3 has no carrier without it: a bounded `displayName`** — the owner-authored per-account name, ≤64 characters, printable, no control characters, sent as a third envelope member and stored beside the lane, never derived from `oauth-account.json`. Without it Q3's answer has nowhere to live: everything else the host holds about the account comes out of a file the pusher wrote (below), and §2l's delegable list — the other place the name appears — is readable only by grants of that principal. The host never receives ids of unselected accounts, usage rows, or the desktop's store. **Rev 22 corrects this sentence, which until now also said “the owner's account *list*” and is false the moment §2l step 1 ships (§8 item 2).** §2l requires the desktop to publish a **delegable-account list** so the phone has something to tap, and §3 row 12 gives it its own write. What the host receives is therefore exactly that list and nothing more: **only the accounts the owner ticked**, each as an opaque host-minted `delegatedAccountId` plus the same two bounded display fields the push envelope already permits — the owner-authored `displayName`, and an `email` **only when the owner opted that account in**. No credential, no account id from the desktop's store, no usage, no sha, and nothing at all for an account the owner did not tick. **The widening is deliberate and its blast radius is §4's:** the rows persist host-side in `claudeLaneDelegationRows`, on a box §4 grades shell-equivalent to a paired peer, so a co-tenant who is already shell-equivalent learns the owner's ticked account **names** — which §2d already discloses one of, as Q3's peer-visible label — and any email the owner opted in. §2d's projection still keeps the list itself readable only by grants of that principal. **Owner ratification required:** Q3 answered “owner label + friendly account name” for the peer-visible label; it did not answer “the host may hold the ticked *list*”, and §7 question 11 now asks that in as many words.

**Payload validation, before any byte reaches the lane.** The envelope has exactly these three members and no others — the two files, each bounded (≤64 KiB), and the optional `displayName`, bounded at 64 characters and rejected outright if it carries control characters; `.credentials.json` must parse and satisfy the shape predicate Orca already uses — a `claudeAiOauth` object with a non-empty `accessToken` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:927-929`) **[code]**, extracted so both callers share it — and `oauth-account.json` must parse to a JSON object. Anything else is refused `accounts.lane.push_malformed` **before** the `0600` write, so a well-formed-envelope-but-garbage blob fails at the RPC and not at the next launch.

**And the account identity is client-asserted.** Everything the host knows about *whose* account this is comes from the pushed `oauth-account.json` (`:965-978`) **[code]**; a pusher can put any email in it. The **owner label is the *principal's*** — host-observed, resolved from `pairedDeviceId` through `principalOf` (§2a, §2h), and not spoofable; the **account email** is. Q3's phrase "grant/owner label" is the owner's wording and it survives as the *name of the field*; what it renders under Q1 is one person, not one device, so a person's desktop and phone carry one label rather than two. **Q3 is answered: grant/owner label plus a friendly per-account name the owner chooses — not the email by default.** That is the right answer precisely because of this asymmetry: the label the design can vouch for is shown, the string it cannot is not, and the owner-authored name gives the useful part of the email without the spoofable part. The email remains available behind an explicit owner opt-in. **The name's own grade, since it is now on the wire and peer-visible (§2d, §2k):** `displayName` is asserted by the **owner's own desktop grant** rather than parsed out of a file that grant merely relayed, so it is exactly as trustworthy as its owner and no more — a peer sees a string one identified grant chose, next to a principal label the host observed.
