That is what Q3 asks for; it is not a claim about the account behind it, and the design does not present it as one.

### (c) A live switch is a push — and something must re-read the lane

Switching on the desktop pushes the newly selected blob into the same lane. The host writes it atomically at `0o600` and merges `oauthAccount` into the lane's `.claude.json`. No PTY restart, no notification to the CLI: every live `claude` in that lane re-resolves on its next credential read, exactly as today's host-wide switch does; every terminal in another lane, and in the shared lane, is untouched because their `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` points elsewhere. R2 and R3 become one property: *the write is scoped to the directory the launch was pinned to*. There is no separate remote "selection" RPC to secure.

**But a writer alone is not enough.** Today's protection against clobbering a CLI-rotated token is the read-back inside `doSyncForCurrentSelection` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:410-453`, persisted at `:440`) **[code]**, driven by launches and usage ticks (`:137-177`) **[code]**. A lane whose writer "does nothing else" never observes a rotation performed by the lane's *own* live `claude`: the desktop's copy goes stale, and the ordinary post-wipe reconnect re-push (§f) then restores an already-revoked blob, after which every terminal in that lane fails `invalid_grant`. **S9b therefore ships a lane sync driver**, `syncLane(laneId)`, the per-lane analogue of `doSyncForCurrentSelection`, on exactly four triggers: (1) before any launch pinned to that lane (the lane arm of `prepareForClaudeLaunch`, `:137-163`), (2) on each rate-limit tick for a lane with live PTYs (`prepareForRateLimitFetch`, `:165-177`) **and — rev 11's second arm, without which §2k's post-probe sync cannot fire — for any lane the per-lane usage pull *probed* on that tick, evaluated after the probe exits and irrespective of whether the lane still has live PTYs**, (3) immediately before applying a push, (4) once per lane at startup, before the wipe (§f). **Trigger (4) is observe-only** — it compares, emits the receipt and records the watermark, and never rotates; §e says why. It compares the lane file's refresh token against the lane's last-written value using the existing comparator (`readRefreshTokenFromCredentials` `:955-963`, `compareRefreshTokens` `:979-984`) **[code]**; a difference means the lane's CLI rotated, so **the receipt covers CLI-observed rotation, not only host-initiated rotation**.

**The watermark has three writers, not one — and rev 5 named only the first.** `syncLane` records it (triggers 1–4); a **successful push** sets it to the pushed blob's refresh-token sha256; and every rotation receipt (`host` or `cli-observed`) sets it to the rotated token's sha. Without the push arm the freshness rule in §e eats itself: `syncLane` runs immediately before applying a push (trigger 3) and records the *pre-push* file's sha, so the next push — which carries `basedOn` = the sha it pushed last time — would mismatch, be refused `accounts.lane.push_stale`, and **R2 would work exactly once per lane**. Ordering: validate → freshness-check against the current watermark → write → set the watermark to the new sha, all under the lane's write queue and the lane's switch gate (§f).

**The two writers cannot be reused as-is.** `writeRuntimeCredentials(contents)` is private and opens with `const credentialsPath = this.pathResolver.getRuntimePaths().credentialsPath` (`:1743-1744`) while mutating the shared `lastWrittenCredentialsJson` (`:1760`); `writeRuntimeOauthAccount(oauthAccount)` opens with `const configPath = this.pathResolver.getRuntimePaths().configPath` (`:1584-1585`) **[code]**. Neither takes a path, and there are 17 `getRuntimePaths()` call sites in this one file **[code]**. The reusable primitive is `writeFileAtomically(path, contents, { mode: 0o600 })` from `codex-accounts/fs-utils` (imported at `:12`, used at `:1759` and at `managed-auth-path.ts:8`, `:92`) **[code]**. **Decision:** the lane writer is a new file calling that primitive directly and owning its own per-lane last-written cache; `runtime-auth-service.ts` gets a path-taking extraction budgeted in S9b (§6), not an in-place widening — AGENTS.md forbids disabling `max-lines` (`AGENTS.md:18-20`) **[code]**.

### (d) Authorization

Lane RPCs derive their target lane from `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` **only** — never from a parameter, and the params schema has no lane or principal field to spoof. The derivation is two host-side lookups, both host-written: socket → `device.deviceId` (`runtime-rpc.ts:1384`) **[code]** → the registry row's `principalId` (§2a). A grant bound to no principal addresses no lane at all, and is refused exactly as an anonymous caller is. This is the pattern the Run mailbox already argues explicitly (`rpc/methods/orchestration-remote-run-mailbox.ts:11-22`) **[code]**. Anonymous callers are refused: a Unix-socket caller cannot address a lane at all.

**Every refusal below is conditioned on the *caller's own* lane state, never on "some lane exists on this host."** A host-global dormancy switch would let one grant's single RPC downgrade what every other client may do — the cross-grant influence §a's provisioning rule closes.

- `accounts.selectClaude` (`accounts.ts:118-127`) materializes the chosen account into the shared `~/.claude` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:477-478`) **[code]** and is gated on **two independent conditions, checked in this order**. *Caller scope:* **anonymous local callers** unchanged and host-wide (this is `orca account select` and the host's own renderer); **identified callers with no provisioned lane** unchanged and host-wide — they own no lane, their terminals are in the shared lane, and today's semantics are right for them; **identified callers that hold a lane** — **refused** `accounts.selection_out_of_scope`, telling them to switch in their own lane. Not silently rescoped: a client that today believes it moved the host must not believe it moved something it did not. *Target residency — L1's second edge:* **for every caller class, including the anonymous local one, the call is refused `accounts.lane.account_resident_elsewhere` when the target account is resident in any lane.** Without this, the ordinary pre-S9 state (both developers' accounts already in `<userData>/claude-accounts/`) lets a lane-**less** peer — or either developer's local shell, or the renderer — call `selectClaude(X)` after A pushed X into A's lane and re-create the exact double residency L1 exists to prevent, single-use refresh token and all. The check is the same predicate `removeClaude` uses, written once as `assertNotLaneResident(accountId)` over §e's index and called from `selectClaude`, `removeClaude` and `accounts.lane.push`; the refusal message names the holding **principal's** presence label and the remedy (`accounts.lane.clear`, addressed to that principal's lane) — principal, not grant, per §2a's re-key list, because the label a peer reads and the lane a remedy names are both keyed by the person.
- `accounts.removeClaude` (`:187-191`): refused for an identified caller when the target account is **resident in any lane** (target-scoped, not host-scoped) — deleting it would strand a live lane. Unrelated accounts remain removable.
- `accounts.addClaudeFromConfigDir` (`:197-212`) and `accounts.addCodexFromHome` (`:213-225`) are **not** safe as they stand. Their `clientKind` guard refuses identified sockets but not anonymous local callers (`:203-205`, `:217-219`) **[code]** — and on this box the anonymous local caller is *either developer*, same Unix user. `captureFromExistingConfigDir` accepts any resolved path containing `.credentials.json` with no containment check (`claude-accounts/service.ts:208-249`, resolve at `:217`) **[code]**, then `writeClaudeManagedAuthFile` copies the blob into `<userData>/claude-accounts/<newId>/auth/` permanently (`:823-834` esp. `:832`; `:836-847` esp. `:842`) **[code]**, after which `selectClaude` makes it host-wide. So `orca account add --config-dir <userData>/claude-lanes/<A>` converts a co-tenant's momentary read (§4) into permanent host-managed possession — defeating the wipe promise with one supported command. **Fix (S9a):** refuse a `configDir` whose **canonical** path is the `claude-lanes` root or under it, and mirror it for `addCodexFromHome`'s `sourceHome`. A path-level test is not enough: `captureFromExistingConfigDir` calls `resolve(trimmed)` at `:217` and the next check follows symlinks through `existsSync` at `:221` **[code]**, so `--config-dir /tmp/decoy` linking into a lane would defeat a `startsWith` on the resolved string. The check canonicalizes exactly as `managed-auth-path.ts` does — refuse a symlink outright (`:23-25`), `realpathSync` both sides (`:26-27`), then `startsWith(canonicalRoot + sep)` (`:28-32`) **[code]**.
- `accounts.list` (`:102-115`) and `accounts.subscribe` (`:182-217`) are wholly unscoped disclosure paths: today grant B receives the host's full account snapshot including emails and usage. `subscribe` is identity-blind by construction — it destructures only `{ runtime, connectionId }` (`:185`) and emits `runtime.getAccountsSnapshot()` verbatim at **both** the fan-out (`:187-189`) and the ready frame (`:212`) **[code]**. S9b newly destructures `pairedDeviceId` and converts the shared snapshot into a per-connection **projection** at both emit points as well as in `list`. The projection shows (i) host/shared-lane accounts and (ii) the caller's own lane row, whose usage comes from §k; another principal's lane appears only as an opaque `occupied` boolean, the principal's presence label, and — because Q3 is explicitly about "the identity label the other developer sees" — the account's `displayName` **when its owner has set one**, and nothing else: no account id, no email unless the owner opted in, no usage, no sha, no lane path. That single peer-visible string is the one deliberate widening of this projection, and it is stated here rather than left to §2k to imply. With zero lanes the projection is byte-identical to today's snapshot.

Compatibility consequence, in full: a paired client **loses** host-wide Claude switching exactly when it has been given its own lane — i.e. only after the local human granted it one. A grant with no lane keeps everything it has today, including (residual, §4) the ability to move the shared lane under lane-less peers — for accounts that are **not** resident in a lane; that one exception applies to every caller class. **Q5 = RESOLVED: NO — the shared lane stays open, and the residual is accepted with its name on it.** The owner declines to lock the shared lane once a lane exists. So a grant bound to no principal keeps host-wide `accounts.selectClaude` over every account that is **not** lane-resident, for every caller class, exactly as today. **The residual, stated in full and accepted:** such a grant — either developer's un-bound phone, an old client, a freshly paired device before the human binds it — can move the shared `~/.claude` (on the Windows host, `%USERPROFILE%\.claude`) under **every other lane-less terminal on the box**, including terminals another person opened before their grant was bound. It cannot touch a provisioned lane, it cannot name a lane-resident account, and it cannot make a lane pane resolve anywhere but its lane; what it can do is move the *default*. The mitigation is procedural, not technical, and the design says so: **bind and provision both developers before either relies on the box**, because the window in which this residual matters is exactly the window before binding. The alternative — locking the shared lane to local callers once any lane exists — was rejected by the owner as too blunt, and it is a host-global switch of the kind §2a's provisioning rule otherwise forbids one grant to flip.

### (e) Refresh — the host rotates, keyed by account, not by lane

**Decision: the host.** Refresh tokens are single-use; a double rotation revokes one copy. Only the host can observe the fact that makes rotation safe — whether a live `claude` holds that credential — and it already encodes that: rotate only when no live PTY holds the creds, otherwise defer and let read-back preserve whatever the CLI rotated (`runtime-auth-service.ts:459-475`, `:410-453`) **[code]**. The desktop cannot see that and would race the CLI.

**Which store a lane rotation writes to.** Not managed storage: `refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded` (`:1032-1050`) persists through `writeManagedCredentials(account, …)` (`:1009-1022`), which requires a `ClaudeManagedAccount` and an Orca-owned `<userData>/claude-accounts/<id>/auth` path (`:1013-1015`; the service-side twin asserts the same at `claude-accounts/service.ts:823-834` esp. `:828`) **[code]** — and §b is explicit that the host never receives the owner's account store, so a lane account has **no** managed record to persist into. S9b's lane rotation therefore calls the shared network primitive `refreshClaudeOauthCredentials(credentialsJson)` (used at `:1063`) **[code]** and persists through §c's lane writer into the lane's own `.credentials.json`, then emits the receipt and moves the watermark. **Trigger:** the §c sync driver on (1)–(3) — launch, rate-limit tick, pre-push — and **not** on (4). The startup pass must be observe-only because it runs *before* `seedLiveClaudePtysFromPersistence`, and `index.ts:2241` states that ordering invariant in so many words — "run before `ClaudeRuntimeAuthService`'s constructor sync — a surviving daemon Claude CLI holds the single-use refresh token; early refresh rotates it out mid-session" (seeded `:2249-2250`, reconciled `daemon/daemon-init.ts:1043-1061`) **[code]**. At that instant the lane-scoped liveness query is empty by construction, so a rotating startup pass would hand that lane `invalid_grant` across every live session in it. There is no separate timer.

**Invariant L1 — one account, at most one lane.** The single-use-refresh invariant is per **account**, so keying rotation by *lane* is unsound: nothing otherwise stops account X being resident in grant A's lane and grant B's lane (or in a lane and the shared lane), and the host — the only party that can see both — would rotate X on B's behalf while A's live `claude` holds it. The docstring on the rotation path states the precondition a per-lane split would break (`:1041-1043`) **[code]**. **Therefore:** the host maintains a residency index and refuses **both** edges through one predicate, `assertNotLaneResident(accountId)`, stated once in §d and called from `accounts.lane.push`, `accounts.selectClaude` (every caller class, including anonymous local) and `accounts.removeClaude` — `accounts.lane.account_resident_elsewhere`, naming the holding **principal's** presence label (§2a's re-key; the residency index keys its rows by principal). It keys **rotation, deferral and the refresh serialization axis by account across all lanes** — never by lane. `managedRefreshDeferredByLivePtyAccountId` (`:106`) is already account-keyed **[code]** and stays that way; what becomes lane-aware is only the *liveness query* feeding it, single-valued precisely because of L1 — and, per §a consequence 2, fed by every lane-pinned pane, not only command-detected Claude launches.

**The index's `host` row cannot come from the managed account, because usually there isn't one.** The shared lane legitimately holds an **unmanaged system login**: `runtime-paths.ts:14-25` resolves `~/.claude`, and `getPreparation`'s host branch returns `provenance: 'system'` with `stripAuthEnv: false` whenever `activeAccountId` is null (`:655-670`) **[code]**. That is the default on a box where nobody ran `orca account add`. With no host row, a lane push of the very account whose refresh token is live in `~/.claude` is accepted, the single-use token is resident in two config dirs, and an `accountUuid`-keyed axis cannot see the unmanaged side. **Fix:** the index derives its `host` row from the shared lane's files directly — identity via `readIdentityFromOauthAccount(readRuntimeOauthAccount())` (`:963-976`, `:1549-1564`) **[code]**, plus, because an unmanaged login may carry no `oauthAccount`, a second residency key of `sha256(readRefreshTokenFromCredentials(<shared .credentials.json>))` (`:953-961`) **[code]**. A push is refused if **either** key collides. Every lane row carries both keys. **And the `host` row is re-derived, not merely seeded at startup:** it is recomputed on every shared-lane `doSyncForCurrentSelection` (launch and rate-limit tick, `:113-127`) **[code]** and immediately before each `selectClaude`, `removeClaude` and push residency check — otherwise an external `claude login` into `~/.claude`, or a `selectClaude` landing between two syncs, produces a collision nothing observes. Reading two small files on those paths is the same cost the sync already pays.

**The desktop must be explicitly suppressed.** The owner's desktop runs its own `ClaudeRuntimeAuthService` over the same managed account — it must, to have selected and pushed it. Its rotation is gated only by `hasLiveClaudePtys()` (`:407`), a module-global set fed exclusively by that machine's own spawns (`markClaudePtySpawned` is called only from `pty.ts:5364` and `:6999`) **[code]**. The desktop cannot see the host lane's live PTYs, so whenever no local `claude` runs and the token is expiring it calls `refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded` (`:414-417`) **[code]** and rotates the token out from under the lane. **S9b adds a delegation lease:** while an account is `delegatedToLane: { hostId, principalId, delegatedGrantId, since }` (rev 11: the lease names the principal that owns the lane **and** the one grant inside it that is allowed to push — §2l says why both are needed), (i) `refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded` is a no-op for that account on **every** desktop bound to the principal — **the `delegatedGrantId` itself included**; (ii) **every** such desktop refuses its own managed launches under it, the lease-holder included; (iii) the lane's rotation receipt is the only writer of that account's stored credential; and **(iv), new in rev 14, every such desktop clears that account out of its *own* runtime `~/.claude` / `%USERPROFILE%\.claude` and refuses a local `selectClaude` of it — the third copy rules (i) and (ii) left untouched, below**. **And the lease must reach a third copy of the credential, which rev 13's rules did not touch — this is round 3's fourth major and it lands on the *pushing* desktop, not on a second install.** Rules (i) and (ii) govern Orca: its rotator, and its managed launches. Neither governs the desktop's own **runtime credential file**. A prior (or subsequent) `accounts.selectClaude` on the owner's own machine materializes that account's blob into that machine's `~/.claude` / `%USERPROFILE%\.claude` — `writeRuntimeCredentials(credentialsJson)` over `pathResolver.getRuntimePaths().credentialsPath` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:477-478` — `:477` resolves the paths and `:478` is the write; rev 16's `:476-478` included a blank line and is corrected here, matching §1 and §2d) **[code]** — and §2d's `assertNotLaneResident` cannot see it: that refusal is a **host-side** RPC gate whose index, by §2e's own admission, "can only enumerate lanes on this host". A plain `claude` in any non-Orca terminal on that Windows 11 desktop then reads that file and rotates the lane's single-use refresh token, and Orca's live-PTY gate on that machine cannot see it either (`markClaudePtySpawned` is called only from `ipc/pty.ts:5364` and `:6999` **[code]**). That is R-DR2's exact mechanism on the machine §2e and §2l both describe as the *safe* one — "one person, one principal, **one pushing and one rotating desktop**" — and it is covered by neither §4's residual, which says "a second Orca **install**", nor §2e's operating rule, which says "must not be live in another Orca install". **Decision — rule (iv), on the delegating desktop, stated as a clearing action and not only a refusal:** taking the lease for account X **clears X from that desktop's own runtime credential file** (the same sweep §2f performs on a lane, applied to `getRuntimePaths().credentialsPath` and the `oauthAccount` key in the co-located `.claude.json`) when X is the materialized account, and while the lease stands that desktop **refuses its own `accounts.selectClaude` of X** with the reason named — the local twin of §2d's residency refusal, on the one machine §2d cannot reach. **What rule (iv) does not reach, named rather than claimed closed:** an *external* `claude login` into that desktop's `~/.claude`, or a copy of X placed there by anything that is not Orca. That is R-DR2 arriving on the **ordinary** configuration rather than only on the laptop/reinstall class, and §4's residual is re-worded for it rather than left implying that only a second install can produce it. **The lease is durable, not process-local:** persisted in the desktop's own store, reloaded at start, released only by an explicit `accounts.lane.clear`, a grant revoke, or expiry — never by a desktop restart or a dropped connection. **And rev 11 makes it *host-published*, which is the change that lets it suppress a rotator it does not run in.** The host holds the principal's `delegatedGrantId` — **written by the local human at the same consent surface that binds grants, at any time, persisted in the principal registry, and by nothing else** — and publishes it on the lane status stream §2c already ships, where §2d's projection makes it readable by **every** grant of that principal and by no one else. **Rev 13 offered a second writer — "or pinned from the `delegation` member of the first accepted push" — and it is deleted in rev 14, because it could not ship beside the rules §3 row 1 and §5 state.** Those say flatly that a push is refused unless the caller's grant *is* the `delegatedGrantId`, with no first-push carve-out. Implemented that way against a trap-door lease, the two rules contradict; implemented as the trap-door, an **authority** value is written by a client action — which contradicts §2a's own provisioning rule ("no client can cause it") and lets any grant of the principal seize the lease by racing to push first, the second desktop this very section spends a paragraph closing included. Implemented as the *refusal alone* with no writer at all, a principal whose human never set a lease could never push, so no lane would ever load: R2 never starts, Q2's `pullRotated` has nothing to pull, and §2l's phone path — step 3 is "the desktop performs an ordinary `accounts.lane.push`" — is dead, i.e. Q1 unmet. **The lease therefore has a bootstrap and it is the consent action (§2a):** provisioning a lane designates its pusher in the same tick — one tick, for the one-human-one-desktop case — a lane cannot exist without a designation, and a principal with none is not provisionable at all (`accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated`, §2a). The designation is a choice among that principal's **already-bound** grants, is offered at any time on the same surface, and is **persisted** with the bindings, so nothing about it depends on a socket being open when the human ticks. **There is no eligibility predicate — the designation is a human tick over the principal's already-bound grants, persisted in the principal registry (§2a), and rev 19 deletes every predicate that stood here before it.** Rev 15 keyed it on `scope: 'runtime'`; rev 16 on a capability the *host* advertises and the *client* checks, which put no per-grant value in the host's hands and refused every provisioning (round 5's blocker); revs 17 and 18 on a capability the client declares, carried by a process-lifetime memo because the socket record dies with the socket. **All three are gone, for one reason: every candidate signal is either absent from this tree or asserted by the client itself, so none of them is evidence.** `'runtime'` covers an `orca` CLI grant as well as a desktop (`runtime-types.ts:56`, `runtime-rpc.ts:660`) **[code]**; `clientCapabilities` is copied verbatim out of the client's own handshake (`rpc/core.ts:78-79`, set at `rpc/e2ee-channel.ts:256`) **[code]**; and `clientKind`, which *is* host-stored (`rpc/core.ts:77` ← `runtime-rpc.ts:1784`) **[code]**, is only that same `scope` under another name, fixed at pairing-offer creation (`runtime-rpc.ts:725`) **[code]** and therefore a fact about the invite rather than about the device (§2a states this at length). **What replaces the predicate is not a weaker predicate but a different authority:** the local human decides which bound grant pushes, and a wrong decision is inert — the lane receives no push, `laneState` stays `absent`, the UI says so by name, and one re-tick fixes it (§2a). Designating an `orca` CLI grant is exactly that case: a recoverable mis-tick, not a refused designation, and this section claims no prevention it cannot deliver. Moving the lease is that same consent surface, audited like a re-bind. A desktop's own store is therefore a *cache* of the host's value, not the authority: on reconnect the host's value wins, and a desktop that has ever seen a delegation for account X keeps suppressing X across restarts and disconnects until the host publishes that the delegation is **released** — an `accounts.lane.clear`, a revoke, or expiry. Being *named* as the `delegatedGrantId` is not what lifts suppression, and rev 12 made it so; release is. §2e's suppression rule (i) is then stated over the published value rather than over the local one: **a bound desktop suppresses `refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded` for any account the host publishes a delegation for — with no exemption for the desktop that is itself the named `delegatedGrantId`** — and a dropped connection does not un-suppress it. **Rev 12 wrote that rule with the holder exempted, and it was wrong in the way that mattered most, so it is corrected here rather than adjusted.** The `delegatedGrantId` *is* the pushing desktop — the exact machine whose rotator this paragraph opens by describing. Exempting it leaves the unmodified local gate `if (!liveClaudePtys) { refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded(…) }` (`:415`, `:419-427`) **[code]** running on that machine over a live-PTY set fed only by its own spawns (`markClaudePtySpawned` is called from `ipc/pty.ts:5364` and `:6999` and nowhere else; `live-pty-gate.ts:97-102`, `:138-176`) **[code]** — so on the **ordinary** principal, one desktop and one phone, an unsuppressed rotator sits on the account's single-use refresh token while the host lane's live `claude` holds it. That is `invalid_grant` across every terminal in the lane, R2 broken, and R-DR2 re-imported into the case this section claims to close; it also makes §5's own control ("a delegated account on the desktop with an expiring token and no local PTY does **not** call `refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded`") unpassable, because that desktop is the `delegatedGrantId`. **What the lease actually names is one *pusher* and one *puller*, never one licensed rotator.** The `delegatedGrantId` is the single grant permitted to `accounts.lane.push` (so §2c's watermark keeps one writer) and the grant that pulls each rotation receipt back into its own store (Q2, `accounts.lane.pullRotated`). Rotation itself belongs to the **host**, keyed by account (this section's opening decision) — no desktop rotates a delegated account, and the holder is not an exception to that, it is the reason the rule exists.

**Q1 does not add a rotator, and one shape of it does — stated exactly before R-DR2 is restated.** Under principals a lane can be reached by a person's desktop *and* their phone. The phone adds **no** rotator: it holds no credential store, runs no `ClaudeRuntimeAuthService`, and never calls `refreshClaudeOauthCredentials` — established from the tree in §2l, not assumed. So for a lane whose principal has one desktop, the rotator set is unchanged from rev 10: the host (keyed by account, above) plus that one desktop, suppressed by the lease. **The shape that does add one is a second *desktop* inside one principal.** Two desktops of one person each run their own `ClaudeRuntimeAuthService` over their own managed store, and only the lease-holder is suppressed — which is R-DR2 arriving through the front door that Q1 just opened. **A push refusal alone does not close it, and rev 11 claimed it did — the non-sequitur is retired here.** Rotation is not a push: the mechanism is the unmodified local gate `if (!liveClaudePtys) { refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded(…) }` (`:409`, `:413-421`) **[code]** running on that second machine over a live-PTY set fed only by its own spawns (`markClaudePtySpawned` is called only from `ipc/pty.ts:5364` and `:6999` **[code]**). Refusing that machine's *push* leaves its *rotator* untouched, and L1's residency index cannot reach it either — by its own admission it enumerates lanes **on this host** only. **Decision, in two parts, because one part is a closure and the other is a residual and they must not be conflated.** **(1) Suppression, which is the actual closure:** the lease is host-published (above), so a second desktop that is **bound to this principal and running an S9-aware build** reads the principal's `delegatedGrantId` off the lane status stream, sees a value that is not itself, and suppresses its own `refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded` for that account — the same no-op the lease-holder's own suppression uses — because the holder suppresses too — keyed on the **existence of a published delegation for that account**, not on the identity of the holder and not on a push arriving. It never rotates, so there is nothing to refuse. **(2) The refusal, which is a consequence of (1) and not a substitute for it:** a push from any grant of this principal that is not the `delegatedGrantId` is refused `accounts.lane.push_not_delegated` until the local human moves the lease, so the watermark keeps a single writer even in the window before a second desktop has read the stream. **What (1) does not reach, named rather than claimed closed:** a second desktop that is **not bound**, **never connected**, or on a **build with no lease code** never sees the published value and rotates exactly like the laptop/reinstall case. That is R-DR2, unchanged and unfixable host-side, and it is listed as such below and in §4 — not as a shape Q1 closed. One person, one principal, **one pushing and one rotating desktop**; the phone is exempt because it never pushes and never rotates (§2l).

**The lease closes one install, and only one — R-DR2, named.** Rev 5 justified the gap with "the lease is per-desktop and cannot see other desktops' leases — which is why L1 is enforced host-side, where both lanes are visible." That is a non-sequitur: L1's residency index can only enumerate lanes **on this host**. A *second copy* of account X in another machine's managed store — the human's laptop, a reinstall, or a desktop on an old build with no lease code — runs the unmodified gate `if (!liveClaudePtys) { refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded(…) }` (`:409`, `:413-421`) **[code]** over a live-PTY set fed only by that machine's own spawns, and rotates the single-use token out from under the lane; every live terminal there then fails `invalid_grant`. §c's receipt/pull path can observe it, never prevent it. **No host-side control exists** — the host cannot reach another machine's store. What ships is detection and recovery: the pushed envelope carries `delegation: { hostId, grantId, since }`; `syncLane` treats an unexpected sha change whose refresh then fails as `laneState: 'reauth-required'` with a receipt naming the cause; and the desktop UI states the operating rule — *an account delegated to a host must not be live in another Orca install*. Accepted residual, in §4 and in the degradation matrix.

**Per-lane state is bigger than it looks.** `ClaudeRuntimeAuthService` holds eight mutable process-global fields (`:111-119`) **[code]**. Three consequences: (1) `lastWrittenOauthAccount`/`hasLastWrittenOauthAccount` (`:116-117`) gate the `.claude.json` identity write, so a lane push that updates them makes a later **host** sync see "already written" and skip — the host `.claude.json` would then report grant A's account, the exact misattribution R1 exists to prevent; (2) `skipNextReadBackForAccountId` (`:118`) is keyed by **account**, so the re-key must be `(lane, account)`; (3) the single `mutationQueue` (`:111`) would serialize all lanes' pushes head-of-line — each lane gets its own write queue, with rotation still serialized per account.

**Push freshness, honestly.** (1) The host rejects a push whose `basedOnRefreshTokenSha256` does not match the lane's current **watermark** — as maintained by all three writers in §c — unless marked `reauthenticated: true`; that flag is client-asserted and therefore advisory, so the host additionally compares issued-at/expiry and refuses a strictly older push. (2) An **empty** lane is not a blanket exemption: an empty lane whose watermark is present is checked against it exactly as a loaded lane is; only a lane with *no* watermark (never pushed, or deprovisioned and re-provisioned) accepts any well-formed push. (3) The host emits a secretless rotation receipt (identity, refresh-token sha256, `expiresAt`, cause `host` | `cli-observed` | `foreign-rotation`) on the lane status stream, and the desktop pulls the rotated blob back over the same grant-scoped channel — the mirror of Orca's existing read-back-to-managed-storage (`:364-366`, `:389`) **[code]**.

### (f) Lifecycle

Lane credential files are wiped when the **principal's** last connection closes — the last authenticated socket of *any* grant bound to that principal, not of any one grant (§2a; that re-key is the one B2's own predicate cannot express, so the wipe site asks the socket registry directly rather than reusing `hasOtherConnections`). B2 computes the *grant*-scoped predicate the lane wipe must **not** reuse: `handleClose` reports `hasOtherConnections` (`rpc/mobile-socket-wiring.ts:209-221`, comparison `:215-219` esp. `:218`) and `runtime-rpc.ts:1408` branches on `!hasOtherConnections`, calling `onClientDisconnected(socket.device.deviceToken)` at `:1409` **[code]**. **Key mismatch to note explicitly:** that path compares and passes `device.deviceToken`, while a lane is keyed by the **principal** its grants are bound to — two hops from the token, not one: `socket.device.deviceId` (in scope at the wipe site, and what `registerConnection` is handed at `runtime-rpc.ts:1384`) **[code]** resolved through `principalOf` (§2a). `socket.device.deviceId` is in scope at the wipe site, and the wipe must read that, not the token it is handed.

**Wiping at close is not enough: it must also happen at startup.** Close detection rides `transport.onConnectionClose` (`mobile-socket-wiring.ts:102`) **[code]**, and a crash, `kill`, or ordinary quit never runs that handler — so a lane's `.credentials.json` would otherwise stay resident with no grant connected until some *future* connection happens to close. Orphan reconciliation (§a) does not cover it: a still-paired grant's lane has a matching device entry.

**The startup wipe has a trap of its own, and the watermark defuses it.** `seedLiveClaudePtysFromPersistence` exists precisely because a daemon-hosted `claude` survives an Orca restart still holding the single-use refresh token (`index.ts:2241` comment, seeded `:2249-2250`, reconciled `daemon/daemon-init.ts:1043-1061`) **[code]**. If S9c wipes lanes and the reconnecting grant then pushes into a lane that accepts anything because it is empty, an older desktop blob lands under a surviving daemon session that has since rotated. **Fix:** the startup sequence is (1) an **observe-only** `syncLane` over each resident lane — compare, emit the receipt, record the **watermark**, never rotate (§e) — the watermark being a secretless `{ laneId, identity, refreshTokenSha256, expiresAt }` row persisted beside the live-PTY session ids; (2) wipe the credential material; (3) seed the live-PTY gate; (4) bind the listener. A re-push is then checked against the watermark even though the lane is empty (§e rule 2), so the stale blob is refused `accounts.lane.push_stale`. This is the same reason the persisted live-PTY rows gain a lane id (§3 row 6): a seed with no lane attribution defers **all** accounts until `confirmSeededClaudeLivePtys` resolves it (`live-pty-gate.ts:65-70`, `:82-94`) **[code]**.

**Residency window, stated next to the promise:** the credential is resident from push until the earliest of last-close, idle/keepalive timeout, or the next Orca start; a half-open TCP connection that never emits close extends it to the idle timeout. §4 concedes a co-tenant can read the file at any resident moment; the UI states the window rather than promising instant removal.

**The wipe must fence an in-flight usage probe first, and rev 13 gave it no rule for one — round 3's second blocker.** §2k joins the lane usage probe to three mechanisms (refresh deferral, the switch gate, the watermark) and stops there; the close-wipe is the fourth and it was missed. The probe is a real `claude` running under the lane's `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (row 32: `spawnEnv` is `applyClaudeEnvPatch({ ...process.env, TERM }, authPreparation.envPatch)` at `rate-limits/claude-pty.ts:247-251`, spawned at `:303-311`) **[code]**, and §2c's own `cli-observed` cause exists precisely because such a CLI **writes a rotated `.credentials.json` back into the config dir**. A sweep that runs while one is in flight is a sweep the probe can undo: the lane the header promises is "wiped when the **principal's** last connection closes" then holds a live credential at rest afterwards, `laneState` reads `loaded` rather than `absent`, and §2a consequence 2's "a lane launch fails closed when the lane is unloaded" — which that consequence calls load-bearing — is false. **Decision: the wipe takes the same staleness path §2k already defines for a mid-flight push, and the sweep runs only after the probe's process is confirmed dead.** The lifecycle hook marks every probe in flight in that lane stale, invokes `cleanupHiddenRateLimitPty(term, termDisposables, { kill: true })` (`rate-limits/hidden-pty-cleanup.ts:25-58`, `term.kill()` at `:36`) through the handle named in §2k — `fetchViaPty`'s existing `options.signal` (`claude-pty.ts:221`), wired at `:349-358` to `settleAborted` (`:335-347`), which performs exactly that call at `:345` **[code]** — drops the probe's result, and **then** sweeps. A probe that cannot be confirmed dead inside a bounded window leaves launches failing closed with the sweep retried rather than reported done, because reporting a wipe that did not happen is the failure this rule exists to prevent. **But `laneState` is a *published* value (§2h) and `'absent'` is the value this very section treats as the proof the credential is gone — so publishing `'absent'` during a retried sweep asserts a removal that may not have happened, which is the residency-window honesty §2f otherwise insists on. Rev 16 separates the two properties rather than overloading one string.** `laneState` still reads `'absent'` in that window, because it is the value **launches** key on and failing closed is the right direction for them; alongside it the row carries an additive **`laneWipePending: true`** (Rule 1 — a new optional boolean, ignored by every old client), set the moment the lifecycle marks the lane wipe-pending and cleared only after the post-sweep re-read comes back clean. So the launch path reads one value and the *residency* claim reads the other: while `laneWipePending` is set, nothing in this design or its UI may state that the credential is gone. **The old-client consequence, stated:** a client with no string for the new field renders `absent` alone — conservative for launches, and wrong about residency — which is why the honest statement lives in the host's own surfaces (§2h, S9d) and not only in the flag. **And the fence has a second half, which the kill alone does not give it: a probe must not *start* into a lane the wipe has already claimed.** Killing what is in flight closes the window that was open when the close arrived; it says nothing about the tick that fires a millisecond later, while the sweep is still running or has just returned, and *that* probe's `claude` can rotate and write `.credentials.json` into a directory the host has already reported wiped — the same harm through the other door. **Two start-side preconditions, evaluated by the lane pull rather than by the sweep (§2k states them at the implementer's site):** no probe is started for a lane whose **wipe is pending or in progress**, and none is started for a lane whose `laneState` is **`absent`** — the second is what §5's existing "a lane with no credential loaded is skipped, not probed" already asserts; the first is new in rev 15 and is the one that closes the race. The lifecycle marks the lane wipe-pending **before** it aborts anything, so the whole sequence is mark → abort → confirm dead → sweep → re-read → clear the mark, and there is no edge inside it that admits a probe. **And because a precondition is a check rather than a proof, the sweep re-reads before it reports:** after sweeping, the lane directory is re-scanned for credential artifacts, and anything found — the only way one gets there is a probe that slipped the preconditions — is swept again, with the wipe reported only after a clean read-back. A wipe reported over an unread directory is the same failure as one that never ran. Wipe = **sweep the lane directory for credential artifacts**, not delete one filename: `.credentials.json` plus every `.credentials.json.*.tmp` and any other stray `*.tmp` sibling. `writeFileAtomically` stages at `${targetPath}.${process.pid}.${randomUUID()}.tmp` (`codex-accounts/fs-utils.ts:12`) and unlinks it only on a *thrown* error (`:17`) **[code]**, so a crash between the `writeFileSync` at `:14` and the rename at `:15` leaves a full-credential blob at `0600` under a name a filename-scoped wipe never touches. Lane **open** sweeps too. Then delete the `oauthAccount` key from the lane's `.claude.json` (plus the lane's scoped Keychain item on macOS, §i), drop the lane's rows from §e's residency index, and **keep** the watermark. The lane dir, its `settings.json`, its mirrored memory and its transcripts survive — they are not secrets, and destroying transcripts on dropped Wi-Fi would be hostile. On reconnect the desktop re-pushes; until it does, a lane launch **fails closed** ("your account is not loaded on this host"), never silently falling back to the shared lane. **Revoke, stated principal-scoped here rather than only in §2a — this paragraph is the normative home of the wipe, and rev 11 left it grant-keyed.** A revoke removes the *grant*. The lane directory and its watermark are removed **only when the revoked grant was that principal's last**: the hook resolves `principalOf(deviceId)` **before** the registry row disappears, and after the removal asks whether any surviving row is still bound to that principal — if one is, the lane, its files and its watermark are untouched and the principal's other grants keep launching into it. **The ordering is forced by the sites.** Both revoke paths in this tree are grant-scoped, identical in shape, and call `removeDevice(deviceId)` *before* `forgetGrant(deviceId)`: `revokeMobileDevice` at `runtime-rpc.ts:648` then `:653`, and `revokeRuntimeAccess` at `:660` then `:666` **[code]**. So a wipe hooked *beside* `forgetGrant` alone runs after the `DeviceEntry` is already gone and cannot tell a last grant from a first — it must capture the principal at entry and evaluate the survivor query after `removeDevice` returns true. **What the grant-keyed version would have cost, named because rev 11 shipped it:** revoking a lost phone would delete the owner's *live* lane credential, every lane terminal of that person would fail closed on its next launch, and §7 open question 5's stated answer — “yes, until the desktop disconnects” — would be false. Deprovisioning a lane deliberately (the same consent UI as §2a's provisioning) removes it whatever the grant count; §7 question 5 asks whether the revoke dialog should offer that as a checkbox.

**The gate becomes per-lane.** `beginClaudeAuthSwitch`/`isClaudeAuthSwitchInProgress` are module-global booleans (`live-pty-gate.ts:178-191`) and `liveClaudePtyIds` is one global set (`:1`) **[code]**. S9c keys both by lane id (`'host'` for the shared lane, mirroring B2's reserved attachment key at `terminal-presence-registry.ts:10`) **[code]**, so one developer's switch blocks only their own spawns; the four spawn-side reads (`ipc/pty.ts:4527`, `:4639`, `:6174`, `:6223`) **[code]** become lane-scoped with them — and, per §a, their condition widens from `isClaudeLaunch` to `isClaudeLaunch || lanePinned`, so a push blocks the plain-shell panes that now carry lane credentials too. **And `accounts.lane.push` must take that gate.** `beginClaudeAuthSwitch` has exactly one caller today — `syncRuntimeAuthWithLivePtyGate` (`claude-accounts/service.ts:572-582`, call at `:576`) **[code]** — the *host* switch path; a lane switch is a push and never passes through it, so without this a push races spawns in its own lane. The push handler wraps its lane write in `begin`/`endClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)`. Control: a spawn in lane A during a push to lane A is refused — including a plain-shell lane pane; a spawn in lane B during the same push is not. Lane is the right key for the *spawn gate*; it is **not** the key for refresh — the refresh path asks "is a live PTY running in the lane where this account is resident", resolved through §e's residency index, single-valued by L1.

### (g) Handoff — narrowly defined, mostly scoped out

**Q4 = RESOLVED, and the answer splits the question in two — the half the owner asked for is already native.** The owner: *"if we're switching accounts for ourselves we want to continue the conversation, just with a different claude account."* **That needs no feature and S9 must not build one.** A self-switch is a push into the *same* lane (§2c): same `principalId`, therefore the same lane directory, therefore the same `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, therefore — because `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` relocates credentials, `settings.json`, `.claude.json`, transcripts and memory **as a unit** **[doc]** — the same `.claude.json`, the same session transcripts and the same `--resume`/`/resume` surface. The push replaces exactly two things: the bytes of `.credentials.json`, and the `oauthAccount` key inside the lane's `.claude.json` (§2b, §2c). Nothing forks, nothing is copied, and no transcript crosses a lane boundary. **The running session re-resolves the new account on its next credential read** — the one assumption §4 pins and §5 probes — so the conversation continues in place, in the same terminal, with a different Claude account. Under Q1 this now holds across the person's own devices: a switch *requested* from the phone (§2l) is still a push into the same lane, so the desktop's running session continues on the new account without the phone ever holding a credential. If the §5 probe fails, this degrades exactly as R2 degrades and no further: `laneState: 'restart-required'`, new terminals in that lane get the new account, and the AccountsPane hedge (`AccountsPane.tsx:808`) **[code]** becomes accurate.

**The half that stays out is the cross-*person* one, and §2g's scope-out stands unchanged.** Transcript-seeded "continue *your* session in *my* account" is not built, for exactly the reason rev 10 gave and Q1 does not touch: the source JSONL lives inside the **other person's** lane, and copying it into a different principal's credential directory defeats the wipe promise and makes a data-mixing decision the owner has not made. Under principals the boundary is *between people*, which is the boundary the owner drew. The containment refusal below is what enforces it, and it is unchanged.

**Shipped (S9d):** `terminal.openInMyLane` — grant-scoped, creates a **new** terminal in the same workspace (git worktree *or* folder workspace, `AGENTS.md:43-45`) **[code]**, under the caller's lane, optionally seeded with a plain-text prompt. It moves no process and copies no files. A caller with **no** lane is **refused**, never silently served from the shared lane; the seed prompt is bounded (≤4096 bytes, control characters other than newline rejected) and delivered as terminal input, never interpolated into a shell command line.

**The authorization check is a real join.** `registerConnection` (`terminal-presence-registry.ts:111-124`) stores into `participants`, a map keyed by **connectionId** (`:49`, set at `:121`), and it is called for *any* authenticated socket (`runtime-rpc.ts:1376-1387`, comment `:1377-1381`) **[code]** — so "is the caller a registered participant" is true for every connected grant on every terminal and authorizes nothing. Per-terminal membership lives in `attachments`, ptyId → subscriptionKey → attachment (`:51`, `attachmentsFor` `:226-233`, `attach` `:235-241`, public reader `attachmentsOf` `:272`) **[code]**. **The check S9d ships — and this is its normative statement, re-keyed here because rev 12 left it one hop short of §2a's re-key list:** `attachmentsOf(ptyId)` (`:272`) → each attachment's `connectionId` (`:23-27`) → `participants.get(connectionId)` (`:50`, written by `registerConnection` at `:122`) → `.pairedDeviceId` → **`principalOf(…)`**, which must equal `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)`. **Not `.pairedDeviceId === ctx.pairedDeviceId`, which is what rev 12 wrote here and what §5 tested.** Both forms are implementable against the registry exactly as cited — the shape was re-opened and is unchanged **[code]** — so this is a choice, not a constraint, and the grant-keyed form gets Q1 wrong in the one place Q1 is most visible: a person attached from their **phone** would be refused `openInMyLane` on a pane their **desktop** is attached to, while the same person from the desktop is allowed. One person, two answers to "may I open this in my lane" — the precise class Q1 exists to remove (§2a: the adopt gate, the ownership predicate and the owner label all compare principals; this join is the last one that did not). The registry itself stays principal-blind: the new accessor is still `grantsAttachedTo(ptyId)` — it exposes the `pairedDeviceId`s the registry actually holds, because `participants` is private — and `principalOf` is applied in the handler, so `terminal-presence-registry.ts` gains no dependency on the principal registry.

**Scoped out — not built, and not the same as not reachable.** Transcript-seeded "continue this session in my account" is not built: session transcripts relocate with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` **[doc]**, so the source JSONL lives inside the *other* developer's lane, and `--resume` in the caller's lane would copy one person's transcript into another person's credential directory — defeating the wipe guarantee and making a data-mixing decision the owner has not made. **The other half, plainly:** `resumeProviderSession.transcriptPath` (`terminal.ts:1027`) and `launchConfig.ompResumeFilePath` (`workspace-session-sleeping-agents.ts:71-77`, reachable on `terminal.create`, `session.tabs.createTerminal` and `worktree.create`) are client-supplied paths on the very RPCs that derive the lane **[code]**. §a's `computeLaneLaunch` containment-checks both against **only** the caller's own lane root and the workspace — which also refuses a path under the shared `~/.claude`, so a pre-S9 session cannot be resumed from inside a lane (§a; the refusal names the shared-lane pane as the place to do it). Residual is the §4 one: a paired grant can read those bytes with a shell anyway.

### (h) Presence integration

Two additive fields on terminal/presence rows: `credentialLane: 'grant' | 'host' | 'remote' | 'wsl' | 'shared-runtime' | 'unknown'` — a union of **display strings**, and a different thing from §2a's host-internal `credentialLane` *create option*, which rev 12 re-keys to `{ kind: 'principal', principalId }`. The published string `'grant'` deliberately keeps its rev-10 name and now reads "pinned to a principal's lane": it is a value old clients render, so renaming it would be a Rule-3 meaning change bought for nothing (`'shared-runtime'` is the OpenClaude downgrade of §a consequence 3 — a lane pane observed running a runtime the lane cannot isolate) and `laneState: 'loaded' | 'absent' | 'reauth-required'` (plus `'restart-required'` if the §4 live probe fails), **and — rev 16 — an additive optional `laneWipePending: true` beside it**, set while a wipe is marked, aborting or retrying its sweep and cleared only on §2f's clean post-sweep read-back. It exists because `'absent'` is what *launches* key on and is therefore the right fail-closed value in that window, while the same string would otherwise assert a residency claim the host cannot yet make; a client with no string for the flag simply renders `absent` (Rule 1). **These are properties of the terminal, not of a participant** — they come from the pane's `lanePrincipalId` (§a), so a shared-lane terminal never renders as owned by a person and a person's desktop pane and phone pane render one owner, not two and a wiped lane says `absent` rather than looking healthy. Because §a's decoupling pins the lane env to *every* lane pane, `credentialLane: 'grant'` now means what the row claims it means for plain shells too.

**The owner label is the authority label — but it is a join, not a shared key, and rev 11 makes it a three-hop join.** `participants` is keyed by connectionId with `pairedDeviceId` as a *field* (`terminal-presence-registry.ts:49`, `:14-16`), `attachments` by subscriptionKey (`:50`) **[code]**; the lane is keyed by `principalId`, which neither map carries. The row projection resolves the terminal's bound lane to its **principal**, resolves each participant's `pairedDeviceId` to a principal, and marks the participants whose principal matches as the credential owner — plural, deliberately: one person connected from a desktop and a phone is **one** owner label over two participants, which is what Q1 means on screen. The label itself is the principal's name, seeded from the `--pair-name` string the registry already carries (`runtime-rpc.ts:1385` reads `deviceRegistry.getDevice(...)?.name ?? ''`) **[code]** and editable at the consent step; it is host-observed and not spoofable. Written once, in the projection, it cannot drift. The account **email** on that row, if Q3 says to show one, is client-asserted (§b); the owner label is not.

**The pane's lane must survive a restart — after rev 8 it *is* the binding, not an attribute of it.** `lanePrincipalId` (rev 10's `laneGrantId`, re-keyed) — carrying the **principal id**, resolved once at bind time, immutable for the pane's life, so that neither a revoke (which drops the `DeviceEntry` at `runtime-rpc.ts:648`/`:660` **[code]**) nor a re-bind can orphan or re-point a live pane — lives on the in-memory `RuntimePtyWorktreeRecord` (`orca-runtime.ts:1399-1427`, beside `connectionId` at `:1403`) **[code]** and is persisted on the pane's binding row, which both spawn paths already sync-flush before `pty:spawn` returns — `persistPtyBinding` (`src/main/persistence.ts:6993-7027`, paneKey composed at `:7008`, beside `terminalPtyIncarnationsByPaneKey`) — which has **four** writers, not the two rev 8 named: `ipc/pty.ts:5299`/`:5303` (path A), `:6875`/`:6877` (path B), and two *reattach* writers, `ipc/pty.ts:820` (inside the stable-pane persistence fence, `:814-824`) and `ssh/ssh-relay-session.ts:2558` **[code]**. The reattach writers rewrite an existing row from an owner record, so each must **preserve** the row's lane field rather than re-emit the row without it — a dropped lane on reattach is a silent downgrade to `shared`, and §5 asserts it on both; §3 row 6 additionally carries the lane alongside each `claudeLivePtySessionIds` entry (`persistence.ts:7228-7282`) **[code]** so the live-PTY seed can attribute accounts, and the sleeping-agent record carries it too (§a). Rehydrated onto the record at restore **[inferred — the field is new; the two store shapes it joins are those]**, and the reattach writers above are the ones that can silently drop it. Without it, cold restore, the wake, `terminal.split` and relay-lease recovery (`orca-runtime.ts:17328`) **[code]** all read no lane. A pane restored from an *older* state carries none: it renders `credentialLane: 'unknown'` (never attributed to a person), and a split, a recovery **and a resume** of it fail closed.

### (i) macOS and Linux — and where Windows and WSL now live

**macOS — a hard refusal, not a degradation.** Per-lane isolation is nearly free: the Keychain service is already suffixed by `sha256(CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR)` (`keychain.ts:85-93`) **[code]**. What breaks it is the legacy fallback: `getActiveClaudeServices` returns `[scoped, unsuffixed]` and readers/writers walk both (`:95-100`) **[code]**, and `runtime-auth-service.ts:480`'s comment explains why — "Claude Code 2.1+ reads the scoped service, older builds the legacy unsuffixed one" **[code]**. With a pre-2.1 CLI every lane collapses onto the single unsuffixed item: all grants share one credential and **R1/R3 fail silently**. **Decision:** on macOS the lane path never touches the unsuffixed service, and lane provisioning **refuses** below the pinned CLI version rather than degrading. The shared lane keeps the fallback. Wipe must also delete the lane's scoped Keychain item. **The fail-closed claim for a *wiped* lane needs its own probe:** `getActiveClaudeServices` returning `[scoped, unsuffixed]` (`keychain.ts:95-100`) **[code]** is *Orca's reader* behavior, not documented CLI behavior. If the CLI does fall back, a wiped lane silently runs on the shared credential. §5 adds a macOS probe on the pinned version; until it passes, macOS lane provisioning stays off — **a hard gate restated in §6, not a note**. And because a live probe on a real Mac cannot protect the reader from regressing on CI, S9a also ships an **in-repo control with a mutation proof**: a unit test asserts that the lane read/write path resolves the *scoped* service only, and making any lane path reach the unsuffixed service (e.g. restoring the `[scoped, unsuffixed]` walk of `keychain.ts:95-100` **[code]** on the lane branch) must turn a named test red. This failure class — every lane silently collapsing onto one credential — is the worst in the design, so it must fail loudly in CI, not only in a manual step.

**Linux.** Unchanged, and still the reference platform for the file-mode reasoning: `.credentials.json` is a plain file at `0600` **[doc]**, `ensureOwnerOnlyMode` chmods it (`lane-credential-writer.ts:208-217`) **[code]**, and every §4 residual in this document was written against a Linux co-tenant. It is no longer the *shared host*, but the owner's VPS is Linux and single-owner, so nothing here is demoted.

**Windows Server — promoted out of this subsection.** Rev 10 answered Windows in one paragraph. It is now the shared host, so it gets its own first-class section: **§2m**. The one claim rev 10 made here that survives verbatim, because §2a already depends on it, is the second-order effect: a `DeviceRegistry.load` that throws anywhere inside its `try` returns **zero** devices (`device-registry.ts:350-385`, `hardenExistingSecureFile` at `:358`, `JSON.parse` at `:359`, catch at `:382-385`) **[code]**, which is why orphan reconciliation is gated on a successful load. **Rev 11 gave that sentence a *Windows* justification and it was wrong; it is withdrawn here rather than quietly patched.** `hardenExistingSecureFile` (`src/shared/secure-file.ts:167-175`) hardens the directory (`:170`) and the file (`:173`) through `hardenSecurePathOnce`, and on `win32` **both** arms end at `applySecurePathRestriction(…, sync=false)` — the file arm at `:84`, and the directory arm at `:64` inside `hardenSecureDirectoryOnce` (`:59-67`, cached at `:66` whether or not the ACL landed), which `:70-73` is merely the win32 short-circuit into (rev 12 cited `:70-73` as the call site; corrected here on re-opening); the dispatch is at `:201-209`, which fires `bestEffortRestrictWindowsPath` and returns `true` optimistically with an empty `execFile` error callback (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:24-41`, callback `:37-39`) **[code]** — asynchronous, best-effort, and structurally unable to throw. That is the *same* property §2m(1) leans on to argue the opposite about the ordinary directory-hardening path, so the two paragraphs contradicted each other. The two synchronous PowerShell ACL spawns the tree does name (`device-registry.ts:62-63`, `:286-288`) **[code]** belong to the **save** path — `writeSecureJsonFile` → `writeSecureFile` → `applySecurePathRestriction(…, sync=true)` (`secure-file.ts:120-126`) — not to load's harden. **The `loadSucceeded` gate is unchanged and still load-bearing**; what changes is the evidence under it: the real zero-device modes are a JSON parse error or a truncated write at `:359`, on every platform, plus a POSIX `chmodSync` failure at `secure-file.ts:210`. Windows is not the platform that makes it real; corruption is.

**WSL.** For S9a–S9d, unchanged and unchanged deliberately: lanes do not apply (§a), a WSL-target launch keeps today's per-account WSL config dir labelled `credentialLane: 'wsl'`, and §a's WSLENV export invariant is asserted so no host lane path ever crosses into a distro. On the Windows host a principal's WSL panes are therefore **not** isolated from another principal's WSL panes — the gap rev 10 named. Rev 11 stops deferring it indefinitely and designs it as an independently shippable slice: **§2n, S9e**. Whether S9e ships *with* S9a or after it was rev 11's open question for the owner; **rev 15 records the answer — native shells, so S9e ships neither: it is deferred** (§2m(8), §7 question 1), and a WSL lane pane is refused rather than un-isolated until it is revived.

### (j) Launch scrub — unchanged, plus new negative controls

The strip and the refusal stay as they are: the `authEnvToDelete`/`envToDelete` construction gated on `claudeAuth?.stripAuthEnv` (`ipc/pty.ts:4742-4744`, `:6467-6469`) over `CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS` (`environment.ts:9-14`), with the refusal at `:4594-4598`/`:6211-6215` **[code]** — the lane branch's `stripAuthEnv: true` is what arms them, now on every lane pane (§a). (`applyClaudeEnvPatch`'s strip path, `environment.ts:29-57`, is never invoked from `pty.ts` **[code]**; it serves Orca's two own probes.) These are joined by the `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and `agentArgs` guards from §a.

Because `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`/`ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`/`apiKeyHelper` **outrank** stored logins **[doc]**, a lane is only as strong as the settings files the CLI reads *and the arguments that choose them*. Rev 6 answered "three surfaces" one at a time and missed a fourth (host-wide `agentDefaultArgs`); rev 7 answers them from §2's enumeration instead, and only one row needs an answer §2 cannot give structurally: **row 22**, a project-level `.claude/settings.json`, resolved from the cwd rather than the config dir **[doc]** — and that cwd is a folder workspace as often as a git worktree (`AGENTS.md:44-46`) **[code]**. The host does not own that file, so the answer is a **refusal**: S9a refuses a lane launch when resolved project settings define `apiKeyHelper` or auth `env` keys. Rows 5–14 (client `env`/`envToDelete`/`agentEnv`/`agentArgs`/`agentCommand`, and host-wide `agentDefaultArgs`/`agentDefaultEnv`) are computed away by `computeLaneLaunch`; row 20 (the host's own `settings.json`) is ignored except the §a mirror. Two neighbours are **named rather than refused**: enterprise **managed policy** (`/etc/claude-code/managed-settings.json`) outranks every lane `settings.json` and is not relocated **[doc]** — root-only, so §4 class and invisible to this refusal; and the **base-URL class** env keys (row 5), which redirect only the lane owner's own pane. Controls: a lane launch in a workspace whose `.claude/settings.json` defines `apiKeyHelper` must throw (once for a worktree, once for a folder workspace); a lane whose own `settings.json` is mutated on disk to add `apiKeyHelper` must be rewritten or refused at next launch; a lane launch whose `agentArgs` contains a settings-redirecting flag must be refused, once per spawning RPC; and a lane launch made while a peer's `settings.update` has set `agentDefaultArgs`/`agentDefaultEnv` must carry neither; and a lane launch made while a peer has set `agentStatusHooksEnabled: false` still carries the hook coordinates (row 16).

### (k) Usage and rate-limit attribution

Relocating `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` breaks Claude usage telemetry. The managed statusline posts `configDir=$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`claude/statusline-script.ts:160`; Windows branch `:57-58`) **[code]** — and §a requires every lane's `settings.json` to carry that hook block. `rate-limits/claude-usage-attribution.ts:68-71` snapshots **one** host-wide `configDir` from `authPreparation?.envPatch.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and drops any event whose `configDir` does not match (`:1476-1482`) **[code]**. Rev 8 adds the dependency row 16 now carries: the post exists only if the hook env was injected, and that injection is gated at spawn by `isAgentStatusHooksEnabled(ptySettings)` at three sites — `pty.ts:4730`, `:6455` (daemon) and `:2508` (non-daemon, via `local-pty-provider.ts:713`) → `agentHookServer.buildPtyEnv()` `:1770` **[code]** — a host-wide, ungated, client-writable flag, which is why the lane read is lane-scoped and always on. Without a change, every lane terminal's usage post is silently discarded while §d promises the projection carries "the caller's own lane row".

**S9b makes the snapshot a map.** `lastClaudeAuthSnapshot` becomes `Map<normalizedConfigDir, { provenance, laneId, identity }>`, populated by the shared-lane fetch as today plus one entry per loaded lane from §c's `syncLane`; `ingestLiveClaudeRateLimits` (`:1467-1484`) looks the event's `configDir` up in that map and attributes to the matching lane, still dropping unknown dirs. Two consequences: (1) the posted `configDir` embeds another **principal's** host-minted `principalId` after the re-key (rev 13 still said "another grant's `pairedDeviceId`" here; the decision is unchanged, the premise was stale — §2a), and although it travels only a loopback POST to Orca's own agent-hook server (`statusline-script.ts:155-163`) **[code]**, it must be replaced by the opaque lane label before it reaches any presence or status row a peer reads — and per §a the same is true of `provenance`, which surfaces as the published `usageMetadata.authProvenance` (`claude-usage-attribution.ts:118`, `shared/rate-limit-types.ts:41`) **[code]**. (2) The *pull* path is not fixed by this, and **Q7 moves it into S9b** — the owner wants "usage bars per-person, per-account and depending on the account attached to the terminal/agent", which is two asks: a per-lane pull, and a per-row join. Three code facts set the design.

**Fact 1 — the pull is already parameterised by a config dir.** `resolveOAuthCredentialReadOptions(authPreparation)` returns `{ credentialsFileConfigDir: authPreparation.configDir, keychainConfigDir: authPreparation.configDir }` (`rate-limits/claude-fetcher.ts:236-248`, the two fields at `:244-245` — `:243` opens the object literal and `:246` closes it; rev 15 cited `:243-246`, corrected here), consumed at `:705` and `:779` **[code]**. **Correction to rev 10, which named this function `buildClaudeUsageReadOptions`:** no such symbol exists in this tree; the name was wrong, the behaviour rev 10 described was right, and the line range `:239-247` was off by a line at each end. Because the fetcher already takes a preparation, giving the pull a lane is a question of *which preparation it is handed*, not of rewriting it.

**Fact 2 — the per-account preparation already exists, and it returns `null` on Windows.** `getManagedUsagePanelAuthPreparation(account, location)` (`rate-limits/claude-fetcher.ts:1058-1088`) builds exactly the shape a lane needs — `configDir`, `envPatch: { CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR }`, `stripAuthEnv: true`, `provenance: 'managed:<id>:inactive-preview'` — in a WSL arm (`:1069-1077`) and a host arm (`:1079-1087`) **[code]**. **But its first statement is `if (process.platform === 'win32') { return null }` (`:1062-1064`) **[code]**.** On the shared **Windows Server** host, the very precedent this slice is modelled on is switched off, and the code carries no comment saying why. **Stated rather than assumed away:** the per-lane pull is a **Linux/macOS capability in this tree**, and shipping it on the Windows host requires first establishing what `:1062-1064` is protecting against — a §5 **Windows-only** probe and a named gate in §6. Until that probe passes, a lane on the Windows host has **statusline-derived usage only**, which is exactly what rev 10 shipped, so the failure mode is "usage bars cover live sessions only", not "no bars".

**Fact 3 — row 32 needs a lane, not a new spawn shape.** `rate-limits/claude-pty.ts` assembles its env with `applyClaudeEnvPatch({ ...process.env, TERM }, options?.authPreparation?.envPatch ?? {}, { stripAuthEnv })` (`:245-249`) **[code]**, overlays the configured proxy (`:254-255`), then branches: a WSL target writes `export CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR=${shellQuote(wslConfig.linuxConfigDir)}` into its `bash -lc` line (`:280`) and a host target rides the env patch alone; the spawn is `pty.spawn(spawnFile, spawnArgs, { …, env: spawnEnv })` (`:289-297`), with `spawnFile` resolved to `wsl.exe` / `cmd.exe` / `claude` at `:266` **[code]**. So the lane arm of row 32 is **one more `authPreparation` handed in** — the same object the fetcher already threads — and nothing about the spawn changes, Windows `cmd.exe /c` arm (`:285-287`) **[code]** included. **Two things do have to change about the env it hands in, and both are `win32`-only.** The strip is exact-case, so an inherited lowercase `anthropic_api_key` survives it (§2m(5)); and the **write** is exact-key too — `baseEnv.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR = patch.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`claude-accounts/environment.ts:44-46`) **[code]** — over a plain spread of `process.env`, so an Orca process carrying `Claude_Config_Dir` (row 19, `runtime-paths.ts:14-25`) leaves **two** keys in the object and Win32 resolves the inherited one. **This path is the exception §2m(5)'s collapse decision had no site for**, because that collapse is sited post-merge inside the provider and the probe never reaches a provider. §2m(5) now sites the third collapse here, over the object `applyClaudeEnvPatch` returns and before `pty.spawn` (`:289-297`); the three consequences of getting it wrong are §2k's own three mechanisms — the paneKey join, the synthetic live-PTY id and the post-probe `syncLane` — each writing the **lane's** row while the probe holds the **shared** lane's token.

**The critical interaction, and the reason this is a design decision rather than plumbing: a usage pull that runs `claude` under a lane's config dir *is* a live Claude process holding that lane's single-use refresh token.** **Four** existing mechanisms must all be joined, and none of them sees this PTY today. (Rev 13 enumerated three and missed §2f's close-wipe, which is round 3's second blocker; the fourth bullet is below, and its normative home is §2f.)
- **Refresh deferral (§2e).** `markClaudePtySpawned` is called only from `pty.ts:5364` and `:6999` **[code]**, and row 32 never touches `PtyProvider`, so the usage PTY is invisible to the live-PTY gate. That is harmless today because the shared-lane probe is preceded on the same tick by `doSyncForCurrentSelection` (`prepareForRateLimitFetch`, `runtime-auth-service.ts:165-177`) **[code]**, which serializes host rotation against it by construction. Under a **per-lane** pull that serialization is gone: `syncLane` trigger (2) fires per lane, and a lane rotation can land while the previous tick's probe still holds the token — a double rotation, `invalid_grant` across that lane. **Decision: the lane usage probe registers into that lane's live-PTY set for its lifetime** — and rev 12 named the wrong carrier for it, so the mechanism is stated here instead of implied. That set is `liveClaudePtyIds`, a `Set<string>` of pty **ids** (`live-pty-gate.ts:1`), written only by `markClaudePtySpawned(ptyId)` (`:87-91`) and cleared by `markClaudePtyExited(ptyId)` (`:129-136`) **[code]**; the hidden probe never reaches `provider.spawn` (row 32) and therefore has **no id in that namespace at all**. So the probe **mints a synthetic, lane-scoped gate id** and registers and releases it through that same pair, wired into the probe's own termination path (below) so no exit route can leak a permanent deferral. **One hazard on that path, named because it is one line away:** `markClaudePtySpawned` persists every id it is handed — `persistence?.addClaudeLivePtySessionId(ptyId)` (`:101`) **[code]** — so a synthetic id would land in `claudeLivePtySessionIds` (§3 row 6) and be seeded back at the next startup, deferring that account's rotation until `confirmSeededClaudeLivePtys` drops it as unknown to the daemon (`:82-94`) **[code]**. It self-heals, but only after the startup pass §2e already had to make observe-only. **The probe therefore registers through a non-persisting arm** — the gate gains an in-memory-only companion set that `hasLiveClaudePtys` unions (`:138-176`) — and §5 asserts a synthetic id never reaches `addClaudeLivePtySessionId`. A probe in lane L then **defers** rotation of L's account exactly as a user's `claude` does. Deliberately conservative, and the same trade §2a consequence 2 already made: over-defer rather than risk a double rotation.
- **The per-lane switch gate (§2f).** A probe is a read, but it reads the file a push is about to replace. **Decision: the probe takes the lane gate the way a spawn does** — `isClaudeAuthSwitchInProgress(laneId)` (`live-pty-gate.ts:178-191`) **[code]** — and a probe that finds the gate closed is **skipped, not queued**. Usage is sampled on a tick and a missed tick costs nothing; a queued probe would run against a credential the human has already switched away from and post usage attributed to the wrong account, which is the misattribution class this whole design exists to remove. **The reverse ordering produces the same harm and needs its own rule, because the gate is a *spawn* gate and not a barrier.** §2f makes `begin`/`endClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)` lane-scoped at the four **spawn-side** reads (`ipc/pty.ts:4527`, `:4639`, `:6174`, `:6223`) **[code]**; it stops a new spawn and does nothing to a PTY already running. A push arriving *after* the probe spawned therefore replaces `.credentials.json` underneath it — and on Windows it **succeeds** in doing so, inside `renameFileWithWindowsRetry`'s ~750 ms window (`codex-accounts/fs-utils.ts:200-202` → `:208-223`, six attempts at `:209` over `EPERM`/`EACCES`/`EBUSY` at `:216`) **[code]**, by §2m(2)'s own design. Two harms, not one: the probe posts usage for the **pre-push** account under a row that now names the post-push one, and it still holds the **pre-push** single-use refresh token, so any rotation it performs revokes a credential the lane no longer holds. **Decision: the push does not wait for an in-flight probe; it invalidates it.** `beginClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)` marks every probe in flight in that lane **stale**; a stale probe's result is **dropped, not attributed** — no usage row, no `cli-observed` receipt from it, no watermark move — and its PTY is **killed** rather than left to finish. **The carrier rev 12 named does not do that, and the difference is the second of the two harms above, so it is corrected rather than adjusted:** `registerHiddenRateLimitPty(term)` (`rate-limits/hidden-pty-cleanup.ts:12-19`) does `activeHiddenRateLimitPtys.add(term)` and returns a disposable whose `dispose()` is a bare `.delete(term)` (`:15-18`) **[code]** — it is bookkeeping, and it terminates nothing. Termination is `cleanupHiddenRateLimitPty(term, disposables, { kill: true })` (`:25-58`, the disposable drain at `:30-32`, `term.kill()` at `:36`) **[code]**, which the probe already calls on each of its own exit paths (`claude-pty.ts:345`, `:364`, `:419`, and with `kill: false` at `:514`) **[code]**. **Decision: the staleness signal invokes that same call** — `cleanupHiddenRateLimitPty(term, termDisposables, { kill: true })` — which kills the `claude` still holding the **pre-push** single-use refresh token *and*, by draining the registered disposables at `:33-35`, releases the lane gate id the bullet above minted. **And the handle that reaches it from outside already exists, which rev 13 left unnamed and therefore implementable only as new machinery:** `term` and `termDisposables` are locals of `fetchViaPty`, but `fetchViaPty` already takes an `options.signal: AbortSignal` (`claude-pty.ts:221`) and wires it at `:349-358` to `settleAborted` (`:335-347`), whose body performs exactly this call at `:345` **[code]**. **The staleness signal is that abort**: the lane pull holds each in-flight probe's `AbortController` and aborts it, so the decision is implementable as stated rather than as a new handle, and §2f's close-wipe uses the same one. Disposing the registration alone would have left that CLI running on a credential the lane no longer holds, which is precisely the harm this decision enumerates. Dropping rather than waiting is the same trade as the skip above, in the other direction: a missed usage tick costs nothing, and a push that blocks on a hidden process is a switch the human watches hang. **Mutation proof in §5:** attributing a stale probe's post, or letting it move the watermark, turns a named test red.
- **The watermark (§2c).** A probe never writes `.credentials.json`, but the CLI it spawns may **rotate** it — that is precisely what the `cli-observed` cause exists for. **Decision: the lane pull's tick runs `syncLane` over every lane it probed, *after* that probe exits — and that run is explicitly **not** subject to trigger (2)'s live-PTY precondition**, so a rotation the probe's own CLI performed is observed and the watermark moves. **Ordering alone would not have been enough, and stating it as ordering alone was rev 11's bug:** trigger (2) is defined as "a lane with live PTYs" (§2c), and the decision one bullet above registers the probe into the lane's live-PTY set *for its lifetime* — so on an **idle** lane the probe is the only member, and at the instant the sync is evaluated (after the probe exited and its `cleanupHiddenRateLimitPty` released the synthetic gate id, `claude-pty.ts:345`/`:364`/`:419` → `hidden-pty-cleanup.ts:25-58` **[code]**) the lane has no live PTYs and trigger (2) skips itself by its own precondition. The `cli-observed` rotation would never be recorded, the watermark would stay at the pre-probe sha, and the desktop's next push would be refused `accounts.lane.push_stale` — the rev-5 watermark bug reproduced in a new place, with a §5 mutation proof that passes on ordering while the real path fails. **So §2c's trigger (2) carries a second arm — "or a lane probed on this tick" — and §5 asserts *that arm*, not the ordering:** the named test drives an **idle** lane whose only PTY was the probe, and a rotation performed by the probe's own CLI must move the watermark. **Mutation proof:** restoring the live-PTY precondition on the post-probe run turns it red.
- **The close-wipe (§2f) — the fourth, and the one rev 13 did not enumerate.** A probe is not merely a reader of the lane: its `claude` can rotate and **write** `.credentials.json` back into the lane's config dir, which is the whole reason `cli-observed` exists.
