So a wipe that sweeps the lane directory while a probe is in flight can be undone by that probe's own write *after* the sweep completes — a lane reported wiped, holding a live credential at rest, with `laneState: 'loaded'` and §2a consequence 2's fail-closed promise false. **Decision: the wipe kills first and sweeps second, through the same staleness path the push uses** — mark stale, `cleanupHiddenRateLimitPty(term, termDisposables, { kill: true })` (`hidden-pty-cleanup.ts:25-58`), reached through `fetchViaPty`'s `options.signal` (`claude-pty.ts:221`, wired `:349-358` → `settleAborted` `:335-347`, the call at `:345`) **[code]**, result dropped, and only then the sweep. **And killing what is in flight is only half of it, which rev 14 left implied: the next tick must not start one.** The pull therefore refuses to start a probe for a lane whose **wipe is pending or in progress** (the lifecycle sets that flag before it aborts, §2f) and for a lane whose `laneState` is **`absent`** — the second the pull already did, the first new in rev 15 — and §2f's post-sweep re-read is the backstop for a flag read a moment too early. §5 asserts the start-side refusal **separately from the kill**, because a suite that tests only the kill passes while the very next tick re-loads the lane it just watched being wiped. Stated normatively at §2f; stated here because this bullet list is what an implementer reads when wiring the probe's lifecycle, and the omission is what made the gap invisible.

**"The usage of the account attached to this terminal/agent" — the join, and why it does not go through `configDir`.** The obvious key is rev 10's: make `lastClaudeAuthSnapshot` a map keyed by normalized config dir. That is right for *attribution* and is kept. It is the wrong key for the **row**, for two reasons this tree makes concrete. First, `normalizeClaudeConfigDir` trims and folds separators but deliberately does **not** fold case — "preserve Linux case sensitivity" (`rate-limits/claude-usage-attribution.ts:29-33`) **[code]** — and the test that drops a mismatch is a bare `!==` (`:1476-1482`) **[code]**; on the **Windows** host the CLI's `%CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR%` and Orca's stored lane path can legitimately differ in drive-letter case or by an 8.3 component, and every post from that lane is then silently dropped (§2m(4)). Second, the config dir is the identifier §2a spent an entire decision making opaque before it reaches a peer-readable row.

**A better key is already on the wire and is being thrown away.** The managed statusline **already posts the paneKey**: `--data-urlencode "paneKey=${ORCA_PANE_KEY}"` (`claude/statusline-script.ts:159`; the Windows `cmd` twin at `:65`) **[code]**. But the parser reads only two fields out of the form body — `const fields = body as { payload?: unknown; configDir?: unknown }` (`shared/claude-statusline-rate-limits.ts:76`) — and returns `{ configDir, fiveHour, sevenDay }` (`:87`, the function spanning `:72-105`), which is the whole of `ClaudeStatusLineRateLimits` (`:22-34`); the route hands that object straight on (`agent-hooks/server.ts:2138-2141`) **[code]**. The paneKey is posted, parsed away, and never seen. **Decision: `ClaudeStatusLineRateLimits` gains `paneKey`** — a local type, additive, no wire change, no statusline-script change — and `ingestLiveClaudeRateLimits` resolves **pane → lane** through the binding registry §2a already made authoritative, falling back to the config-dir map only when the paneKey is absent (an older pane, a hand-run statusline). **That new key creates a cross-principal misattribution vector of its own, at the one spawn site that does not go near `computeLaneLaunch`, and it must be closed in the same slice — round 3's third major.** The lane usage probe (row 32, handed a lane `authPreparation` by Fact 3) builds its env as `{ ...process.env, TERM: 'xterm-256color' }` (`rate-limits/claude-pty.ts:247-248`) **[code]** with **no pane-identity scrub**. The PTY path scrubs exactly this and says why: `removeUnspecifiedPaneIdentityEnv(spawnEnv, args.env)` (`providers/local-pty-provider.ts:686`, def `:167-176`) under the comment "Orca can be launched from an Orca terminal; pane identity belongs to the child PTY, not the parent shell", with `ORCA_PANE_KEY` first in `PANE_IDENTITY_ENV_KEYS` (`shared/pane-identity-env.ts:15`, local twin `shared/pane-identity-env.ts:14`) **[code]**; `pty.ts:4775`'s `getInheritedAgentHookEnvKeysToDelete(env)` covers the hook coordinates for the same reason. So when Orca is launched **from an Orca terminal** — the case the comment documents — the probe's `claude` runs the lane's managed statusline with a **stale inherited paneKey** and posts it (`claude/statusline-script.ts:159`, Windows `cmd` twin `:65`) **[code]**. Today that is inert because the parser drops the field (`shared/claude-statusline-rate-limits.ts:76`, `:87`) **[code]**; this decision is what makes it harmful, because the paneKey now **wins** over the (correct) lane configDir, so one principal's probe usage lands on whatever pane that stale key names — plausibly the other principal's lane row. **Decision: the probe mirrors `:701` — pane-identity keys are deleted from its `spawnEnv` before `pty.spawn`** (a one-line scrub at `claude-pty.ts:247-251`, over the same key list), and §5 asserts it, because per-row usage attribution is exactly what Q7 is about. Strictly better than the config-dir key on every axis that matters here: exact, case-free, needs no opaque label, and it is the *same* identifier the lane is bound to, so usage attribution cannot disagree with §2h's owner label.

**The row is then a three-hop join, and every hop already exists:** `terminal.paneKey` → the pane's bound lane (§2a's binding registry, persisted by `persistPtyBinding` at `src/main/persistence.ts:6993-7027`, paneKey composed at `:7008`) **[code]** → that lane's **current** account (§2e's residency index) → that account's usage row. So each terminal/agent row surfaces the usage of the account its lane holds *right now*, and a push re-points it without the row being rebuilt. Two additive fields, **Rule 1**, written in the same projection that already emits `credentialLane`/`laneState` (§2h): **`laneAccountLabel`** — Q3's friendly per-account name, falling back to the principal/owner label when the owner has set none — and **`laneUsage`**, the two windows plus `resetsAt`, the shape `ProviderRateLimits` already publishes (`shared/rate-limit-types.ts:48`) **[code]**. **Not** added: any account id, and the email only where Q3's owner setting permits it. **The two fields have *different* cross-principal visibility, and rev 11 left that undefined:** `laneAccountLabel` **is** peer-visible — Q3 asks precisely about "the identity label the other developer sees", so a row the other developer looks at carries the principal label plus, when the owner has set one, that account's owner-authored display name (§2b's third envelope member); `laneUsage` is **not**. §2d's projection rule applies to this row too, so a peer looking at another principal's terminal sees `laneUsage` **omitted**, never zeroed — zero reads as "no usage left to worry about", omission reads as "not yours", and the client renders nothing.

**Cost, honestly.** The pull becomes `N` hidden `claude` processes per tick, where `N` is the number of **loaded** lanes; each holds its lane's token for the probe's lifetime and defers that lane's rotation while it runs. On a two-developer box `N ≤ 3` and the tick interval is unchanged. If Fact 2's Windows gate does not clear, `N = 0` on the shared host and only the statusline path feeds the bars. And a lane provenance still deliberately fails `isManagedClaudeAuth` (`claude-fetcher.ts:493-496`) **[code]**, which gates CLI *supplementation* of an OAuth result — that stays false for lanes, because the lane pull is the CLI path rather than a supplement to an OAuth path the host cannot run for a lane account.

### (l) The phone, and who pushes — the hard half of Q1

Q1 makes the owner's phone a member of the owner's principal, so the phone's account switcher now names a **lane**. The question rev 10 never had to ask is whether the phone can *push*.

**It cannot, and the tree says so plainly rather than by omission.** The mobile client holds **no Claude account store and no `ClaudeRuntimeAuthService` equivalent**. `grep -rn "claudeAiOauth\|refreshToken\|refreshClaudeOauth" mobile/src mobile/app` returns **zero hits** **[code]**. The only Claude-account code under `mobile/` is a *render* path — `mobile/src/components/accounts-snapshot.ts`, a zod parser for the snapshot the host publishes, plus `account-usage-state.ts` and `AccountUsage.tsx` — and `mobile/scripts/mock-server-account-rpc.ts` / `mock-server-account-state.ts`, a dev harness **[code]**. Its only secret storage is *pairing* material, in `expo-secure-store`: the `deviceToken` (`mobile/src/transport/types.ts:100-102`, the note at `:101`), the relay credential bundle (`mobile/src/transport/mobile-relay-credential-bundle.ts:78`, `:96`, `:103`) and the pairing journal secret (`mobile-relay-pairing-journal-store.ts:44`, `:63`) **[code]** — nothing Claude-shaped anywhere. And the phone's "switch" today is one RPC and nothing else: `client.sendRequest('accounts.selectClaude', { accountId })`, method chosen at `mobile/app/h/[hostId]/accounts.tsx:228` and sent at `:176`, followed by an optimistic `refresh()` at `:183` **[code]**. It asks the *host* to move a credential the host already has. **A phone cannot push a blob it does not hold**, and S9 must not pretend otherwise.

**Two shapes rejected before the third.** **Rejected (i) — give the phone an account store.** It puts the owner's OAuth refresh tokens on a phone, adds a *third* rotator to an account whose refresh token is single-use (R-DR2, §2e, made worse rather than better), and needs a `claude login` flow on a device with no CLI. Wildly out of proportion to "switch which account my agents use". **Rejected (ii) — let the phone push a blob the host already holds**, i.e. "select lane-resident account X". There is nothing to select among: L1 says a lane holds exactly one account at a time, so this is a no-op dressed as a feature.

**Decision — delegated selection: the phone chooses, the desktop pushes, the host arbitrates.** The desktop stays the **sole pusher and sole source of truth** for the principal's account inventory (R5 and §2b unchanged; the host still never receives the account list). The phone gets a *selection* RPC over a list the desktop has explicitly delegated:

1. The desktop publishes into its principal's lane state a **delegable account list**: per account the owner ticks, an opaque `delegatedAccountId` (a host-side random token, minted with the lane, **not** the desktop's account id) plus the display fields §2b already permits — the same `displayName` the push envelope carries and, only where the owner allows it, the email. **No credential bytes, no refresh tokens, no shas.** *This list* is readable only by grants of that principal, through §2d's projection; that is a narrower audience than the `displayName` on a terminal row (§2k), which is peer-visible by design, and the two must not be conflated: what a peer may see is the name of the account a lane holds **now**, not the set of accounts its owner could switch to.
2. The phone calls `accounts.lane.requestSwitch({ delegatedAccountId })` — **a real change to the mobile client, budgeted in §6's S9b since rev 20**, because `mobile/app/h/[hostId]/accounts.tsx` sends `accounts.selectClaude` unconditionally today (`:167`/`:176`) **[code]** and would otherwise run into §2d's `accounts.selection_out_of_scope`. **The host does not switch.** It resolves the principal from `ctx.pairedDeviceId`, validates the token against that principal's delegable list, and emits a `switch-requested` frame on the lane status stream §2c already ships — which every grant of that principal receives, the desktop included.
3. The desktop, seeing a request on its own principal, performs an ordinary `accounts.lane.push` of that account: same envelope, same shape validation, same watermark, same freshness rule, same delegation lease. **No new credential path is created anywhere.** The push handler is byte-identical to §2b's.
4. The phone's RPC returns `pending`. It learns the outcome from the lane status stream — `laneState` plus the identity on the rotation receipt — not from a return value, because the host genuinely does not know yet.

**When the desktop is offline the switch is refused by name, and never degrades.** `accounts.lane.desktop_unavailable`, carrying a complete human sentence ("Your desktop is not connected, so this account cannot be switched from here") because an old client has no string for the code (§3's Rule-3 row). It **never** falls back to the shared lane, **never** falls back to `accounts.selectClaude`, and **never** reports success while leaving the lane's credential in place. The host answers without guessing: a request is refused **before** any stream frame is emitted unless an authenticated socket exists **whose grant is this principal's `delegatedGrantId`** — the lease holder, the one grant the human designated to satisfy this request, and, since rev 14, a value every provisioned lane is guaranteed to have, because provisioning requires a designation and refuses `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated` without one (§2a, §2e) — resolved over the same `authenticatedSockets` map the wipe predicate walks (`rpc/mobile-socket-wiring.ts:209-221`) **[code]**, so no request is ever left pending against nobody. **That guarantee is about *liveness*, and rev 19 resolves it against the transport shape this tree actually has rather than against “an authenticated socket” in the abstract — round 7's third finding.** The desktop does not hold one steady socket to this host. Its ordinary request path is **one-shot**: `sendRemoteRuntimeRequestOnSocket` (`shared/remote-runtime-client.ts:114`, reached from the two exports the desktop and the CLI share, `sendRemoteRuntimeRequest` `:86` and `sendRemoteRuntimeRequestWithStatusPreflight` `:96`) opens a WebSocket, authenticates it at `:140`, settles one request and closes it, and `handleClose` then deletes the host's record (`rpc/mobile-socket-wiring.ts:209-212`) **[code]**. Only two connections are **pooled** and long-lived — `RemoteRuntimeRequestConnection` and `RemoteRuntimeSharedControlConnection` (`ipc/runtime-environment-request-connections.ts:39` and `:122`) **[code]** — and the first is created lazily and used only for `terminal.send`/`terminal.updateViewport` (`shouldUseCachedRequestConnection`, `ipc/runtime-environment-transport-routing.ts:271-273`) **[code]**, so an idle desktop is not holding it. Read naively over `authenticatedSockets`, this precondition would refuse a phone's switch `accounts.lane.desktop_unavailable` while the desktop is running, connected and visibly present in the UI. **Decision: the precondition names the socket the delegated desktop's *lane-status subscription* runs on — the pooled shared-control connection in the ordinary case, and the dedicated subscription socket in the two fallback cases below — and the delegated desktop is required to hold that subscription open.** (Rev 19's headline named the pooled connection alone and the rule two sentences later named the subscription; rev 20 makes the headline say what ships, round 8's second minor. §3 row 9 already stated it this way.) That is not a new obligation: step 2 above already requires the desktop to be *receiving* `switch-requested` frames on §2c's lane status stream, so the subscription exists for the mechanism to work at all; rev 19 only makes the socket it runs on the socket the precondition reads. Subscriptions route through `subscribeRemoteRuntimeSharedControlRequest` (`ipc/runtime-environment-request-connections.ts:69`) onto the pooled shared-control connection whenever `shouldUseSharedControlSubscription` names the method (`ipc/runtime-environment-transport-routing.ts:233-245`, the list at `:294-306`, which already carries `accounts.subscribe`) **[code]** — the lane-status subscribe joins that list, one line, budgeted in §6's S9b. Where shared control is unavailable — `supportsSharedControl`'s `status.get` preflight fails (`ipc/runtime-environment-shared-control-support.ts:33`) **[code]**, or the method is one of the two `shouldKeepDedicatedSubscriptionSocket` names (`:290-292`) **[code]** — the call falls back to `subscribeRemoteRuntimeRequest` (`:246`, its own handshake at `shared/remote-runtime-client.ts:512`) **[code]**, which is *also* a socket held open for the subscription's lifetime. **Either way the delegated desktop's lane-status subscription is a durable authenticated socket, and a one-shot request socket never satisfies the precondition.** The host resolves it over the same `authenticatedSockets` map the wipe predicate walks (`rpc/mobile-socket-wiring.ts:209-221`) **[code]**, matching the socket whose grant is this principal's `delegatedGrantId` **and** which is carrying that principal's lane-status subscription. **The second conjunct's carrier is named rather than left implicit, because every other join in this document names one (round 8's third minor).** The lane-status stream is a streaming method, and a streaming handler in this tree already receives the socket identity it needs: `accounts.subscribe` destructures `{ runtime, connectionId }` out of its context (`rpc/methods/accounts.ts:192`) **[code]**, and the host's socket record carries that same `connectionId` (`rpc/mobile-socket-wiring.ts:166`, written into `authenticatedSockets` at `:171` inside `onReady`) **[code]**. So the bookkeeping is the new stream's **own per-connection subscriber state**, keyed by `connectionId` and torn down by the same `handleClose` that deletes the socket record (`:209-212`) **[code]** — the identical `connectionId` join §2h cites for presence, and no new registry. The precondition is therefore two lookups over state that already exists: `delegatedGrantId` → grant, and `connectionId` → this principal's live lane-status subscribers. **The two refusals stay where they were and now partition cleanly.** No lane-status subscription open for the designated grant → `accounts.lane.desktop_unavailable`, refused **before** any frame is emitted, which is the precise scope of “no request is ever left pending against nobody”. Subscription open, no push inside the bounded window → the frame *is* emitted and the request expires `accounts.lane.switch_timed_out`, with the lane untouched. **The second is the mis-designation residual §2a names, and it is not closed here:** the host can prove the designated grant is subscribed; it cannot prove that grant can push. A designated **phone** is exactly that case, and it is the only client kind that is: it subscribes like anything else and answers nothing — the phone already opens an `accounts.subscribe` subscription on this host (`mobile/app/h/[hostId]/accounts.tsx:122`) **[code]**, and step 4 above has it holding the lane status stream for exactly this outcome — recovered by re-designating on the consent surface in one tick, not prevented by any predicate, and §2a says so in the same words. **A designated `orca` CLI grant is *not* that case, and rev 21 stops folding it in with the phone (round 9's first major).** The residual is scoped to grants that can actually *hold* a lane-status subscription, and a CLI grant cannot: `subscribeRemoteRuntimeRequest` has exactly one production caller in this tree — `ipc/runtime-environment-transport-routing.ts:246`, inside the desktop's main process — and the CLI's entire transport is one-shot, `src/cli/runtime/websocket-transport.ts` being 51 lines that export `sendWebSocketRequest` (`:11` → `sendRemoteRuntimeRequest` `:19`) and `sendWebSocketRequestWithStatusPreflight` (`:28` → `:37`) and nothing else, with **no** subscribe entry point and no shared-control use at all (`grep -rn subscribe src/cli` returns zero hits) **[code]**. A designated CLI grant therefore holds no lane-status subscription — normally no socket at all — and neither does a **retired or offline desktop**. **Both land on the *first* branch of the partition, not the second: `accounts.lane.desktop_unavailable`, refused before any frame — never `switch_timed_out`.** The `switch_timed_out` residual is the connected-**and-subscribed** case and only that, which is the scope §2a:121 writes **as of rev 22**, in the same two conjuncts — *connected **and holding a lane-status subscription***. Rev 21 cited §2a for that scope while §2a still said “connected”, full stop; the citation claimed support §2a did not give, and §8 item 1 is closed by correcting §2a rather than by re-wording this sentence alone. Rev 20's wider phrasing here asserted a subscription the CLI client cannot open. **And the precondition keys on `delegatedGrantId` — a value the host wrote when the human ticked it — never on `scope`, never on `clientKind`, and since rev 19 never on a capability string a client asserts about itself.** **Not `scope`, and this is a correction to rev 11 rather than a preference.** There is no desktop scope in this tree: `export type DeviceScope = 'mobile' | 'runtime'` (`src/shared/runtime-types.ts:56`, re-exported at `device-registry.ts:23`, the field at `:29`, defaulted to `'mobile'` by `addDevice` `:81`, `getOrCreatePendingDevice` `:120` and `mintPendingDevice` `:144`) **[code]**, and the only non-mobile value, `'runtime'`, covers **every** non-phone grant including an `orca` CLI grant (`revokeRuntimeAccess` gates on `scope !== 'runtime'`, `runtime-rpc.ts:660`) **[code]**. Keyed on scope, a connected CLI grant would satisfy the precondition — precisely because scope drops the subscription conjunct the shipped rule keys on, and a one-shot CLI socket is all a CLI grant ever has — the `switch-requested` frame would be emitted, no desktop would answer, and the request would sit until `accounts.lane.switch_timed_out` — defeating the guarantee stated one clause earlier. The `delegatedGrantId` is the only *host-written* identifier of a pushing desktop this design has, and rev 19 leaves it the only one: it is written by the human tick, on a surface that lists the principal's own bound grants and nothing else (§2a). The host knows that a grant holds the designation and that it is subscribed; it never knows that it is a pushing desktop, and this document no longer contains a sentence that claims otherwise — which is why §2e makes the value host-published (there, for the rotator; here, for the precondition). A second refusal covers the desktop that is connected but does not act: a request unanswered inside a bounded window expires `accounts.lane.switch_timed_out`, and the lane is untouched.

**Why this is the right shape and not a workaround.** It preserves every invariant the design already paid for. One pusher per lane, so §2c's watermark keeps a single writer; and §2e's lease leaves the **host** as the only rotator of that account, since every bound desktop suppresses under it — the holder included. The phone gains no secret it did not already have. "Who may spend this account" stays answered by the **principal**, not by which device happened to ask. And it is exactly the owner's own framing: the phone is *an extension of the desktop*, not an independent client. **What it costs, stated:** a phone switch requires the desktop to be reachable, so this is the one lane operation the phone cannot complete alone — and on a shared host that is the honest answer, because the alternative is a credential on a phone.

**R-DR2 under one principal, stated exactly.** R-DR2 is *"two Orca **installs** holding the same account rotate the single-use refresh token out from under each other"*. **Desktop + phone of one person is not a second install and adds no rotator**: the phone holds no credential store, runs no `ClaudeRuntimeAuthService`, and never reaches `refreshClaudeOauthCredentials` — established above from the tree, not assumed. So for a principal with one desktop, the rotator set is unchanged from rev 10: the host (account-keyed, §2e) plus that one desktop, suppressed by the lease — **and, since rev 14, that desktop's own runtime `~/.claude` is cleared of the delegated account and refuses a local `selectClaude` of it (§2e rule (iv)), because a plain `claude` reading that file was a third copy neither the lease's rotator rule nor its launch rule reached**. **The shape Q1 *does* create is a second desktop inside one principal**, and §2e answers it in two parts that must be read together: the **host-published** lease suppresses `refreshManagedAccountTokenIfNeeded` on **every** bound desktop, the `delegatedGrantId` included (the closure — a refusal of a desktop's *push* would leave its *rotator* running, and an exemption for the lease-holder would leave the *pushing* desktop rotating, §2e), and `push_not_delegated` refuses any non-holder grant's push so the watermark keeps one writer. A second desktop that is unbound, never connected, or on a build with no lease code reads no lease and is **R-DR2**, below — detection only. R-DR2 across *machines outside this principal* — a laptop, a reinstall, an old build — is unchanged and remains unfixable host-side: detection only, `laneState: 'reauth-required'` with a `foreign-rotation` receipt, and the operating rule stated in the UI.

### (m) Windows Server as the shared host

Rev 10 answered Windows in a paragraph, because the shared box was Linux. It is now the host. This section re-opens every platform-dependent claim in the design against `win32`. **Nothing below demotes the macOS or Linux material**; §2i keeps it.

**(1) The lane directory's DACL — and Orca already has the read-back verification this asks for.** `writeFileAtomically(…, { mode: 0o600 })` is a no-op for Windows ACLs — `writeFileSync`'s `mode` is inert there, and `ensureOwnerOnlyMode` returns early on `win32` before its `chmodSync` (`lane-credential-writer.ts:208-217`, the guard at `:1795-1797`) **[code]**. What Orca does instead is `src/shared/secure-file.ts` → `src/shared/secure-path-windows-acl.ts`, and it is stronger than rev 10 assumed. `restrictWindowsPathSync(targetPath, isDirectory)` (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:43-64`) runs `WINDOWS_RESTRICT_ACL_SCRIPT` (`:66-114`) under PowerShell, which: disables inheritance and drops inherited ACEs, `$acl.SetAccessRuleProtection($true, $false)` (`:77`); removes **every** existing ACE (`:78-80`); grants `FullControl` to exactly three SIDs — the current user, `S-1-5-18` (SYSTEM) and `S-1-5-32-544` (Administrators) (`:71`, `:85-95`); and then **reads the DACL back and verifies it**, throwing if inheritance is still enabled (`:97-100`), if any ACE names a SID outside the allowed set (`:102-106`), if any ACE is a deny (`:107-109`), or if any grant is less than FullControl (`:110-112`) **[code]**. That is precisely rev 10's "verify by reading back the DACL, not the mode bits" — already written, already synchronous, already throwing. **Decision: the lane writer uses this, not a mode bit, and uses the *synchronous* arm.** That matters because the default arm is not synchronous: `applySecurePathRestriction(…, sync=false)` on `win32` fires `bestEffortRestrictWindowsPath` and **returns `true` optimistically** (`secure-file.ts:201-209`, esp. `:206-208`), errors swallowed in the callback (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:37-39`) **[code]**; and directory hardening always takes that arm — `hardenSecurePathOnce` short-circuits every `win32` directory into `hardenSecureDirectoryOnce`, which caches the path as hardened whether or not the ACL landed (`secure-file.ts:59-73`, cache set at `:66`) **[code]**, deliberately, to stop a main-thread storm (#4901). So on Windows **a lane directory hardened through the ordinary path is best-effort and unverified**, which is not good enough for the directory a credential is about to land in. **The lane's rule, and it is scoped to a *native* lane — rev 13 stated it unconditionally and that version could not ship beside §2n, which is round 3's sixth major and the unfixed twin of the UNC refusal rev 13 did correct.** For a lane whose directory is a **local drive path**: `restrictWindowsPathSync(laneDir, true)` at creation, its boolean return **checked**, and provisioning **fails closed** if it is false — no lane exists until its DACL verified. This is the same fail-closed shape §2i uses for macOS, for the same reason: a silently unisolated lane is the worst class in this design. **For an S9e lane the rule does not apply at all, and the difference is a skip and not a no-op.** §2n writes **every** S9e lane through `\\wsl.localhost\<distro>\home\<user>\.local\share\orca\claude-lanes\<principalId>\`; `restrictWindowsPathSync` (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:43-64`) runs `Get-Acl`/`Set-Acl` under PowerShell and returns **`false` on any throw** (`:59-63`) **[code]**, so a redirector path that cannot carry a DACL returns false — and the unconditional rule would then refuse S9e provisioning outright, every time. §2m(4)(b) called the verification "a no-op there", which is not what a `false` return is. **Decision, mirroring §2m(4)'s carve-out exactly rather than inventing a second shape:** the DACL step is **not run** for a candidate `parseWslUncPath` matches (`shared/wsl-paths.ts:6-17`, regex at `:8`) **[code]**; that lane's isolation control is §2n(i)'s in-distro `chmod 0700/0600`, read back and verified inside the distro the way `assertManagedAuthPath` already re-canonicalizes there, and provisioning fails closed on *that* verification instead. Every other UNC root stays refused at provisioning (§2m(4)). This mattered for scheduling as well as for tidiness, and rev 15 records how the schedule resolved: **the owner's answer is native shells (§2m(8)), so S9e is deferred** — but the carve-out stays exactly as written, because the two rules still could not both ship, and §2n is a design held ready rather than a slice to be re-derived when a WSL-forced pane appears.
   **And one host-side interaction that would otherwise undo it.** Orca re-grants ACEs across `userData` at startup: `ensureWindowsUserDataAclGrant` (`startup/windows-user-data-acl.ts:125-163`) runs `icacls <userData>\* /grant:r <identity>:(OI)(CI)(F) /C` over the immediate children and then over the root (`runIcaclsGrant` `:84-100`, targets at `:147-148`), marker-gated to run at most once per identity (`:136-140`), fired from `index.ts:1316-1326` because "Chromium's BrowserWindow ctor resets userData to a Protected DACL" **[code]**; and `writeFileAtomically`'s EPERM path calls `grantDirAcl(dirname(targetPath))` — `icacls <dir> /grant:r <identity>:(OI)(CI)(F)` (`win32-utils.ts:184-202`, args at `:189`) — on every failed write (`codex-accounts/fs-utils.ts:24-38`, the call at `:26`) **[code]**. `<userData>\claude-lanes` is a direct child of `userData`, so the startup grant **does** touch the lane root, and both calls add an **inheritable** ACE. **[inferred, and graded as such] — and rev 12's version of this inference was wrong in its mechanism while right in its conclusion, so the mechanism is restated rather than the conclusion softened.** The two calls do not have the same target. `ensureWindowsUserDataAclGrant` grants over `<userData>\*` and then `<userData>` (`runIcaclsGrant` targets at `:147-148`) **[code]** — the wildcard names the lane **root**, `<userData>\claude-lanes`, and a lane directory one level below it is defended by protection exactly as rev 12 said: `/grant:r` writes an *inheritable* ACE on the root, and a protected child does not inherit it. But `writeFileAtomically`'s EPERM path calls `grantDirAcl(dirname(targetPath))` (`codex-accounts/fs-utils.ts:26` → `win32-utils.ts:184-202`, args at `:189`) **[code]**, whose target for a lane credential write **is the lane directory itself** — and `/grant:r` on a directory writes an **explicit** ACE, which DACL protection does not block at all; protection only stops *inherited* ACEs. So protection is not what defends that second call. What defends it is that the granted identity is `resolveCurrentIdentity()` (`win32-utils.ts:185`) — the same current-user principal the hardening script already puts in its allowed set (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:71`, granted at `:85-95`, verified at `:102-106`) **[code]** — so the ACE it adds is one the read-back verification already accepts. The read-back still passes; the reason it passes is this one, not the other. That inference is exactly the kind this design refuses to rest on silently: **the control is a test, not the reasoning** — after a simulated `ensureWindowsUserDataAclGrant` and a simulated `grantDirAcl` on the lane root, re-read the lane directory's DACL and assert it is still protected and still names only the three allowed SIDs, with a mutation proof (creating the lane unprotected turns it red). Windows-only test, §5.

**(2) Atomic writes while the CLI holds `.credentials.json` open — Orca already has the retry, and it names this exact case.** On Windows a rename over a file another process holds open fails `EPERM`/`EACCES`/`EBUSY`. `writeFileAtomically` does not call `renameSync` directly: it calls `renameFileWithWindowsRetry` (`codex-accounts/fs-utils.ts:15` → `:200-202` → `runFileOperationWithWindowsRetry` `:208-223`), which retries **6 times on `win32`** (`:209`) on exactly those three codes (`:216`), parking the thread with `Atomics.wait` on a 50 ms-per-attempt ramp (`:217`, `:227-230`) — ~750 ms total — and the comment names the offenders outright: *"antivirus, Claude CLI, Codex CLI"* (`:195-199`) **[code]**. So rev 10's §2c decision to build the lane writer on `writeFileAtomically` is **validated on Windows for the rename**, which was not obvious and is now checked.
   **But neither existing primitive is sufficient alone, and that is a new rev-11 decision.** `writeFileAtomically` has the Windows rename retry and **no ACL work**. `writeSecureFile` has the ACL work — it restricts the *temp* file synchronously **before** the rename so the credential is never briefly published under inherited ACEs (`secure-file.ts:120-122`), then restricts the published path and caches only on confirmed success (`:123-126`) — but it publishes with a bare `renameSync` (`:122`) **[code]**, i.e. **no retry**, so a live `claude` holding the lane's `.credentials.json` makes a push throw where `writeFileAtomically` would have succeeded. **Decision: the lane writer combines them** — ACL-restrict the temp file synchronously, publish through `renameFileWithWindowsRetry`, re-assert and verify the published path's DACL, and treat a failed verification as a failed push (`accounts.lane.push_write_failed`) rather than a silent success. **And the retry can be exhausted, which rev 12 defined no refusal for.** `runFileOperationWithWindowsRetry` **rethrows** the last error once the six attempts are spent (`codex-accounts/fs-utils.ts:220`, inside `:208-223`) **[code]** — the window is ~750 ms and a `claude` that holds `.credentials.json` open past it is entirely possible on a loaded box. **Decision: the lane writer catches that rethrow and refuses `accounts.lane.push_write_locked`**, with a complete human sentence naming the cause and the remedy ("a Claude session on the host is holding this lane's credential file; retry in a moment"), because an untyped throw is the one class §3's Rule-3 row says every new refusal must not be — the client has no string for the code and nothing at all for a raw `EBUSY`. The lane is unchanged when it fires: the rename never published, so the old credential is still in place and the watermark has not moved. Extracted once, in `claude-accounts/lane-credential-writer.ts` (§6, S9b), not by widening either existing helper.
   **Two consequences for §2f's wipe sweep.** First, the two primitives use **different temp-name shapes** — `${target}.${pid}.${randomUUID()}.tmp` (`fs-utils.ts:12`) and `${target}.${pid}.${Date.now()}.${8 hex}.tmp` (`secure-file.ts:111`) **[code]** — so the sweep must match the pattern, not either literal, and §5's planted-tmp test plants **both**. Second, `writeFileAtomically`'s EPERM retry writes a **second** temp file (`fs-utils.ts:27-33`) and removes it only in its own catch (`:33`) **[code]**: on Windows a crash mid-retry can leave *two* full-credential blobs at rest. The sweep already covers this by matching the pattern; §5 asserts it.

**(3) Where the Windows CLI keeps the credential — graded honestly, and not assumed.** Orca's own model is unambiguous and is **[code]**: `writeRuntimeCredentials(credentialsJson)` writes `<configDir>/.credentials.json` on **every** platform (`runtime-auth-service.ts:477-478`, path from `runtime-paths.ts:21`), and only `darwin` *additionally* writes the Keychain (`:479-489`, the guard at `:479` and its comment at `:480`) **[code]**; there is no Windows branch, and `grep -rni "dpapi\|wincred\|CredRead" src` returns nothing Claude-related — its only hits are Git Credential Manager suppression (`hooks.ts:775`, `ipc/repos.ts:2434`) **[code]**. Orca also drives the Windows CLI through `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` today: `runClaudeCommand`'s `win32` arm sets `env: { ...process.env, CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR: configDir.windowsPath }` (`claude-accounts/service.ts:1078-1085`) **[code]**. **What that establishes:** Orca *treats* the plain file under `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` as the whole credential store on Windows, and the variable is honoured. **What it does not establish** is that the Windows CLI keeps **no** additional store — a DPAPI blob or a Credential Manager entry outside the config dir. Nothing in this tree observes that either way, and the [doc] statement "`.credentials.json` is a plain file at `0600` on Linux" says nothing about Windows. **Grade: [inferred], unverified.** It is load-bearing — if the Windows CLI keeps any credential state outside `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, lanes on Windows collapse the way macOS collapses under a pre-2.1 CLI, silently. **Decision, mirroring §2i's macOS rule exactly: Windows lane provisioning is gated on a live probe** (§5, Windows-only) that provisions two lanes, logs in to a different account in each, and asserts each lane's `claude auth status --json` reports its own account with the other lane's credential file deleted. Until that probe passes on the pinned build, Windows lane provisioning stays **off** — a hard gate in §6, not a note.

**(4) Path canonicalization — the containment checks are not Windows-correct today.** Every containment check in this design canonicalizes the way `managed-auth-path.ts` does: refuse a symlink (`:23-25`), `realpathSync` both sides (`:26-27`), then `canonicalCandidate === canonicalRoot || !canonicalCandidate.startsWith(canonicalRoot + sep)` (`:28-33`), then an exact relative-segment match (`:37-47`) **[code]**. On Windows that is four separate problems, and Orca already knows the fix for three of them elsewhere in the tree.
   - **8.3 short names and case.** `realpathSync` is Node's own resolver; `realpathSync.native` calls the OS and returns the true long name and true case. Orca uses `.native` precisely where canonical identity matters — `codex/config-toml-trust.ts:167`, `:209`, `:812`, `codex-trust-config-rollback.ts:27`, `agent-trust-presets.ts:163`, `ssh/ssh-config-include-expander.ts:290`, `git/repo.ts:238` **[code]** — and `managed-auth-path.ts` is one of the places that does **not**. So `--config-dir C:\Users\OWNER~1\AppData\...\claude-lanes\<id>` (an 8.3 component) or a drive-letter case flip can defeat a `startsWith` on a non-native realpath. **Decision:** every lane containment check uses `realpathSync.native` on both sides, and compares **case-insensitively on `win32`** — the precedent for the comparison is already here: `linkTargetsMatch` branches on platform and folds through `normalizeWindowsLinkTarget`, `linkTarget.replace(/^\\\\\?\\/, '').toLowerCase()` (`codex/codex-home-paths.ts:249-258`) **[code]**, which also strips the extended-length prefix.
   - **Junctions.** A directory junction needs **no privilege** on Windows — Orca's own worktree code prefers it for exactly that reason (`ipc/worktree-symlinks.ts:40-54`, the function at `:47-55`) **[code]** — so it is the cheap escape a `--config-dir` capture would use. Node reports a junction as **both** a symlink and a directory, and Orca has already been bitten: *"check `isSymbolicLink` before `isDirectory` — a Windows junction reports both, and the symlink branch must win"* (`opencode/hook-service.ts:1100`) **[code]**. The lane check's existing `lstatSync(...).isSymbolicLink()` refusal (`managed-auth-path.ts:30-33`) therefore does catch a junction — **provided it stays ordered first**, which §5 asserts with a Windows-only planted-junction test rather than leaving it to the reading.
   - **Extended-length and UNC forms.** `\\?\C:\…` and `\\server\share\…` compare unequal to their ordinary forms. Precedents for normalizing both are in-tree: `codex/codex-session-resume-home.ts:33-36` strips `\\?\` from a drive path, and `window/clipboard-windows-image-file.ts:39-42` matches both the `\\?\X:\` and `\\?\UNC\` shapes **[code]**. The lane check normalizes the prefix before comparing. **A *native* lane path is additionally required to be a local drive path** — never a remote UNC share — because such a root's ACLs are set by a machine this design has said nothing about; it is refused at provisioning. **The rule is scoped to native lanes, and rev 12's unscoped version could not ship beside §2n: S9e writes *every* lane through `\\wsl.localhost\<distro>\home\<user>\.local\share\orca\claude-lanes\<principalId>\`, which is the exact shape the unscoped rule refused.** **Decision: the refusal carves out the WSL redirector forms and nothing else.** A UNC candidate is accepted only when `parseWslUncPath` matches it — `^//(wsl\.localhost|wsl\$)/([^/]+)(/.*)?$`, case-insensitive, both the modern and legacy prefixes (`src/shared/wsl-paths.ts:6-17`, regex at `:8`) **[code]** — and it is then handed to §2n's **in-distro** canonicalizer rather than to a Windows-side `startsWith`. That is the in-tree precedent doing exactly this, not an exception invented for S9e: `assertManagedAuthPath` **parses** the WSL UNC path (`claude-accounts/service.ts:969-970`) and on `win32` re-canonicalizes *inside* the distro — `readlink -f` on candidate and root, the marker file read and compared, and a `case "$candidate_real" in "$managed_root_real"/*/auth)` match with exit 35 otherwise (`:978-1014`) **[code]**. Every other UNC root is refused as before. **Two consequences stated rather than left to the reader.** (a) For an S9e lane the containment *proof* lives inside the distro, not in `shared/lane-path-containment.ts`, so its mutation proof lives in §2n's in-distro tests; the Windows-side module's job for that shape is to recognise it and refuse everything else. (b) **The redirector's own reachability is the permission story §2n(i) does not cover.** `\\wsl.localhost\<distro>` is not a remote share: it is served by the WSL 9p redirector for *this* Windows user on *this* machine, so no foreign ACL authority is introduced — but the file's permissions on the other side are **Linux** modes, not a DACL, which is why §2n(i) mandates `chmod 0700/0600` in-distro and why §2m(1)'s DACL step is **not run** there at all — rev 13 wrote "explicitly a no-op", and a `restrictWindowsPathSync` that cannot set a DACL does not no-op, it returns `false` (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:60-63`) **[code]**, which under rev 13's unconditional fail-closed rule refused every S9e lane. §2m(1) now carries the same `parseWslUncPath` carve-out this bullet does. What that buys is the §2n residual, unchanged: on the one-account shared host both developers are the same Linux uid inside the distro, so those modes bound nothing between them either.
   - **Where this applies, so the list is not decorative:** the lane root containment in `principal-credential-lane.ts`; §2d's `addClaudeFromConfigDir`/`addCodexFromHome` capture refusal (which already had to be realpath-based, and on Windows must additionally be `.native` + case-folded); §2a's resume-path and `ompResumeFilePath` containment; guard 3(a)'s "no value in the merged env canonicalizes under `<userData>\claude-lanes\`"; **and — decided rather than left named, which is what rev 13 did — `resolveOwnedClaudeManagedAuthPath` itself (`managed-auth-path.ts:36-42`: plain `realpathSync` on both sides at `:26-27`, case-sensitive `startsWith(canonicalRoot + sep)` at `:37-41`) **[code]**.** §2a states that the pre-lane injection target "that exists right now is `<userData>/claude-accounts/<id>/auth`, a real managed-account credential store", so the one containment check guarding *that* store is Windows-incorrect on the pivot platform while this design calls it the live target. Guard 3(b) and §2d close the two S9 surfaces, so the residual was narrow — but "narrow" is not a disposition. **Decision: it adopts `shared/lane-path-containment.ts` too**, in S9a, which is a two-line import swap at an existing call site rather than new machinery, and it gets the same Windows-only mutation proof as the rest of the list. Each gets its own Windows-only mutation proof: reverting any one of them to `realpathSync` + case-sensitive compare must turn a named test red.

**(5) Environment: case-insensitivity, ConPTY, and the WSLENV export.**
   - **The env-key guards are case-sensitive and Windows environment variables are not. This is a genuine hole, found by re-opening §2a's guards against `win32`.** `CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS` is a literal tuple (`claude-accounts/environment.ts:9-14`); `applyClaudeEnvPatch` deletes by exact key, `delete baseEnv[key]` (`:23-25`), deletes `ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS` by exact key too (`:22-23`), and writes `baseEnv.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`:44-46`); `hasClaudeAuthEnvConflict` tests `CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS.some((key) => Boolean(env[key]))` and `env.ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS` (`:66-70`) **[code]**. Every one is exact-case — `environment.ts:9-14`, `:19-23`, `:66-70` re-opened **[code]**, and *that* is the load-bearing support for what follows. **Rev 13 supported it instead with a grep claim that does not reproduce, so the claim is corrected rather than restated:** `grep -rn "case-insensitive\|caseInsensitive" src/main/providers src/main/daemon src/main/pty --include="*.ts" | grep -v "\.test\."` returns **four** hits, not nothing — `pty/legacy-terminal-shim-dir.ts:258`, `:261`, `:264` (an explicitly case-insensitive PATH-entry matcher, used by a strip that already folds Windows keys at `:204-206`, `:216`, `:222`) and `pty/windows-path-segment-merge.ts:18` **[code]**. The spawn env is an ordinary `Record<string, string>` built by spreads (`local-pty-provider.ts:675-684`) **[code]**, so `env.claude_config_dir` and `env.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` are two distinct keys to Orca — and one variable to Windows. **Consequence on the shared host:** a client-supplied `env: { claude_config_dir: 'C:\\…' }`, or `envToDelete: ['Claude_Config_Dir']`, passes §2a's guard 1 and guard 3 untouched and reaches the child, where Windows resolves it as `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` — the *exact* injection §2a exists to close, wearing different capitalization. **Decision: on `win32`, guard 1, guard 3, `hasClaudeAuthEnvConflict` *and `applyClaudeEnvPatch`'s own strip loop* compare env keys case-insensitively**, and the lane keys are **collapsed** to one canonical casing — every case-variant deleted before the canonical one is written. **Two things rev 13 got wrong about that collapse, and the second of them is round 3's third blocker.** *First, the justification.* Rev 13 said "a record carrying two casings of one Windows variable has undefined precedence". The precedence is **defined**, and this tree states it: "Why: Win32 uses the first case-insensitive key, and object order preserves block order" (`pty/windows-path-segment-merge.ts:18`), tested at `pty/windows-environment-path.test.ts:652-655` **[code]**. The collapse is still right; its reason is the opposite of the one given — a residual duplicate is a **deterministic** wrong resolution, not a coin flip, which is what makes the second point a blocker rather than a hygiene note. *Second, the site.* `computeLaneLaunch` runs at the **anchor** (`spawnForStablePane`, `pty.ts:907-919`), i.e. it writes `spawnOptions.env` — and the provider then **rebuilds the env after it**: `...mergeGitConfigEnvProtocol(stripInheritedBuildModeEnv(process.env), args.env)` (`local-pty-provider.ts:675-676`; daemon twin `pty-subprocess.ts:610`), whose body is `{ ...baseEnv, ...overrideEnv }` (`shared/git-credential-prompt-env.ts:11`) **[code]** — plain exact-key overwrite. A collapse performed upstream of that merge does not survive it: row 19 explicitly supports the Orca process carrying its own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`runtime-paths.ts:14-25`) **[code]**, so if the *process* env holds it under a different casing — `Claude_Config_Dir` — the spread reinstates that key **before** `args.env` adds the canonical one, both land in the plain object, and by the rule above the child resolves the inherited shared-lane path. A lane pane then runs on `%USERPROFILE%\.claude` — the other developer's credential — while §2h renders `credentialLane: 'grant'` and §2k bills its usage to the lane owner. R1 and R3 fail silently, on the pivot platform, through the one post-anchor step the §2 preamble's invariant asserts is safe. **Decision: the *comparisons* stay at the anchor (guard 1, guard 3, `hasClaudeAuthEnvConflict`, `applyClaudeEnvPatch`), and the *collapse* moves post-merge into the provider — the in-tree precedent is exact and already sits there.** `collapseWindowsPathEnvKeys(env, requestedEnv)` runs **after** the daemon's merge (`daemon/pty-subprocess.ts:163-190`, called at `:833`, under the comment "Removes the second PATH key only when the daemon's env merge created it") **[code]** — the same problem, the same shape, for PATH. S9a adds the lane-key twin beside it and, because the **local provider has no equivalent at all**, adds one there too, at the corresponding point after `expandWindowsPathEnvironmentVariables(finalEnv)` (`local-pty-provider.ts:847`). The rule is the daemon's own: a one-key lane patch is authoritative, so every other casing of that key is deleted; two or zero came from inherited state and are left to the guards. This is the same shape as §2a's WSL-shell refusal — a decision that cannot be evaluated at the anchor, moved to the step that can. Not a POSIX change: on Linux and macOS the keys stay case-sensitive, which is correct there. **`applyClaudeEnvPatch` is the call site rev 11 named nowhere and newly put on a lane path, so it gets its own sentence.** Its strip is `for (const key of CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS) { delete baseEnv[key] }` (`claude-accounts/environment.ts:36`; `:35` is the `if (options?.stripAuthEnv)` that opens the block — rev 15 cited `:18-21`, corrected here) plus the exact-case `delete baseEnv.ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS` (`:22-23`) **[code]** — and §2k Fact 3 hands the **lane's** `authPreparation` to `rate-limits/claude-pty.ts:247-251` **[code]**, which runs that strip over `{ ...process.env, TERM }`, i.e. over the whole host process environment. On the shared Windows host an inherited lowercase `anthropic_api_key` survives the exact-case strip, reaches the hidden probe's `claude` as `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, **outranks** the lane's stored login **[doc]**, and the probe then reports the wrong account's usage under the lane's row — the misattribution class §2k exists to remove, arriving through the one path that does not go near `computeLaneLaunch`. Same file, same fix, one more call site; the probe is the named vector in §5. **And rev 15 fixed only the *auth-var* half of that call site, which is round 4's second major — the same paragraph names `applyClaudeEnvPatch` “the call site rev 11 named nowhere and newly put on a lane path” and then leaves the *lane key* on it with no collapse.** Re-opened: `claude-pty.ts:247-251` builds `{ ...process.env, TERM: 'xterm-256color' }` — a plain `Record`, so the **stored casing** of every inherited variable survives the spread — and `applyClaudeEnvPatch` then writes `baseEnv.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR = patch.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` by **exact key** (`claude-accounts/environment.ts:44-46`) **[code]**. Row 19 explicitly supports the Orca process carrying its own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`runtime-paths.ts:14-25`, the read at `:15`) **[code]**; stored as `Claude_Config_Dir`, **both** keys reach `pty.spawn` (`:303-311`) **[code]** and, by this bullet's own rule — Win32 resolves the first case-insensitive key and object order preserves block order (`pty/windows-path-segment-merge.ts:18`, tested `windows-environment-path.test.ts:652-655`) **[code]** — the probe's `claude` runs on `%USERPROFILE%\.claude`, the shared lane. **The collapse decision above is written as a property of every lane key, and this is the one path with no site for it, because the collapse was placed post-merge *inside the provider* and the probe never reaches a provider (row 32).** **Decision: the probe's env build is the *third* collapse site**, beside the provider's and the daemon's — `shared/lane-env-key-case.ts`'s collapse runs over the object `applyClaudeEnvPatch` returns, before `pty.spawn` at `claude-pty.ts:303-311`, so exactly one casing of `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` reaches the hidden `claude`. **What it prevents, spelled out because §2k's join makes each consequence a wrong write rather than a wrong read:** with the shared config dir winning, §2k would (a) attribute the probe's usage to the **lane's** row through the new paneKey/configDir join, (b) hold the **shared** lane's token while the synthetic gate id defers **the lane's** account rotation, and (c) run the post-probe `syncLane` over the lane, so a `cli-observed` rotation of the *shared* credential would move the **lane's** watermark. **Latent, not live, and only on the pivot platform — stated rather than used as a reason to defer it:** §6 gate (iv) ships the per-lane pull disabled on `win32` (`claude-fetcher.ts:1062-1064` returns `null` for every per-account preparation) **[code]**, so the lane arm of row 32 does not run there yet. The collapse lands with the lane arm, in the same slice, because the alternative is a decision stated as universal with a known hole on the platform it was written for. **Mutation proof:** a lane create supplying `claude_config_dir` must be refused/stripped and a lane *probe* launched with a lowercase `anthropic_api_key` in the host env must strip it, and reverting either comparison to exact-case must turn its test red.
   - **ConPTY.** Both spawn implementations pass `useConptyDll: true` on `win32` — the daemon directly (`daemon/pty-subprocess.ts:556-565`, the option at `:563`), and the local provider through `windowsConptyDllOptions()` (`providers/local-pty-utils.ts:206-207`), spread into the primary spawn inside `spawnShellWithFallback` (`:246`, applied at `:275`) and into its fallback chain (`:225`), reached from `local-pty-provider.ts:883` **[code]** — the local half of a claim about "both implementations", uncited in rev 12 and re-opened here — and Windows console membership is probed out-of-process through node-pty's console-list agent (`providers/windows-conpty-process-membership.ts:1-30`) **[code]**. Neither reads or writes an environment variable, so the two-part post-anchor invariant (§2 preamble) is **unaffected by ConPTY**: the env handed to `pty.spawn` is the env the child gets. Stated because the invariant claims to be a property of *every* post-anchor step, and on Windows there is one more step than on Linux.
   - **The WSLENV export sites, re-read.** Unchanged and confirmed at the cited lines: `if (finalEnv.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR) { addWslEnvKeys(finalEnv, ['CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR']) }` (`providers/local-pty-provider.ts:762-764`) and its daemon twin (`daemon/pty-subprocess.ts:762-764`) **[code]**. Two facts sharpen §2a's invariant now that Windows is the host rather than a footnote. First, **both sites are nested inside `if (pathWin32.basename(shellPath).toLowerCase() === 'wsl.exe')`** — `local-pty-provider.ts:733` for the local twin, and `pty-subprocess.ts:723` for the daemon one, whose export at `:762-764` sits inside that block; `:775` is a *later, sibling* basename check guarding `addOrcaWslInteropEnv(env)` after the `:723` block closes at `:774`, and cannot gate a line above it (rev 11 cited `:775`; corrected here on re-opening) **[code]**. Meanwhile the *shell* classifier further up the same function is broader — `const isWslShell = Boolean(wslInfo) || pathWin32.basename(shellPath).toLowerCase() === 'wsl.exe'` (`local-pty-provider.ts:707`) **[code]**. So the two classifiers §2a warned about really are different predicates in the same function, **22 lines apart**, with `buildSpawnEnv` (`:712-722`), the `envToDelete` replay (`:725-726`) and the `TERM` restore (`:728-730` — `:728` `if (args.env?.TERM) {`, `:729` the assignment, `:730` the closing brace; rev 19 corrected the §2 preamble's copy of this citation and left this one at `:728-729`, corrected here on re-opening so the two sites agree, round 8's minor) between them — the distance strengthens the point rather than softening it. Second, the export keys on the variable's **mere presence**, so the invariant *"a lane path is never present in a spawn env that takes the WSL branch"* is the only thing standing between a Windows lane path and a distro that cannot use it. On the Windows host this is no longer a hypothetical: it is the default platform. §5 keeps its positive control and mutation proof and adds a Windows-only case — a lane pane whose shell resolves to `wsl.exe` must be **refused in the lane-aware `buildSpawnEnv` arm** (`local-pty-provider.ts:712-722`, over the ctx's own `shellPath`/`isWsl` at `:718-719`, using the export gate's own predicate at `:733`; daemon twin `pty-subprocess.ts:723`), not silently exported. **Not at `computeLaneLaunch`, where rev 12 sited it:** the two classifiers this bullet has just measured at 22 lines apart both live *inside* the provider, and `PtySpawnOptions` (`pty-provider-contract.ts:41-108`) carries neither the resolved shell nor `isWsl` **[code]** — so the anchor cannot evaluate this rule, and §2a moves it to the one site that already has both values and the lane (§2a, the WSLENV invariant).
   - **Correction to a rev-10 [code] claim, since this line was re-opened.** Rev 10 described step (a) of the post-anchor enumeration as the provider's base merge `{ ...process.env, ...args.env }` at `local-pty-provider.ts:676`. The line is `...mergeGitConfigEnvProtocol(stripInheritedBuildModeEnv(process.env), args.env)` (`:675-676`) **[code]** — process env is filtered and the two are merged by a helper rather than spread directly. The substance rev 10 relied on is intact **on POSIX**: `args.env` still wins over the process env by exact-key overwrite (`shared/git-credential-prompt-env.ts:11`, `{ ...baseEnv, ...overrideEnv }`) **[code]**, so the lane patch travelling in `args.env` still wins. **On `win32` it does not, and rev 13 carried the POSIX reading forward as if it were platform-neutral:** exact-key overwrite leaves a differently-cased inherited `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` in the object beside the canonical one, and Win32 resolves the *first* case-insensitive key in block order — the inherited one. That is why the collapse above is sited **after** this merge and not at the anchor. The description was imprecise twice; both corrections are made here rather than left standing.

**(6) Threat model: one `orca serve` under one Windows account, serving both developers.** The Linux statement was "both developers run as the same Unix user, so `0600` protects nothing from a peer who *is* that user". The Windows statement is the same shape and slightly worse. **Say it plainly: on the shared box there is one Windows account, one `orca serve` process under it, and both developers' grants are served by it.** The Windows equivalents of the Linux residuals:
   - **`/proc/<pid>/environ` → the process environment block.** Any process running as the same user can open another's handle with `PROCESS_QUERY_INFORMATION | PROCESS_VM_READ` and read its PEB environment block; `Get-Process`/WMI expose enough to locate it. So the lane path — and §2a already conceded the grant id inside it — is readable by a co-tenant, exactly as on Linux.
   - **`ptrace` → `DebugActiveProcess` / `ReadProcessMemory`.** Same-user debugging needs no extra privilege, so a running `claude`'s in-memory token is readable. `SeDebugPrivilege` is the *administrator* escalation, not the same-user one.
   - **Reading the lane file at rest.** This one is **worse than Linux, and it is worth naming exactly.** The lane's own DACL grants FullControl to three SIDs — the current user, `S-1-5-18`, and **`S-1-5-32-544`, the local Administrators group** (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:71`, `:85-95`) **[code]**. On a Windows **Server** shared by two developers, both are plausibly administrators, and an administrator is inside the lane's ACL **by construction**. So on Windows the "co-tenant can read a resident lane file" residual holds for *any local administrator*, not only for the account Orca runs as. Changing the script's allowed-SID set is not a fix — SYSTEM and Administrators are there because a backup, an installer or a repair otherwise cannot touch the file, and an administrator can rewrite any DACL anyway.
   - **The named pipe — and rev 13 named the wrong one, which matters because this bullet is what scopes every refusal §2d deliberately leaves open.** The endpoint an anonymous local `orca` CLI reaches the **runtime** on is `createRuntimeTransportMetadata` (`runtime/runtime-rpc.ts:1853-1870`): `\\.\pipe\orca-${pid}-${suffix}` on `win32` (`:1864`), `join(userDataPath, 'o-<pid>-<suffix>.sock')` on POSIX (`:1869`) **[code]**. `\\?\pipe\orca-terminal-host-v<version>-<hash>` (`daemon/daemon-spawner.ts:98-110`, win32 branch `:105-107`) **[code]** is the **daemon PTY-host** endpoint — a different pipe, a different client — and rev 13 cited it here, in §2a and in §4. Corrected at all three. **And the substance the right file carries, which rev 13's citation could not:** the in-tree comment reads "named pipes lack the chmod hardening of Unix sockets; a per-runtime suffix avoids a stable, guessable endpoint name" (`:1863`) **[code]**. So on Windows the POSIX socket's mode-bit protection is **replaced by obscurity plus the pipe's default DACL** — not transposed into an equivalent. §2d's "anonymous local caller" and §2a's "local `orca` equivalent over the Unix socket" therefore mean *whoever can open that pipe on this machine*, which on a default Windows ACL is any process of the same user plus administrators — and the per-pid, per-suffix name is a discovery cost, not an access control. Every §2d refusal that is deliberately scoped to leave the anonymous local caller unchanged — `selectClaude` for a lane-less caller, `resolveInheritedLane` case (ii) — is therefore scoped to that set. Unchanged in intent, restated in Windows terms so the envelope is not accidentally read as narrower than it is.
   - **The conclusion is unchanged and should not be softened:** lanes defend against accidental cross-switching and the `selectClaude`-class hijack; they do **not** defend against a hostile co-tenant. On Windows, "co-tenant" additionally includes any local administrator.
   **The alternative, named as an alternative and not recommended.** Two `orca serve` instances under **two Windows user accounts** would make the residuals real boundaries: separate profiles, separate `%USERPROFILE%\.claude`, separate `userData`, DACLs that actually exclude the other person, and separate named pipes. It would make S9's lane machinery largely unnecessary on that box. **Why it is not the recommendation:** it forfeits the thing the owner is building toward — B2 **shared presence**. Two serves are two hosts: R4 dies (neither developer can view or type into the other's terminals, because a terminal belongs to one runtime), the presence registry has nothing to join across, `orchestration.workerStart` cannot span them, one worktree cannot be shared without a second sharing mechanism, and each developer pairs to a different host from their phone. Lanes exist precisely to keep one shared host *and* per-person credentials; two serves buy isolation by giving up the shared host. Recorded so the trade is visible, and so that if the owner ever chooses it, the reason S9 shrinks is on the record.

**(7) The §5 live probes must run on Windows Server, and CI cannot run them.** The §5 in-repo suite (vitest, `config/vitest.config.ts`) runs anywhere; the ACL, junction, 8.3, rename-retry and env-case controls are **Windows-only tests** that must be skipped on other platforms rather than faked — a `describe.runIf(process.platform === 'win32')` shape, with the mutation proofs attached to the Windows arm. The **live** probes are harder: §5's steps (0)–(8) drive a real `claude` binary, a real Keychain or DACL, and a real second developer, and the Linux VPS CI has none of them. **They cannot execute on the Linux VPS CI and must not be marked green there.** They need either a **Windows runner** or the **owner's own Windows Server box**, run by hand before S9b merges. Which live steps are Windows-only: (0)/(0b) `echo %CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR%` in a lane pane under **PowerShell** and **Git Bash** — the two shells the owner named (§2m(8)) — and under `cmd`, which is not a habit but is the resolver's middle fallback (`local-pty-provider.ts:599`) **[code]**; the new **(12b)**, the three-shell repeat of (0b) — PowerShell, Git Bash and `cmd` — **at least one of which must be driven through a per-tab `shellOverride` rather than the host setting** (row 36), which rev 16 introduced in §5 and left out of this list (round 5 minor); the new **(12c)**, the Git Bash environment-passthrough probe §2m(8) gates its refusal on; the new **(9)** DACL read-back (`Get-Acl` on the lane directory: protected, three SIDs, FullControl, no deny); the new **(10)** credential-store probe of item (3); the new **(11)** rename-under-lock probe — push to a lane while a `claude` in it holds `.credentials.json` open, and assert the push succeeds inside the ~750 ms retry window rather than throwing; and **(12)** the 8.3/junction/UNC containment attempts of item (4). Step (8), the macOS Keychain probe, stays macOS-only and stays a gate for macOS provisioning only. Steps (1)–(7b) are platform-neutral in intent but must be **re-run on Windows** as well as on the platform they were first written for, because the pinned-CLI assumption (§4) is per-platform.

**(8) The two native shells the developers actually use — the owner's answer, and what each one does to a lane's environment.** Owner, 2026-08-22: on the shared box the developers use **native Windows terminals only** — the owner works in **Git Bash**, the other developer in **PowerShell**, deliberately not WSL. That resolves §7's first new open question as **native** and **defers S9e** (§2n) out of the S9 delivery order and out of every gate; §2n stays designed and independently shippable, and the trigger that would revive it is named there. Three consequences follow, and two of them are obligations on the lane environment rather than notes.
   - **The shell setting is host-wide, so both shells are in scope for every lane — and on path B the *resolved* shell is per-pane, which rev 15 missed and rev 16 states as round 4's third major.** `terminalWindowsShell` is one setting, read into the provider as `getWindowsShell: () => getSettings?.()?.terminalWindowsShell` (`ipc/pty.ts:2433`) and consumed at `local-pty-provider.ts:596-600` as `args.shellOverride || this.opts.getWindowsShell?.() || process.env.COMSPEC || 'powershell.exe'` **[code]**. Rev 15 disposed of the **first** disjunct by citing path A, where `requestedShellOverride: undefined` (`pty.ts:4529-4536`, the field at `:4532` → `shared/local-windows-terminal-runtime.ts:30-67`, the branch at `:41-44`) **[code]** leaves the setting deciding. **Path B does not pass undefined.** `pty:spawn`'s args declare `shellOverride?: string` (`ipc/pty.ts:5923`) and **both** of its resolution sites pass `requestedShellOverride: args.shellOverride` (`:6074`, `:6167`) **[code]**, which `resolveLocalWindowsTerminalRuntimeOptions` prefers over the setting (`shared/local-windows-terminal-runtime.ts:41`) **[code]**; the renderer supplies it from the tab (`terminal-pane/pty-connection.ts:3591`, sent at `:3831`; minted at `terminal/terminal-tab-create.ts:39`; a whole `newTerminalWithShell` entry point at `tab-group/useTabGroupWorkspaceModel.ts:656-672`) **[code]**. Path B is **row 24** — the cold-restore respawn, the sleeping-agent wake and the nine renderer launch builders — i.e. the edge every *existing* lane pane re-enters through, so a lane pane's resolved shell is a property of the pane, not of the host. **Two consequences, both carried rather than absorbed.** (1) The fact set is wider than two: a lane pane can resolve to `pwsh.exe` or `cmd.exe` as well as to Git Bash and PowerShell, so the requirement below is *“every fact below holds for every lane, whatever the pane resolved to”*, and the `cmd` statusline note at the end of this section is what makes the third case harmless. (2) `shellOverride` is a **launch-shaping input reaching the provider** that the §2 enumeration did not carry — the same class as row 18, one layer lower and client-reachable — so it is enumerated and dispositioned as **row 36** (allowlisted: it picks a shell binary, never a credential), and a `wsl.exe` override is caught by §2a's provider-sited refusal over the *resolved* `shellPath` (`local-pty-provider.ts:733`) and by nothing upstream of it. There is still no per-principal shell in this design and rev 16 does not add one. Git Bash resolution itself falls back: `resolveWindowsGitBashShellPath(shellFamily)` at `:606`, used at `:622-625`, drops to `powershell.exe` when Git for Windows is not installed **[code]** — one more reason the PowerShell fact is not optional. And **"native only" is a statement about habits, not an enforcement:** a project runtime of kind `wsl` still forces `shellOverride: 'wsl.exe'` (`shared/local-windows-terminal-runtime.ts:52-57`) **[code]** whatever the developers type, so §2a's `terminal.lane_wsl_shell_unsupported` refusal remains the enforcement and §2n remains the slice that would lift it.
   - **Git Bash: MSYS rewrites path-looking *arguments*, not the environment block — which is what makes the lane path safe there, and exactly why it must stay an environment value. [inferred], unverified in this tree, gated by §5 step (12c).** The MSYS/Git-for-Windows runtime converts POSIX-looking values on the **command line** into Windows form when it spawns a native (non-MSYS) program, and `MSYS_NO_PATHCONV` is the documented escape hatch for suppressing that conversion; environment variables are handed to the child **unchanged** except for the names the runtime treats as path lists, which is what `MSYS2_ENV_CONV_EXCL` selects **[doc/inferred — neither behaviour is observable from this tree]**. `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is not one of those names, and Orca never puts a lane path on a command line: `computeLaneLaunch` writes it into `spawnOptions.env` and the provider hands it to the child in the environment block (§2 preamble) **[code]**. **So a native `claude.exe` launched from a Git Bash pane receives the Windows lane path verbatim, exactly as it would from PowerShell**, and no Git-Bash arm is needed on `computeLaneLaunch`. **What that makes load-bearing, stated because it was previously incidental:** the rule *never pass the lane path as an argument* — `claude --config-dir <lane>` is precisely the shape §2m(4) already refuses at the capture surfaces, and it is also the one shape MSYS would rewrite, so the two rules now protect each other. Orca already branches on Git Bash inside this same env build — `if (isWindowsGitBashShellPath(shellPath)) { spawnEnv.CHERE_INVOKING ??= '1' }` (the branch at `local-pty-provider.ts:701`, its comment at `:702` — "Git for Windows login files otherwise cd to $HOME, ignoring node-pty's cwd for repo-scoped terminals" — and the body at `:703`; rev 15 cited `:700-703`, whose `:700` is `spawnEnv.PYTHONUTF8 ??= '1'`, corrected here on re-opening) **[code]** — so if the probe shows otherwise the fix has a site and is one branch, not a redesign. **Failure mode if (12c) fails, and its refusal:** the value arrives as `/c/Users/…`, a native `claude.exe` does not find the lane, the pane still renders `credentialLane: 'grant'` (§2h) and §2k bills its usage to the lane owner — the misattribution class this design exists to remove — so a failing (12c) **refuses Git Bash lane panes** (`terminal.lane_shell_unsupported`, the same fail-closed shape as the WSL refusal and enforced at the same site, the lane-aware `buildSpawnEnv` arm, which is where the resolved shell is) until the lane preparation carries a Git-Bash arm. **One thing the shell does *not* change:** the managed statusline is the **cmd** variant on this host whatever the pane's shell is — `getManagedStatusLineScript` branches on `target === 'local' && process.platform === 'win32'` (`claude/statusline-script.ts:17-18`) and reads `%CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR%` (`:57-58`) **[code]**, which `cmd` resolves case-insensitively — so §2k's attribution path is identical under both shells, and §2m(5)'s collapse is what makes that read unambiguous: `cmd` will resolve *a* casing, and exactly one must survive to the child.
   - **PowerShell: `$PROFILE` runs at pane start, after every guard in this design — a §4 residual of the shell-rc class, named and not closed.** PowerShell executes its profile scripts when a session starts **[doc]**, so one line — `$env:CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR = 'C:\Users\Public\x'`, or `Remove-Item Env:CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` — takes effect **after** `computeLaneLaunch`, **after** the provider's post-merge collapse (§2m(5)) and **after** every assertion guard 3 makes on the spawn env, because all of those act on the environment block handed to the shell and the profile runs inside the shell. Git Bash carries the exact twin (`/etc/profile`, `~/.bash_profile`, `~/.bashrc` — the files `CHERE_INVOKING` exists because of), which is why this is stated as a **class** and not as a PowerShell quirk. **It is not closable host-side, and it sits inside §4's envelope rather than outside it:** the profile is a file belonging to the one Windows user both developers run as, and §4's shell-equivalence concession already covers files that user can write — a co-tenant who can edit that profile can equally `type` the lane credential while it is resident. **What it changes is what a green test proves,** so it is stated rather than left implicit: §5 step (0b) reads `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` *inside* the pane, after the profile has run, and is therefore the only check in this document that can observe a profile rewrite at all — a lane whose profile rewrites the variable turns (0b) red instead of passing silently. Named residual in §4; **no refusal**, because a refusal would have to inspect the user's own dotfiles, which §2j declines for the same reason it declines to police `~/.claude/settings.json`.

### (n) WSL on the Windows host — slice S9e

Rev 10 deferred the WSL-visible lane with a one-line "later slice". On a Windows Server host that is no longer a footnote: if the two developers run their agents inside WSL, S9a–S9d isolate **nothing** for them, because every WSL launch falls through to §2i's per-*account* WSL branch and both people's WSL panes share it. So it is designed here, as **S9e**, independently shippable. **The owner has since answered (§2m(8)): native shells only — Git Bash and PowerShell, deliberately not WSL — so S9e is deferred**, out of the S9 delivery order and out of every gate, and this section is a design held ready rather than a slice waiting on a question. What would revive it is named rather than left to taste: a project runtime of kind `wsl` forces a WSL shell whatever anyone types (`shared/local-windows-terminal-runtime.ts:52-57`) **[code]**, and until S9e ships such a lane pane is **refused** (`terminal.lane_wsl_shell_unsupported`, §2a) rather than silently un-isolated — which is why deferring the slice costs correctness nothing.

**The precedent is complete and is not a sketch.** Orca already builds per-account, Linux-side, Orca-owned auth directories inside a distro. `createManagedWslAuthStorage` mints `wslLinuxAuthPath = ${home}/.local/share/orca/claude-accounts/${accountId}/auth` (`claude-accounts/service.ts:939`), writes its ownership marker **inside the distro** through `wsl.exe -d <distro> -- bash -lc 'mkdir -p … && printf … > …'` (`:940-953`), and converts the result to a Windows-visible UNC path with `toWindowsWslPath` (`:954`) **[code]**. Containment is enforced *on the Linux side*: `assertManagedAuthPath` refuses a path whose Linux form is outside `/.local/share/orca/claude-accounts/` or does not end in `/auth` (`:969-977`), and on `win32` re-canonicalizes **inside the distro** — `readlink -f` on both candidate and root, the marker file read and compared, and a `case "$candidate_real" in "$managed_root_real"/*/auth)` pattern match, exit 35 otherwise (`:978-1014`) **[code]**. And the launch side already knows how to split the two paths: `getPreparation`'s managed-WSL branch returns `configDir: activeAccount.managedAuthPath` (the Windows UNC path Orca reads and writes through) alongside `wslLinuxConfigDir` and `envPatch: { CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR: activeAccount.wslLinuxAuthPath }` (the Linux path the CLI sees) (`runtime-auth-service.ts:683-697`, esp. `:686-692`) **[code]**. **S9e is that machinery re-parameterised from `accountId` to `principalId`** — a per-lane Linux-side config dir at `${home}/.local/share/orca/claude-lanes/${principalId}` — not a new mechanism.

**How the blob reaches the distro side.** It does not travel through `wsl.exe`. The host writes it to the **UNC path** (`\\wsl.localhost\<distro>\home\<user>\.local\share\orca\claude-lanes\<principalId>\.credentials.json`) with the same lane writer as a native lane, after `assertManagedAuthPath`-style canonicalization inside the distro. Three consequences, each stated because each is a real difference from a native lane: **(i) the §2m(1) DACL is not run there** — not "runs and no-ops": `restrictWindowsPathSync` returns `false` when it cannot set a DACL (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:60-63`) **[code]**, and §2m(1)'s fail-closed rule is therefore carved out by `parseWslUncPath` rather than applied and ignored (§2m(1), corrected in rev 14). The file's permissions are the **Linux** file's, so the lane writer's WSL arm must `chmod 0700`/`0600` inside the distro (the existing marker write at `:949` uses a bare `printf >` with no mode, so this is an addition, not an assumption) **and read those modes back inside the distro, provisioning failing closed on the read-back** — the substitute for §2m(1)'s DACL verification, in the place S9e's isolation actually lives; and the **UNC root itself is permitted by name**, under §2m(4)'s carve-out for the `wsl.localhost`/`wsl$` forms `parseWslUncPath` recognises (`shared/wsl-paths.ts:6-17`) **[code]** — it is the local WSL redirector for this Windows user, not a remote share, which is the reachability half §2m(4) now states and this clause no longer has to imply; **(ii) `writeFileAtomically`'s rename retry still applies**, because the write goes through the 9p/`\\wsl.localhost` redirector from the Windows side and can still hit `EBUSY`; **(iii)** the wipe (§2f) must sweep the Linux-side directory, including both temp-name shapes (§2m(2)).
